∎

Remark 3.4
.

As mentioned earlier, Proposition
3.3
is stated for
a regularized version of the noise
\dot{W}
. The pathwise interpretation
of the martingale problem related to equation (
3.1
)
can then be reduced to a limiting procedure in equation (
3.10
). We give some hints about this procedure in the current section, the technical details being deferred to Section

4
.

At the heart of the approach in
[
9
]
is the fact that one can
solve equation (
3.7
), or better said equation (
3.10
),
in a pathwise way. As mentioned in Remark
3.4
, this is achieved by taking limits in equation (
3.13
). We summarize
this result in the following theorem, which is stated here quite informally.

Theorem 3.5
.

Let
f_{0},g
be two given
C_{b}^{2}
-functions,
and consider an arbitrary time horizon
T>0
. Then there exists
a unique function
f
in a proper space of controlled process with
respect to
W
, satisfying the following equation in the rough paths
sense on
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}
:

(3.14)

\partial_{x}f_{t}(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{x}p_{t}(x-y)f_{0}(y)dy+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{x}p_{t-s}(x-y)g_{s}(y)dyds\\
-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{xx}^{2}p_{t-s}(x-y)\left(\int_{x}^{y}\partial_{z}f_{s}(z)dW(z)\right)dyds,

Our aim in this section is to recall the main setting allowing to
properly state and prove Theorem
3.5
. Before
getting into the computational details, let us recall that this theorem
has to be seen as the main building block in order to set up the martingale
problem for equation (
3.1
). Namely our
ultimate goal is to get the result below.

Theorem 3.6
.

Let
f_{0},g
be two given

\mathcal{C}_{b}^{2}
-functions, and consider an arbitrary time horizon

T>0
. Let
f
be the unique solution to equation (
3.14
).
Then there exists a probability
\mathbb{P}^{W}
and a canonical
process
X^{c}
on a filtered space
(\hat{\Omega},(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]})

such that under
\mathbb{P}^{W}
the process
M=M^{f}
is a martingale,
where

(3.15)

M_{t}=f_{t}(X_{t}^{c})-f_{0}(X_{0}^{c})-\int_{0}^{t}\left(\mathcal{L}_{x}^{c}f_{s}(X_{s}^{c})-\partial_{t}f_{s}(X_{s}^{c})\right)\,ds.

In addition, there exists a Brownian motion
B
defined on

(\hat{\Omega},(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]})
such that
the martingale
M
admits the representation

(3.16)

M_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}\partial_{x}f_{s}(X_{s}^{c})\,dB_{s}.

Section
3.3
below is devoted to specify the setting under which we will achieve Theorem
3.5
and Theorem
3.6
.

3.3.
The rough path structure for the fixed point problem

In order to get a better grasp on the rough path setting employed below, let us first vaguely summarize the main philosophy
invoked in
[
9
]
in order to get Theorem
3.5
:

(i) We think of
\partial_{x}f_{t}(x)
in (
3.14
)
as the unknown object, denoted as
v_{t}(x).
For each fixed
t
,
the function
x\mapsto v_{t}(x)
is regarded as a rough path controlled
by the Brownian motion
W.
Therefore, the integral
\int_{x}^{y}v_{s}(z)dW(z)

is well-defined in the rough path sense. Notice that the rough paths point of view is needed here. This is due to the fact that
W
is a double sided Brownian motion, and therefore
z\mapsto v_{s}(z)
cannot be thought of as an adapted process.

(ii) We introduce an essential transformation

(3.17)

\mathcal{M}:v\mapsto(\mathcal{M}v)_{t}(x)\triangleq\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{xx}^{2}p_{t-s}(x-y)\left(\int_{x}^{y}v_{s}(z)dW(z)\right)dyds.

This is a transformation on a suitable space of controlled rough paths. Equation (
3.14
) can thus be written as
v=\mathcal{M}v
. Namely
v
is a fixed point for the mapping
\mathcal{M}
.

(iii) In order to solve the fixed point problem, we must expect that

\mathcal{M}
is a contraction. The major technical challenge here
is to define the rough path metric in a delicate way, so that the
transformation
\mathcal{M}
is indeed a contraction. This is a highly
non-trivial point, as there is no a priori evidence about why
\mathcal{M}

needs to be a contraction at all (it is not surprising that
\mathcal{M}

is a bounded linear transformation though).

Notice that in the setting of
[
9
]
,
an auxiliary component
Z_{t}(x)
is introduced to form a two-dimensional
rough path
(W_{t}(x),Z_{t}(x))
. This is needed due to the time-inhomogeneity
of the diffusion equation therein. However, the generator in our situation
is time-homogeneous. This largely simplifies the rough path viewpoint.
In particular, it allows us to treat
W
as a one-dimensional rough
path with the obvious lifting, and the analysis is also simplified
accordingly. This is why we have included a self contained version
of the computations in Section
4
.
We now introduce the rough path setting which will be used in order to solve equation (
3.14
).

3.3.1.
Metric on the Brownian rough path
W

Our regularities will be quantified in terms of two parameters
1/3<\beta<\alpha<1/2
.
The parameter
\alpha
will be the Hölder-exponent for
W,
while

\beta
will be used for the Hölder-exponent of the solution path

v
.

Recall that
x\mapsto W(x)
is a two-sided Brownian motion. On
each compact interval
[-a,a],
it can be viewed as a one-dimensional

\alpha
-Hölder rough path in the obvious way, that is
{\bf W}=(W^{1},W^{2})

with

(3.18)

W^{1}(x,y)=W(y)-W(x),\quad\text{and}\quad W^{2}(x,y)=\frac{1}{2}\left(W(y)-W(x)\right)^{2}.

It is important for our purposes to consider
W
on the entire real
line. Thus we need to introduce a suitable metric on
{\bf W}
to
take into account its growth at infinity.

Lemma 3.7
.

Let
W^{1}
be defined by (
3.18
)
and recall that
\alpha\in(1/3,1/2)
. For each
a\geqslant 1
, we
define a random variable
H_{a}
by

(3.19)

H_{a}\triangleq\|W^{1}\|_{\alpha}^{[-a,a]}\triangleq\sup_{-a\leqslant x\neq y\leqslant a}\frac{|W^{1}(x,y)|}{|y-x|^{\alpha}}.

Then for any
\chi>1/2-\alpha
, we have
\sup_{a\geqslant 1}\frac{H_{a}}{a^{\chi}}<\infty

almost surely.

Proof.

The Brownian scaling property shows that
\frac{H_{a}}{a^{\chi}}\mathrel{{\mathop{=}\limits}}\frac{1}{a^{\chi-(1/2-\alpha)}}\cdot H_{1}.

As a result, we have

\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(\frac{H_{n+1}}{n^{\chi}}>\varepsilon\right)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(H_{1}>\varepsilon(n+1)^{\chi-(1/2-\alpha)}\cdot\left(\frac{n}{n+1}\right)^{\chi}\right).

The series is clearly convergent since
H_{1}
has Gaussian tail
(according to Fernique’s lemma). Therefore a standard application
of Borel-Cantelli shows that
\frac{H_{n+1}}{n^{\chi}}\rightarrow 0

almost surely, further implying that

\sup_{a\geqslant 1}\frac{H_{a}}{a^{\chi}}\leqslant\sup_{n\geqslant 1}\frac{H_{n+1}}{n^{\chi}}<\infty\ \text{a.s.}

This finishes our proof.
∎

In view of Lemma
3.7
, we will first label our assumptions
on
\alpha,\beta
and
\chi
:

(3.20)

1/3<\beta<\alpha<1/2,\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{1}{2}-\alpha<\chi<\frac{\beta}{2}

We let the reader check that the hypothesis on
\chi
in (
3.20
)
can be met whenever
1/3<\alpha<1/2
. Next, given
\alpha,\beta

and
\chi
satisfying (
3.20
), we define a random variable
\kappa_{\alpha,\chi}({\bf W})

by

(3.21)

\kappa_{\alpha,\chi}({\bf W})\triangleq\sup_{a\geqslant 1}\left(\frac{\|W^{1}\|_{\alpha}^{[-a,a]}}{a^{\chi}}+\frac{\|W^{2}\|_{2\alpha}^{[-a,a]}}{a^{2\chi}}\right),

where the norm
\|W^{2}\|_{2\alpha}^{[-a,a]}
is defined similarly
to (
3.19
). The quantity
\kappa_{\alpha,\chi}({\bf W})

is finite almost surely, as seen from Lemma
3.7
.

3.3.2.
The solution space and the corresponding rough path metric

The definition of the solution space for equation (
3.17
) and the corresponding metric
is much more involved. This is largely due to the need of obtaining
a contraction property for the transformation
\mathcal{M}
defined
by (
3.17
).

We shall identify a Banach space
\mathcal{B}
where the solution
path
v
for the fixed point problem given by (
3.14
) or
(
3.17
) lives. Let us first describe what the object
v
looks
like. Recall that, in the fixed point problem, we want to think of
the integral
\int_{x}^{y}v_{t}(z)dW(z)
as a rough path integral.
This requires viewing
z\mapsto v_{t}(z)
(for each fixed
t
) as
a rough path controlled by
{\bf W}
(similar to
[
14
, Definition 1]
).
Therefore, the object
v_{t}(\cdot)
must also come with a Gubinelli
derivative path
\partial_{W}v_{t}(\cdot)
. In other words, the a
priori shape of the object
v
should be given by a pair

(3.22)

\mathcal{V}=(v,\partial_{W}v),

where

v:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R},\quad\text{and}\quad\partial_{W}v:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}.

To make sense of the rough integral, we require that, for each fixed

t\in[0,T]
, the path

z\mapsto\mathcal{V}_{t}(z)=(v_{t}(z),\partial_{W}v_{t}(z))

is a
\beta
-Hölder path controlled by
{\bf W}.
Namely, both
v_{t}(\cdot)

and
\partial_{W}v_{t}(\cdot)
are
\beta
-Hölder continuous on
compact intervals, and the remainder

(3.23)

\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{V}_{t}}(x,y)\triangleq v_{t}(y)-v_{t}(x)-\partial_{W}v_{t}(x)\cdot(W(y)-W(x))

has
2\beta
-Hölder regularity in the sense that for each
a\geqslant 1

we have

(3.24)

\|\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{V}_{t}}\|_{2\beta}^{[-a,a]}\triangleq\sup_{-a\leqslant x\neq y\leqslant a}\frac{|\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{V}_{t}}(x,y)|}{|y-x|^{2\beta}}<\infty.

Our next task is to define a rough path metric on
\mathcal{V}
quantitatively.
As mentioned above, the main effort here is to tune this metric in
a careful way so that we end up with a contraction for the transformation

\mathcal{M}
described by (
3.17
). We first introduce the following
general notation. For a given function
f:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
,
we define:

(3.25)

\llbracket f\rrbracket_{\beta/2,\beta}^{[0,t]\times[-a,a]}\triangleq\|f\|_{\infty}^{[0,t]\times[-a,a]}+a^{-\beta/2}\|f\|_{\beta/2,\beta}^{[0,t]\times[-a,a]},

where

\|f\|_{\beta/2,\beta}^{[0,t]\times[-a,a]}\triangleq\sup_{(s,x)\neq(s^{\prime},x^{\prime})\in[0,t]\times[-a,a]}\frac{|f_{s^{\prime}}(x^{\prime})-f_{s}(x)|}{|s^{\prime}-s|^{\beta/2}+|x^{\prime}-x|^{\beta}}.

We now define some weight functions in order to take into account
the fact that we are considering controlled processes on the real
line
\mathbb{R}
.

Definition 3.8
.

Recall that
\alpha,\beta,\chi
satisfy relation (
3.20
).
Consider another set of parameters
\lambda,\theta>1
. Then for
a\geqslant 1

and
t\geqslant 0
we set

(3.26)

E^{\theta,\lambda}(a,t)\triangleq e^{\lambda t+\theta a+\theta at},\quad\text{and}\quad Q(a,t):=a^{\chi}\cdot\left(a^{\beta/2}+t^{-\beta/2}\right),

with the convention
Q(a,t)^{-1}\triangleq 0
if
t=0
.

With Definition
3.8
in hand, we can now
introduce the proper notion of controlled processes we wish to consider
in this article. Namely the space
\mathcal{B}^{\theta,\lambda}

below will be the underlying space on which the fixed point problem (
3.17
)
is solved.

Definition 3.9
.

Let the notation of Definition
3.8

prevail, and consider a controlled process
\mathcal{V}=(v,\partial_{W}v)

such that (
3.24
) holds true. We introduce a new parameter
\gamma\triangleq\frac{\alpha-\beta}{4}>0
.
Then we define the norm of
\mathcal{V}
in the following way:

(3.27)

\Theta^{\theta,\lambda}(\mathcal{V})\triangleq\sup_{t\in[0,T],a\geqslant 1}E^{\theta,\lambda}(a,t)^{-1}\\
\times\left(\llbracket v\rrbracket_{\beta/2,\beta}^{[0,t]\times[-a,a]}+\lambda^{-\gamma}\llbracket\partial_{W}v\rrbracket_{\beta/2,\beta}^{[0,t]\times[-a,a]}+\lambda^{-\gamma}Q(a,t)^{-1}\|\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{V}_{t}}\|_{2\beta}^{[-a,a]}\right).

We denote by
\mathcal{B}^{\theta,\lambda}
the space of those
\mathcal{V}
’s
such that
\Theta^{\theta,\lambda}(\mathcal{V})<\infty
. It follows
that
\mathcal{B}^{\theta,\lambda}
is a Banach space under the norm

\Theta^{\theta,\lambda}
.

Remark 3.10
.

Notice that the choice
\gamma=\frac{\alpha-\beta}{4}

in Definition
3.9
might seem obscure at
this point. The reason for this will be made clear in the following
sections. Also observe that the norm
\Theta^{\theta,\lambda}
is
decreasing in
\lambda.
As a result, we have
\mathcal{B}^{\theta,\lambda}\subseteq\mathcal{B}^{\theta,\lambda^{\prime}}

if
\lambda<\lambda^{\prime}
.

Before getting into any technical estimates, it would be helpful to
point out why the norm
\Theta^{\theta,\lambda}
is designed in such
a way. Unfortunately the explanation can only be vague at this stage.

(i) The weight
e^{\theta a}
in
E^{\theta,\lambda}(a,t)

allows the solution to grow at most exponentially in space at infinity.

(ii) The weights
a^{-\beta/2}
in (
3.25
) and the
term
a^{\chi+\beta/2}
in
Q(a,t)
are used to absorb the polynomial
factors in
a
which come out when estimating the transformation

\mathcal{M}\mathcal{V}
. The appearance of polynomial factors is
not surprising due to the polynomial growth of
\|W\|_{\alpha}^{[-a,a]}

as
a\rightarrow\infty
.

(iii) The weights
e^{\lambda t+\theta at}
and
\lambda^{-\gamma}

are used to ensure that
\mathcal{M}
is a contraction. As we will
see, when estimating the norm of the transformation
\mathcal{M}
,
a negative power of
\lambda
comes out due to the introduction of
these weights. As a result, if we make the a priori choice of
\lambda

to be large enough,
\mathcal{M}
becomes a contraction. This is
the most magical part of the analysis.

(iv) The term
t^{-\beta/2}
in
Q(a,t)
accounts for the singularity
of the heat kernel at the origin. Such singularity will appear naturally
when estimating a remainder term in
\mathcal{M}\mathcal{V}
. This
is also related to our one-dimensional rough path viewpoint.

3.3.3.
The transformation
\mathcal{M}

Having introduced the Banach space
(\mathcal{B}^{\theta,\lambda},\Theta^{\theta,\lambda}(\cdot))
,
we will now define the key transformation
\mathcal{M}
as a rough
path in
\mathcal{B}^{\theta,\lambda}
. Namely given
\mathcal{V}=(v,\partial_{W}v)
,
we define

(3.28)

\mathcal{M}\mathcal{V}\triangleq[(t,x)\mapsto((\mathcal{M}\mathcal{V})_{t}(x),\partial_{W}(\mathcal{M}\mathcal{V})_{t}(x))],

where
\mathcal{M}\mathcal{V}
is given by (
3.17
). As far as

\partial_{W}(\mathcal{M}\mathcal{V})
is concerned, we will see
in Section
4.3.4
that for
(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}

we have

(3.29)

\partial_{W}(\mathcal{M}\mathcal{V})_{t}(x)\triangleq-\frac{2}{\sigma^{2}}\,v_{t}(x),

where we recall that
\sigma^{2}
is introduced in Notation
2.3
.
Furthermore, in view of the formal equation (
3.14
), it is
natural to consider the following transformation on
(\mathcal{B}^{\theta,\lambda},\Theta^{\theta,\lambda}(\cdot))

(3.30)

\hat{\mathcal{M}}:\mathcal{V}\mapsto\hat{\mathcal{M}}\mathcal{V}\triangleq\psi^{1}+\psi^{2}+\mathcal{M}\mathcal{V},

where
\psi^{1},\psi^{2}
are functions respectively defined by

(3.31)

\psi_{t}^{1}(x)\triangleq\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{x}p_{t}(x-y)f_{0}(y)dy,\quad\text{and}\quad\psi_{t}^{2}(x)\triangleq\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{x}p_{t-s}(x-y)g_{s}(y)dyds.

Our goal in Section
4
will be to show that
\hat{\mathcal{M}}
admits
a unique fixed point in the Banach space
\mathcal{B}^{\theta,\lambda}
.
More precisely, this is the content of the following result.

Theorem 3.11
.

Let
T
be a finite time horizon and consider
\alpha,\beta,\chi,\theta

as in Definitions
3.8
and
3.9
.
We also consider the quantity
\kappa_{\alpha,\chi}({\bf W}
) introduced
in (
3.21
). Recall that the transformation
\hat{\mathcal{M}}

is defined by (
3.30
). Then there exists
\Lambda=\Lambda_{\alpha,\beta,\chi,\theta,T,\kappa}>0
,
such that for any
\lambda>\Lambda
and
f_{0},g\in C_{b}^{2},

we have:

(i)

The functions
\psi^{1},\psi^{2}\in\mathcal{B}^{\theta,\lambda}

defined by (
3.31
) are elements of
\mathcal{B}^{\theta,\lambda}
.

(ii)

There exists a unique fixed point for
\hat{\mathcal{M}}
, that
is a unique element
\mathcal{V}
of the space
\mathcal{B}^{\theta,\lambda}

satisfying

\mathcal{V}=\psi^{1}+\psi^{2}+\mathcal{M}\mathcal{V}.

Otherwise stated one can solve equation (
3.7
) for
\eta=0
, under its mild form (
3.14
). The unique solution sits in the space
\mathcal{B}^{\theta,\lambda}
.

(iii)

The controlled process
\mathcal{V}
satisfies

\Theta^{\theta,\lambda}(\mathcal{V})\leqslant c,

for a constant
c
depending on
f_{0}
and
g
only.

To put Theorem
3.11
into perspective, let us mention again that this result allows to solve equation (
3.7
) in a pathwise sense for all continuous functions
g
. Hence there is a unique solution to the martingale problem related to equation (
3.1
), that is Theorem
3.5
holds true. Going from Theorem
3.11
to Theorem
3.5
is a
matter of standard considerations, for which we refer e.g. to

[
18
,
31
]
. Let us mention that the martingale
M=M^{f}
in (
3.16
) can now be written as

M_{t}=f_{t}(X_{t}^{c})-f_{0}(X_{0}^{c})-\int_{0}^{t}g_{s}(X_{s}^{c})ds,

where
f
is the solution to the following rough PDE on

[0,\tau]\times\mathbb{R}
:

(3.32)

f_{t}(x)=P_{\tau-t}g_{\tau}(x)+\int_{t}^{\tau}\int_{\mathbb{R}}p_{s-t}(x-y)\partial_{x}f_{s}(y)W(dy)\,ds.

Notice that (
3.32
) is a backward version of the mild equation
(
3.8
), which can be solved exactly in the same way. In our paper we have chosen to deal with the forward equation (
3.8
) for notational convenience. Let us also mention that with Theorem
3.5
in hand, the existence-uniqueness of a weak solution to equation (
3.1
) is again a matter of standard arguments. We summarize this in the following theorem.

Theorem 3.12
.

Fix a realization of the process
W
in
(\Omega,\mathcal{G},\mathbf{P})
. Then there exists a probability
\mathbb{P}^{W}
on the canonical space

\left({\rm C}(\mathbb{R}_{+}),\mathcal{B}({\rm C}(\mathbb{R}_{+}))\right)
and a Brownian motion
B
defined on that canonical space, such that
(X,B)

satisfies the following integral form of equation (
3.1
):

X_{t}^{c}=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\dot{W}(X_{s}^{c})ds+B_{t},\quad\text{ for all }\quad t\geqslant 0.

We close this section by labeling some notation which will prevail
for our computations below.

Notation 3.13
.

We use
C_{\text{subscript}}
to denote a constant depending only
on the parameters specified in the subscript but nothing else. We
also use
C
to denote a constant depending only on
\alpha,\beta,\chi,\theta,T,\varepsilon

but nothing else. Careful inspection on the analysis will reveal that
the dependence of
C
on
\theta,T
is at most
e^{\theta^{2}(1+T)^{3}}.

The value of these constants can change from line to line. We also
use
P_{u}(w)
to denote universal polynomials of
|w|
which only
depend on
\alpha,\beta,\chi
and
T
(the dependence on
T
only
appears in the coefficients and is polynomial).

4.
The rough path formulation of the martingale problem

As mentioned in Section
3
, Theorem
3.11
is the key to establish existence and uniqueness for the martingale problem related to equation (
3.1
). Arguably, our Theorem
3.11
is contained in
[
9
]
Theorem 5. However we have decided to include a detailed proof of the main estimates here for two reasons: first our context where
W
only depends on the space variable lead to simpler considerations than in
[
9
]
. In addition, the bounds presented in this section will
play a prominent role in our analysis of the convergence in Section
6
. We will thus handle the rough path type estimates for
\mathcal{M}v
in Sections

4.1
-
4.2
-
4.3
, and complete the proof of Theorem
3.11
in Sections
4.4
-
4.5
.

4.1.
Rough integral estimates

As mentionned before, the integral
\int_{x}^{y}v_{s}(z)dW(z)
in (
3.17
)
is defined in the rough paths sense. With respect to the usual rough paths setting of
[
14
]
, those integrals in
\mathbb{R}
have to involve weighted norms in space. In the current section we develop some basic tools for integrals of processes in spaces of the form
\mathcal{B}^{\theta,\lambda}

(see Definition
3.9
). Let us start with a bound on those integrals, depending on an interval
[-a,a]\subset\mathbb{R}
.

Proposition 4.1
.

Let
{\bf W}=(W^{1},W^{2})
be the one-dimensional rough path given by (
3.18
). We consider

\alpha,\beta
satisfying (
3.20
) and a controlled path
x\mapsto\mathcal{V}(x)
with
\mathcal{V}(x)=(v(x),\partial_{W}v(x))

as introduced in (
3.22
)-(
3.23
). Then for
any
a\geqslant 1
and
x,y\in[-a,a]
the integral
\int_{x}^{y}v(z)dW(z)

is well-defined, and we have

(4.1)

\Big|\int_{x}^{y}v(z)dW(z)-v(x)W^{1}(x,y)-\partial_{W}v(x)W^{2}(x,y)\Big|\\
\leqslant C_{\beta}\cdot\left(\|\partial_{W}v\|_{\beta}^{[-a,a]}\cdot\|W^{2}\|_{2\alpha}^{[-a,a]}\cdot|y-x|^{2\alpha+\beta}+\|\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{V}}\|_{2\beta}^{[-a,a]}\cdot\|W\|_{\alpha}^{[-a,a]}\cdot|y-x|^{\alpha+2\beta}\right),

where the Hölder norms above are understood as in (
3.19
).

Proof.

This is a mere elaboration of
[
14
, Theorem 1]
, whose proof is omitted for sake of conciseness.
∎

Notice that we have stated Proposition
4.1
for
a usual controlled process, since we are only using the function
x\mapsto\mathcal{V}(x)

in our estimate. Therefore here
v
is just considered as one single
rough path rather than a family of rough paths parametrized by time.
However, in the sequel we shall need a corollary stated for processes
in
\mathcal{B}^{\theta,\lambda}
. This is summarized in the lemma
below.

Lemma 4.2
.

Consider
\alpha,\beta,\chi
such that (
3.20
) is fulfilled. Let
\mathcal{V}=[(t,x)\mapsto\mathcal{V}_{t}(x)=(v_{t}(x),\partial_{W}v_{t}(x))]

be an element in
\mathcal{B}^{\theta,\lambda}
, as given in Definition
3.9
.
To simplify notation, let us set

(4.2)

\kappa\triangleq\kappa_{\alpha,\chi}({\bf W}),\quad\Theta\triangleq\Theta^{\theta,\lambda}(\mathcal{V}),\quad E\triangleq E^{\theta,\lambda}(a,t),

and

(4.3)

D(a,t,z)\triangleq a^{2\chi}|z|^{2\alpha}+a^{2\chi+\beta/2}|z|^{2\alpha+\beta}+a^{2\chi}(a^{\beta/2}+t^{-\beta/2})|z|^{\alpha+2\beta}.

Then for any
t\in(0,T]
,
a\geqslant 1
and
x,y\in[-a,a],
we
have

(4.4)

\Big|\int_{x}^{y}v_{t}(z)dW(z)-v_{t}(x)W^{1}(x,y)\Big|\leqslant C_{\beta}\,\kappa\,\Theta E\,\lambda^{\gamma}\,D(a,t,y-x)

and

(4.5)

\Big|\int_{x}^{y}v_{t}(z)dW(z)\Big|\leqslant C_{\beta}\,\kappa\,\Theta E\,\lambda^{\gamma}\,\left(a^{\chi}|y-x|^{\alpha}+D(a,t,y-x)\right).

Proof.

In order to get the first estimate we resort to relation (
4.1
),
which yields

\Big|\int_{x}^{y}v_{t}(z)dW(z)-v_{t}(x)W^{1}(x,y)\Big|\leqslant\Big|\partial_{W}v_{t}(x)\Big|\cdot\big|W^{2}(x,y)\big|\\
+C_{\beta}\cdot\left(\|\partial_{W}v_{t}\|_{\beta}^{[-a,a]}\cdot\|W^{2}\|_{2\alpha}^{[-a,a]}\cdot|y-x|^{2\alpha+\beta}+\|\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{V}_{t}}\|_{2\beta}^{[-a,a]}\cdot\|W\|_{\alpha}^{[-a,a]}\cdot|y-x|^{\alpha+2\beta}\right).

Next we bound
\big|\partial_{W}v_{t}(x)\big|
and
\big|W^{2}(x,y)\big|

above thanks to their Hölder and supremum norms. We get

\Big|\int_{x}^{y}v_{t}(z)dW(z)-v_{t}(x)W^{1}(x,y)\Big|\leqslant\|\partial_{W}v\|_{\infty}^{[0,t]\times[-a,a]}\cdot\|W^{2}\|_{2\alpha}^{[-a,a]}\cdot|y-x|^{2\alpha}\\
+C_{\beta}\cdot\left(\|\partial_{W}v\|_{\beta/2,\beta}^{[0,t]\times[-a,a]}\cdot\|W^{2}\|_{2\alpha}^{[-a,a]}\cdot|y-x|^{2\alpha+\beta}+\|\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{V}_{t}}\|_{2\beta}^{[-a,a]}\cdot\|W\|_{\alpha}^{[-a,a]}\cdot|y-x|^{\alpha+2\beta}\right).

We now use the definition (
3.21
) of
\kappa_{\alpha,\chi}({\bf W})

as well as Definition
3.9
in order to obtain

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{x}^{y}v_{t}(z)dW(z)-v_{t}(x)W^{1}(x,y)\Big|

(4.6)

\displaystyle\leqslant C_{\beta}\cdot\kappa\cdot\Theta E\cdot\lambda^{\gamma}\cdot\left(a^{2\chi}|y-x|^{2\alpha}+a^{2\chi+\beta/2}|y-x|^{2\alpha+\beta}+a^{2\chi}(a^{\beta/2}+t^{-\beta/2})|y-x|^{\alpha+2\beta}\right),

from which our estimate (
4.4
) is easily achieved. Our second
claim (
4.5
) is also deduced from (
4.4
) plus
the trivial bound

|v_{t}(x)W^{1}(x,y)|\leqslant\|v\|_{\infty}^{[0,t]\times[-a,a]}\cdot a^{\chi}\cdot\kappa\cdot|y-x|^{\alpha}\leqslant\kappa\Theta E\cdot a^{\chi}|y-x|^{\alpha}.

The proof is now complete.
∎

4.2.
A key estimate for heat kernel convolutions with rough integrals

In view of the expression (
3.17
) of
(\mathcal{M}\mathcal{V})_{t}(x)
,
an essential ingredient in the analysis of
\mathcal{M}\mathcal{V}

is to estimate heat kernel convolutions with rough path integrals
carefully. This is summarized in the following key lemma.

Lemma 4.3
.

Consider
\alpha,\beta,\chi
such that (
3.20
)
is fulfilled. Let
\mathcal{V}=(v,\partial_{W}v)
be a controlled
path in the space
\mathcal{B}^{\theta,\lambda}
introduced in Definition

3.9
. Recall that the heat kernel on
\mathbb{R}

is given by (
3.5
) and the constants
\kappa,\Theta,E

are spelled out in (
4.2
). Then the following
two estimates hold true:

(i) Let
0\leqslant\gamma_{1}\leqslant\gamma_{2}\leqslant\beta/2

and
k\geqslant 1.
For any
a\geqslant 1,

x\in[-a,a]
and
\tau_{1}\leqslant\tau_{2}\in(0,T],

we have

(4.7)

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{0}^{\tau_{1}}\frac{|\partial_{x\cdots x}^{k}p_{1}(w)|}{s^{1+\gamma_{1}}}\left|\int_{x}^{x+\sqrt{s}w}v_{\tau_{2}-s}(z)dW(z)\right|dwds\leqslant C\cdot\kappa\Theta E\cdot\lambda^{\gamma-\frac{\alpha-\beta}{2}}a^{\gamma_{2}}\cdot\tau_{1}^{\gamma_{2}-\gamma_{1}},

(ii) Let
0\leqslant\sigma_{1}\leqslant\beta
and
k\geqslant 1.

For any
a\geqslant 1,

x\in[-a,a]
and
\tau_{1}\leqslant\tau_{2}\in(0,T],

we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{0}^{\tau_{1}}\frac{|\partial_{x\cdots x}^{k}p_{1}(w)|}{s^{1+\sigma_{1}}}\left|\int_{x}^{x+\sqrt{s}w}(v_{\tau_{2}-s}(z)-v_{\tau_{2}-s}(x))dW(z)\right|dwds

(4.8)

\displaystyle\ \ \ \leqslant C\cdot\kappa\Theta E\cdot\lambda^{\gamma-\frac{\alpha-\beta}{4}}a^{\chi}\left(a^{\beta/2}+\tau_{2}^{-\beta/2}\right)\cdot\tau_{1}^{\beta-\sigma_{1}}.

Remark 4.4
.

The factors
\lambda^{\gamma-\frac{\alpha-\beta}{2}}

and
\lambda^{\gamma-\frac{\alpha-\beta}{4}}
appearing in the above
two estimates are crucial. In fact, we will choose
\gamma=\frac{\alpha-\beta}{4}

in the a priori definition of
\gamma.
This implies that the factor

\lambda^{\gamma-\frac{\alpha-\beta}{2}}=\lambda^{-\frac{\alpha-\beta}{4}}

is decaying. This
point is critical for obtaining the contraction property of
\mathcal{M}\mathcal{V}.

Proof of Lemma
4.3
.

We will divide
this proof into several steps.

Step 1: Estimate for the rough integral.
Set

(4.9)

{\mathcal{A}}_{x,s,w,\tau_{2}}=\int_{x}^{x+\sqrt{s}w}v_{\tau_{2}-s}(z)dW(z).

Then according to (
4.5
), for any
x\in[-a,a]
we can upper
bound
|{\mathcal{A}}_{x,s,w,\tau_{2}}|
by

\displaystyle C_{\beta}\cdot\kappa\Theta\cdot E(a+\sqrt{s}|w|,\tau_{2}-s)\cdot\lambda^{\gamma}\cdot\left((a+\sqrt{s}|w|)^{\chi}s^{\alpha/2}|w|^{\alpha}+(a+\sqrt{s}|w|)^{2\chi}s^{\alpha}|w|^{2\alpha}\right.

\displaystyle\ \ \ \left.+(a+\sqrt{s}|w|)^{2\chi+\beta/2}s^{\alpha+\beta/2}|w|^{2\alpha+\beta}+(a+\sqrt{s}|w|)^{2\chi+\beta/2}s^{\alpha/2+\beta}|w|^{\alpha+2\beta}\right.

\displaystyle\ \ \ \left.+(a+\sqrt{s}|w|)^{2\chi}(\tau_{2}-s)^{-\beta/2}s^{\alpha/2+\beta}|w|^{\alpha+2\beta}\right).

Hence recalling that we write
P_{u}(w)
for any polynomial in
w

and that our time horizon is
T
, we get that
|{\mathcal{A}}_{x,s,w,\tau_{2}}|

can be upper bounded as follows:

(4.10)

\displaystyle C_{\beta}\kappa\Theta\lambda^{\gamma}\cdot P_{u}(w)\cdot E(a+\sqrt{s}|w|,\tau_{2}-s)\cdot\left((a+\sqrt{T}|w|)^{\chi}s^{\alpha/2}+(a+\sqrt{T}|w|)^{2\chi}s^{\alpha}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+(a+\sqrt{T}|w|)^{2\chi+\beta/2}s^{\alpha+\beta/2}+(a+\sqrt{T}|w|)^{2\chi+\beta/2}s^{\alpha/2+\beta}+(a+\sqrt{T}|w|)^{2\chi}(\tau_{2}-s)^{-\beta/2}s^{\alpha/2+\beta}\right).

In addition, still invoking the fact that our time horizon is
T
,
we can write

(4.11)

\displaystyle E(a+\sqrt{s}|w|,\tau_{2}-s)

\displaystyle=e^{\lambda(\tau_{2}-s)+\theta(a+\sqrt{s}|w|)+\theta(a+\sqrt{s}|w|)(\tau_{2}-s)}

(4.12)

\displaystyle\leqslant E(a,\tau_{2})\cdot e^{\theta(1+T)\sqrt{T}|w|}\cdot e^{-\left(\lambda+\theta(a+\sqrt{T}|w|)\right)s}.

Consider now
\gamma_{1}\leqslant\gamma_{2}\leqslant\beta/2
as in the statement of our current lemma.
Setting
\rho\triangleq a+\sqrt{T}|w|
and plugging (
4.12
) in (
4.10
), we obtain that

(4.13)

s^{-(1+\gamma_{1})}\big|{\mathcal{A}}_{x,s,w,\tau_{2}}\big|\leqslant C_{\beta}\kappa\Theta\lambda^{\gamma}P_{u}(w)E(a,\tau_{2})e^{\theta(1+T)\sqrt{T}|w|}\cdot e^{-(\lambda+\theta\rho)s}\phi_{1}(\rho,s),

where we define two functions
\phi_{i}
,
i=1,2
, as follows:

(4.14)

\phi_{i}(\rho,s)=\left(\rho^{\chi}s^{\alpha/2-1-\gamma_{i}}+\rho^{2\chi}s^{\alpha-1-\gamma_{i}}\right.\\
\ \ \ \left.+\rho^{2\chi+\beta/2}s^{\alpha+\beta/2-1-\gamma_{i}}+\rho^{2\chi+\beta/2}s^{\alpha/2+\beta-1-\gamma_{i}}+\rho^{2\chi}(\tau_{2}-s)^{-\beta/2}s^{\alpha/2+\beta-1-\gamma_{i}}\right),

and we notice that
\phi_{2}
will be used in (
4.17
).

Step 2: Decomposition of a time integral.
With
a proper estimate for
{\mathcal{A}}_{x,s,w,\tau_{2}}
in hand, we
turn to an estimate of the double integral (
4.7
). To this
aim, we first consider the
s
-integral and we set

(4.15)

{\mathcal{D}}_{\tau_{1}}(w)=\int_{0}^{\tau_{1}}s^{-(1+\gamma_{1})}{\mathcal{A}}_{x,s,w,\tau_{2}}ds.

Then taking (
4.13
) and (
4.14
) into
account we get

\big|{\mathcal{D}}_{\tau_{1}}(w)\big|\leqslant C_{\beta}\kappa\Theta\lambda^{\gamma}P_{u}(w)E(a,\tau_{2})e^{\theta(1+T)\sqrt{T}|w|}\int_{0}^{\tau_{1}}e^{-(\lambda+\theta\rho)s}\phi_{1}(\rho,s)ds.

We will now gain a small factor
\tau_{1}^{\gamma_{2}-\gamma_{1}}

by playing with the fact that
\gamma_{2}\geqslant\gamma_{1}
. Namely we
easily obtain

(4.16)

\big|{\mathcal{D}}_{\tau_{1}}(w)\big|\leqslant C_{\beta}\tau_{1}^{\gamma_{2}-\gamma_{1}}\kappa\Theta\lambda^{\gamma}P_{u}(w)E(a,\tau_{2})e^{\theta(1+T)\sqrt{T}|w|}{\mathcal{J}}_{\tau_{1}},

where we have set

(4.17)

{\mathcal{J}}_{\tau_{1}}=\int_{0}^{\tau_{1}}e^{-(\lambda+\theta\rho)s}\phi_{2}(\rho,s)ds,

and where we recall that
\phi_{2}
is introduced in (
4.14
).
In addition, we shall decompose the integral
\mathcal{J}
into
{\mathcal{J}}={\mathcal{J}}^{1}+{\mathcal{J}}^{2}
,
where we define

{\mathcal{J}}_{\tau_{1}}^{1}\triangleq\int_{0}^{\tau_{1}}e^{-(\lambda+\theta\rho)s}\cdot\left(\rho^{\chi}s^{\alpha/2-1-\gamma_{2}}+\rho^{2\chi}s^{\alpha-1-\gamma_{2}}+\rho^{2\chi+\beta/2}s^{\alpha+\beta/2-1-\gamma_{2}}\right.\\
\ \ \ \left.+\rho^{2\chi+\beta/2}s^{\alpha/2+\beta-1-\gamma_{2}}\right)ds

and

(4.18)

{\mathcal{J}}_{\tau_{1}}^{2}\triangleq\int_{0}^{\tau_{1}}e^{-(\lambda+\theta\rho)s}\rho^{2\chi}(\tau_{2}-s)^{-\beta/2}s^{\alpha/2+\beta-1-\gamma_{2}}ds.

The methods in order to estimate
{\mathcal{J}}^{1}
and
{\mathcal{J}}^{2}

are slightly different, and thus those two tasks will be carried out
separately. The main non-trivial effort here is to see that

(4.19)

{\mathcal{J}}_{\tau_{1}}^{i}\leqslant C_{\alpha,\beta}\rho^{\gamma_{2}}\cdot\lambda^{\frac{\beta-\alpha}{2}},\quad\text{for}\quad i=1,2.

This is the critical point where a negative power of
\lambda
appears
(recall that
\beta<\alpha
), which in turn contributes to the contraction
property of the transformation
\mathcal{M}.

Step 3: Estimation of
{\mathcal{J}}^{1}
. By
applying a change of variables
r=(\lambda+\theta\rho)s,
we obtain

\rho^{-\gamma_{2}}{\mathcal{J}}_{\tau_{1}}^{1}=\int_{0}^{(\lambda+\theta\rho)\tau_{1}}e^{-r}\left(\frac{\rho^{\chi-\gamma_{2}}}{(\lambda+\theta\rho)^{\alpha/2-\gamma_{2}}}r^{\alpha/2-\gamma_{2}-1}+\frac{\rho^{2\chi-\gamma_{2}}}{(\lambda+\theta\rho)^{\alpha-\gamma_{2}}}r^{\alpha-\gamma_{2}-1}\right.\\
\left.+\frac{\rho^{2\chi+\beta/2-\gamma_{2}}}{(\lambda+\theta\rho)^{\alpha+\beta/2-\gamma_{2}}}r^{\alpha+\beta/2-\gamma_{2}-1}+\frac{\rho^{2\chi+\beta/2-\gamma_{2}}}{(\lambda+\theta\rho)^{\alpha/2+\beta-\gamma_{2}}}r^{\alpha/2+\beta-\gamma_{2}-1}\right)dr.

Therefore collecting the terms of the form
\rho^{\alpha_{1}}/(\lambda+\theta\,\rho)^{\alpha_{2}}

and computing the integrals
\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-r}r^{\alpha_{3}}\,dr
,
the reader can easily check that

(4.20)

\rho^{-\gamma_{2}}{\mathcal{J}}_{\tau_{1}}^{1}\leqslant C_{\alpha,\beta}\cdot\left(\frac{\rho^{\chi-\gamma_{2}}}{(\lambda+\theta\rho)^{\alpha/2-\gamma_{2}}}+\frac{\rho^{2\chi-\gamma_{2}}}{(\lambda+\theta\rho)^{\alpha-\gamma_{2}}}+\frac{\rho^{2\chi+\beta/2-\gamma_{2}}}{(\lambda+\theta\rho)^{\alpha+\beta/2-\gamma_{2}}}+\frac{\rho^{2\chi+\beta/2-\gamma_{2}}}{(\lambda+\theta\rho)^{\alpha/2+\beta-\gamma_{2}}}\right).

Hence to reach the estimate (
4.19
), we need to show
that

(4.21)

\frac{\rho^{\chi-\gamma_{2}}}{(\lambda+\theta\rho)^{\alpha/2-\gamma_{2}}}\vee\frac{\rho^{2\chi-\gamma_{2}}}{(\lambda+\theta\rho)^{\alpha-\gamma_{2}}}\vee\frac{\rho^{2\chi+\beta/2-\gamma_{2}}}{(\lambda+\theta\rho)^{\alpha+\beta/2-\gamma_{2}}}\vee\frac{\rho^{2\chi+\beta/2-\gamma_{2}}}{(\lambda+\theta\rho)^{\alpha/2+\beta-\gamma_{2}}}\leqslant\lambda^{\frac{\beta-\alpha}{2}}.

In order to achieve (
4.21
), we first observe that the
above four quotients all have the form

(4.22)

\frac{\rho^{b-\gamma_{2}}}{(\lambda+\theta\rho)^{a-\gamma_{2}}},\qquad\ \ \ \text{with }0<b<a\ \text{and }a>\gamma_{2}.

More precisely, we recall from (
3.20
) that
\beta<\alpha
and

\chi<1/2
, and we have also assumed that
\gamma_{1}\leqslant\gamma_{2}\leqslant\beta/2
.
Thus it is readily checked that the exponents in (
4.21
)
satisfy (
4.22
), by respectively considering the following
values for
a,b
:

(4.23)

\text{(i) }\begin{cases}b=\chi\\
a=\alpha/2\end{cases},\ \text{(ii) }\begin{cases}b=2\chi\\
a=\alpha\end{cases},\ \text{(iii) }\begin{cases}b=2\chi+\beta/2\\
a=\alpha+\beta/2\end{cases},\ \text{(iv) }\begin{cases}b=2\chi+\beta/2\\
a=\alpha/2+\beta\end{cases}

With (
4.21
) in mind, we now claim that for all
\lambda,\rho\geqslant 1

we have:

(4.24)

\frac{\rho^{b-\gamma_{2}}}{(\lambda+\theta\rho)^{a-\gamma_{2}}}\leqslant\lambda^{b\vee\gamma_{2}-a}\ \ \ \forall\lambda,\rho\geqslant 1.

Indeed, if
\rho\geqslant\lambda,
we have (recall
\lambda,\theta>1

and
b<a
)

\frac{\rho^{b-\gamma_{2}}}{(\lambda+\theta\rho)^{a-\gamma_{2}}}\leqslant\rho^{b-a}\leqslant\lambda^{b-a}\leqslant\lambda^{b\vee\gamma_{2}-a}.

On the other hand, if
1\leqslant\rho<\lambda
and
b>\gamma_{2}
,
we have

\frac{\rho^{b-\gamma_{2}}}{(\lambda+\theta\rho)^{a-\gamma_{2}}}\leqslant\frac{\rho^{b-\gamma_{2}}}{\lambda^{a-\gamma_{2}}}\leqslant\lambda^{b-a}\leqslant\lambda^{b\vee\gamma_{2}-a}.

Eventually, if
1\leqslant\rho<\lambda
and
b\leqslant\gamma_{2}
,
then

\frac{\rho^{b-\gamma_{2}}}{(\lambda+\theta\rho)^{a-\gamma_{2}}}=\frac{1}{\rho^{\gamma_{2}-b}(\lambda+\theta\rho)^{a-\gamma_{2}}}\leqslant\lambda^{\gamma_{2}-a}\leqslant\lambda^{b\vee\gamma_{2}-a}.

Therefore, the claim (
4.24
) follows. The estimate (
4.19
)
for
{\mathcal{J}}^{1}
is then established by reporting (
4.24
)
into the cases (i)-(iv) in (
4.23
). Notice that we have

a-b\vee\gamma_{2}=\begin{cases}\text{(i) }\frac{\alpha}{2}-\chi\vee\gamma_{2}\geqslant(\alpha-\beta)/2\\
\text{(ii) }\alpha-2\chi\vee\gamma_{2}\geqslant\alpha-\beta\\
\text{(iii) }\left(\alpha+\frac{\beta}{2}\right)-\left(2\chi+\frac{\beta}{2}\right)\vee\gamma_{2}=\alpha-2\chi\geqslant\alpha-\beta\\
\text{(iv) }\left(\frac{\alpha}{2}+\beta\right)-\left(2\chi+\frac{\beta}{2}\right)\vee\gamma_{2}=\frac{\alpha+\beta}{2}-2\chi\end{cases}\geqslant\frac{\alpha-\beta}{2},

in each of the four cases respectively. This achieves (
4.21
),
and recall that plugging (
4.21
) into (
4.20
) we
obtain

(4.25)

\rho^{-\gamma_{2}}\mathcal{J}_{\tau_{1}}^{1}\leqslant C_{\alpha,\beta}\,\lambda^{\frac{\beta-\alpha}{2}},

that is relation (
4.19
) for
{\mathcal{J}}^{1}
.

Step 4: Estimation of

{\mathcal{J}}^{2}
. We
first recall an elementary estimate, which holds true for all
r,c>0

(4.26)

e^{-r}\leqslant c^{c}r^{-c}.

Observe that (
4.26
) can be seen from
x\leqslant e^{x}

for
x>0
and taking
x=r/c
. Now we recall that
{\mathcal{J}}^{2}

is defined by (
4.18
). We use (
4.26
) in this definition
and choose
r\triangleq(\lambda+\theta\rho)s
, with a parameter
c>0

to be specified later on. We end up with

\rho^{-\gamma_{2}}{\mathcal{J}}_{\tau_{1}}^{2}\leqslant\frac{c^{c}\rho^{2\chi-\gamma_{2}}}{(\lambda+\theta\rho)^{c}}\int_{0}^{\tau_{1}}s^{\alpha/2+\beta-\gamma_{2}-c-1}(\tau_{2}-s)^{-\beta/2}ds.

Therefore the elementary change of variable
s\triangleq\tau_{1}u

yields

\rho^{-\gamma_{2}}{\mathcal{J}}_{\tau_{1}}^{2}\leqslant\frac{c^{c}\rho^{2\chi-\gamma_{2}}\tau_{1}^{\alpha/2+\beta-\gamma_{2}-c}}{(\lambda+\theta\rho)^{c}}\int_{0}^{1}u^{\alpha/2+\beta-\gamma_{2}-c-1}(\tau_{2}-\tau_{1}u)^{-\beta/2}du,

and since we have assumed
\tau_{1}\leqslant\tau_{2}
we can write
(\tau_{2}-\tau_{1}u)^{-\beta/2}\leqslant\tau_{1}^{-\beta/2}(1-u)^{-\beta/2}
.
We get

(4.27)

\rho^{-\gamma_{2}}{\mathcal{J}}_{\tau_{1}}^{2}\leqslant\frac{c^{c}\rho^{2\chi-\gamma_{2}}\tau_{1}^{\alpha/2+\beta/2-\gamma_{2}-c}}{(\lambda+\theta\rho)^{c}}\int_{0}^{1}u^{\alpha/2+\beta-\gamma_{2}-c-1}(1-u)^{-\beta/2}du.

We now pick a convenient value for the parameter
c
above. Indeed,
one can choose
c=\frac{\alpha}{2}+\frac{\beta}{2}-\gamma_{2}
, so
that
\tau_{1}^{\alpha/2+\beta/2-\gamma_{2}-c}=1
. With this value
of
c
, we let the reader check that the exponent
\alpha/2+\beta-\gamma_{2}-c-1

in the right hand side of (
4.27
) is larger than
-1
. It follows
that

(4.28)

\rho^{-\gamma_{2}}{\mathcal{J}}_{\tau_{1}}^{2}\leqslant C_{\alpha,\beta}\cdot\frac{\rho^{2\chi-\gamma_{2}}}{(\lambda+\theta\rho)^{\alpha/2+\beta/2-\gamma_{2}}}.

Notice that the ratio in (
4.28
) has the form of (
4.22
),
with
b=2\chi
and
a=(\alpha+\beta)/2
. In addition, our relation
(
3.20
) on
\alpha,\beta,\chi
ensures that
b<a
. Thus according
to (
4.24
), we obtain that

(4.29)

\rho^{-\gamma_{2}}\mathcal{J}_{\tau_{1}}^{2}\leqslant C_{\alpha,\beta}\cdot\lambda^{(2\chi)\vee\gamma_{2}-\frac{\alpha+\beta}{2}}\leqslant C_{\alpha,\beta}\cdot\lambda^{\frac{\beta-\alpha}{2}},

where we have resorted to the fact that
(2\chi)\vee\gamma_{2}\leqslant\beta

for the second inequality above. Therefore, we have established the
estimate (
4.19
) for
{\mathcal{J}}^{2}
.

Summarizing our considerations so far, we have proved (
4.19
)
by gathering (
4.29
) and (
4.25
). Therefore recalling that
we have set
{\mathcal{J}}={\mathcal{J}}^{1}+{\mathcal{J}}^{2}
and
relation (
4.16
), we get that the term
\mathcal{D}_{\tau_{1}}

defined by (
4.15
) satisfies

(4.30)

\big|{\mathcal{D}}_{\tau_{1}}(w)\big|\leqslant C_{\alpha,\beta}\cdot\tau_{1}^{\gamma_{1}-\gamma_{2}}\kappa\Theta P_{u}(w)E(a,\tau_{2})e^{\theta(1+T)\sqrt{T}|w|}\cdot\lambda^{\gamma+\frac{\beta-\alpha}{2}}\cdot\rho^{\gamma_{2}}.

Step 5: Conclusion
. Recall that our aim is to achieve
the upper bound (
4.7
). With (
4.9
) and (
4.15
)
in hand, the left hand side of (
4.7
) can be written as

\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\partial_{x\cdots x}^{k}p_{1}(w)|\,{\mathcal{D}}_{\tau_{1}}(w)\,dw.

Therefore plugging (
4.30
) into the above expression, we get
that (recall
\rho=a+\sqrt{T}|w|
):

\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\partial_{x\cdots x}^{k}p_{1}(w)|\,{\mathcal{D}}_{\tau_{1}}(w)\,dw\leqslant C_{\alpha,\beta}\cdot\kappa\Theta E(a,\tau_{2})\lambda^{\gamma+\frac{\beta-\alpha}{2}}\tau_{1}^{\gamma_{2}-\gamma_{1}}\\
\times\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\partial_{x\cdots x}^{k}p_{1}(w)|\cdot P_{u}(w)\cdot e^{\theta(1+T)\sqrt{T}|w|}(a+\sqrt{T}|w|)^{\gamma_{2}}dw\,.

Hence some elementary heat kernel estimates entail

(4.31)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\partial_{x\cdots x}^{k}p_{1}(w)|\,{\mathcal{D}}_{\tau_{1}}(w)\,dw

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle C_{\alpha,\beta,k,\sigma}e^{\theta^{2}(1+T)^{3}}\cdot\kappa\Theta E(a,\tau_{2})\lambda^{\gamma+\frac{\beta-\alpha}{2}}\tau_{1}^{\gamma_{2}-\gamma_{1}}\cdot a^{\gamma_{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle C_{\alpha,\beta,k,\theta,T,\sigma}\cdot\kappa\Theta E(a,\tau_{2})\cdot\lambda^{\gamma+\frac{\beta-\alpha}{2}}a^{\gamma_{2}}\tau_{1}^{\gamma_{2}-\gamma_{1}}.

This finishes the proof of (
4.7
). For sake of conciseness
we leave the proof of (
4.8
) to the patient reader. It
is based on the same kind of arguments as for (
4.7
), also
taking into account the extra regularity brought in by the increments

v_{\tau_{2}-s}(z)-v_{\tau_{2}-s}(x)
. ∎

For our future computations it will be useful to extend
the estimate (
4.8
) to a context with time increments of

v
. This is the content of the following lemma.

Lemma 4.5
.

We assume that the hypothesis of Lemma

4.3
hold true. In particular, we consider a process

v
in
\mathcal{B}^{\theta,\lambda}
and we recall that the parameter

\gamma
in Definition
3.9
satisfies
\gamma=\frac{\alpha-\beta}{4}
.
Then the following bound holds true:

(4.32)

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{0}^{\tau_{1}}\frac{|\partial_{x\cdots x}^{k}p_{1}(w)|}{s^{1+\sigma_{1}}}\,\Big|\int_{x}^{x+\sqrt{s}w}(v_{\tau_{2}-s}(z)-v_{\tau_{2}}(x))dW(z)\Big|dwds\\
\leqslant C\cdot\kappa\Theta E(a,\tau_{2})a^{\chi}\left(a^{\beta/2}+\tau_{2}^{-\beta/2}\right)\cdot\tau_{1}^{\beta-\sigma_{1}}.

Proof.

We decompose the increment
v_{\tau_{2}-s}(z)-v_{\tau_{2}}(x)

into

v_{\tau_{2}-s}(z)-v_{\tau_{2}}(x)=\left[v_{\tau_{2}-s}(z)-v_{\tau_{2}-s}(x)\right]+\left[v_{\tau_{2}-s}(x)-v_{\tau_{2}}(x)\right]\,.

Then notice that the integral (
4.32
) corresponding to

v_{\tau_{2}-s}(z)-v_{\tau_{2}-s}(x)
has been handled in (
4.8
).
We will thus focus on the following term:

(4.33)

{\mathcal{E}}_{x,\tau_{1},\tau_{2}}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{0}^{\tau_{1}}\frac{|\partial_{x\cdots x}^{k}p_{1}(w)|}{s^{1+\sigma_{1}}}\big|\int_{x}^{x+\sqrt{s}w}(v_{\tau_{2}-s}(x)-v_{\tau_{2}}(x))dW(z)\big|dwds.

In order to bound
{\mathcal{E}}_{x,\tau_{1},\tau_{2}}
we observe
that the increment
v_{\tau_{2}-s}(x)-v_{\tau_{2}}(x)
does not depend
on the variable
z
. Therefore we have

(4.34)

{\mathcal{E}}_{x,\tau_{1},\tau_{2}}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{0}^{\tau_{1}}\frac{|\partial_{x\cdots x}^{k}p_{1}(w)|}{s^{1+\sigma_{1}}}|v_{\tau_{2}-s}(x)-v_{\tau_{2}}(x)|\cdot|W^{1}(x,x+\sqrt{s}w)|dwds\,.

Next we bound the increments of
v
resorting to the bound on
\llbracket v\rrbracket_{\beta/2,\beta}

ensured by Definition
3.9
, together with
the definition (
3.25
) of
\llbracket\,\cdot\,\rrbracket_{\beta/2,\beta}
.
This yields

(4.35)

\big|v_{\tau_{2}-s}(x)-v_{\tau_{2}}(x)\big|\leqslant a^{\beta/2}E(a,\tau_{2})\Theta\cdot s^{\beta/2},

The increments of
W
can be estimated thanks to the fact that
\kappa_{\alpha,\chi}({\bf W})

(see equation (
3.21
)) is a finite quantity. We get

(4.36)

\big|W^{1}(x,x+\sqrt{s}w)\big|\leqslant\kappa\cdot(a+\sqrt{T}|w|)^{\chi}\cdot s^{\alpha/2}\cdot|w|^{\alpha}\leqslant C_{\chi,T}\cdot\kappa\cdot P_{u}(w)\cdot a^{\chi}s^{\alpha/2},

where we recall that
P_{u}(w)
designates any polynomial in the

w
variable. Now plugging (
4.35
) and (
4.36
)
into (
4.34
) we obtain

{\mathcal{E}}_{x,\tau_{1},\tau_{2}}\leqslant C\cdot\kappa a^{\chi+\beta/2}E(a,\tau_{2})\Theta\cdot\int_{0}^{\tau_{1}}s^{\frac{\alpha+\beta}{2}-1-\sigma_{1}}ds=C\cdot\kappa a^{\chi+\beta/2}E(a,\tau_{2})\Theta\cdot\tau_{1}^{\frac{\alpha+\beta}{2}-\sigma_{1}}.

Then we trivially bound
\tau_{1}^{\frac{\alpha+\beta}{2}}
by
C\tau_{1}^{\beta}

(recall that
\tau_{1}
will be chosen as a small constant) and
a^{\beta/2}

by
a^{\beta/2}+t^{-\beta/2}
. We end up with

{\mathcal{E}}_{x,\tau_{1},\tau_{2}}\leqslant C\cdot\kappa E(a,\tau_{2})\Theta\cdot\tau_{1}^{\beta-\sigma_{1}}a^{\chi}\left(a^{\beta/2}+t^{-\beta/2}\right),

which means that we have achieved the bound (
4.32
) for

{\mathcal{E}}_{x,\tau_{1},\tau_{2}}
. Our claim (
4.32
)
is thus obtained thanks to the considerations at the beginning of
our proof.
∎

4.3.
Estimating the norm of
\mathcal{M}\mathcal{V}

Recall that our main object of interest is the transformation
\mathcal{M}\mathcal{V}

introduced in Section
3.3.3
. For convenience recall
that this transformation is defined in (
3.28
) as

(4.37)

\mathcal{M}\mathcal{V}\triangleq[(t,x)\mapsto((\mathcal{M}\mathcal{V})_{t}(x),\partial_{W}(\mathcal{M}\mathcal{V})_{t}(x))],

where the first term
(\mathcal{M}v)_{t}(x)
is defined by (
3.17
)
and where the Gubinelli derivative
\partial_{W}(\mathcal{M}\mathcal{V})_{t}

is defined in (
3.29
). One of our main steps toward
the proof of Theorem
3.11
is the following contraction property
for the norm
\Theta^{\theta,\lambda}(\mathcal{M}\mathcal{V})
.

Proposition 4.6
.

Consider a set of parameters
\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\chi,\theta,\lambda

satisfying (
3.20
). Let
\mathcal{M}
be the map whose definition
is recalled in (
4.37
). Then
\mathcal{M}
is
a well-defined linear transformation on the Banach space
\mathcal{B}^{\theta,\lambda}

introduced in Definition
3.9
. It satisfies
the following estimate:

(4.38)

\Theta^{\theta,\lambda}(\mathcal{M}\mathcal{V})\leqslant C\cdot(1+\kappa_{\alpha,\chi}({\bf W}))\cdot\lambda^{-\frac{\alpha-\beta}{4}}\cdot\Theta^{\theta,\lambda}(\mathcal{V}),

where
C
is a constant depending only on all the parameters
\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\chi,\theta,\lambda
,
but not on
{\bf W}
and
\mathcal{V}
. In particular, by choosing

\lambda
to be large enough, we can ensure that

(4.39)

\Theta^{\theta,\lambda}(\mathcal{M}\mathcal{V})\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\Theta^{\theta,\lambda}(\mathcal{V}).

Therefore
\mathcal{M}
is a contraction on
\mathcal{B}^{\theta,\lambda}
.

Going back to the Definition
3.9
of
\mathcal{B}^{\theta,\lambda}

and its norm
\Theta^{\theta,\lambda}
, the estimate for
\mathcal{M}

can be split in four main terms (i) a uniform estimate for
\mathcal{M}\mathcal{V}
,
(ii) an estimate for the time fluctuations of
\mathcal{M}\mathcal{V}
,
(iii) a bound on the spatial fluctuations of
\mathcal{M}\mathcal{V}

and (iv) a control on the remainder
\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{M}\mathcal{V}}
.
We develop these four estimates in the following sections. At this
point it is helpful to recall the following change of variables, which
will be extensively used in the sequel and is valid for
s>0
,
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
,
with
y=x+\sqrt{s}w
, and
k\geq 1
:

(4.40)

\left.\partial_{x\cdots x}^{k}p_{s}(x-y)\right|_{y=x+\sqrt{s}w}=C_{\sigma}\cdot s^{-\frac{k+1}{2}}\partial_{x\cdots x}^{k}p_{1}(w).

Unless otherwise stated, we always shorten our notation as in (
4.2
)
in order to avoid lengthy expressions.

4.3.1.
The uniform estimate for
\mathcal{M}\mathcal{V}

We begin the proof of Proposition
4.6
with the following
simple uniform estimate for
(\mathcal{M}\mathcal{V})_{t}(x).

Lemma 4.7
.

Under the conditions of Proposition
4.6
,
consider
t\in[0,T]
and
x\in[-a,a]
. Then we have

(4.41)

\big|(\mathcal{M}\mathcal{V})_{t}(x)\big|\leqslant C\cdot\kappa\Theta E(a,t)\cdot\lambda^{-\frac{\alpha-\beta}{4}}.

Proof.

Starting from the expression (
3.17
) for
\mathcal{M}v
, writing

y=x+\sqrt{s}w
and invoking relation (
4.40
)
we get

(4.42)

(\mathcal{M}\mathcal{V})_{t}(x)=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\partial_{xx}^{2}p_{1}(w)}{s}\left(\int_{x}^{x+\sqrt{s}w}v_{t-s}(z)dW(z)\right)dw\,ds.

Our claim (
4.41
) is thus a direct consequence of Lemma

4.3
(i) with
k=2
,
\gamma_{1}=\gamma_{2}=0
and
\tau_{1}=\tau_{2}=t
.
∎

4.3.2.
The time variation estimate for
\mathcal{M}\mathcal{V}

In this section we investigate the time fluctuations of
\mathcal{M}\mathcal{V}
,
which is another step toward the proof of Proposition
4.6
.

Lemma 4.8
.

We work under the conditions of Proposition
4.6
.
In particular, recall that
\alpha,\beta
satisfy (
3.20
).
Then for any elements
x\in[-a,a]
and
t_{1},t_{2}\in[0,T]
such
that
t_{1}<t_{2}
, we have

(4.43)

\big|(\mathcal{M}\mathcal{V})_{t_{2}}(x)-(\mathcal{M}\mathcal{V})_{t_{1}}(x)\big|\leqslant C\kappa\Theta E(a,t_{2})a^{\beta/2}\lambda^{-\frac{\alpha-\beta}{4}}|t_{2}-t_{1}|^{\beta/2}.

Proof.

Invoking equation (
3.17
), it is readily checked
that the time increments of
\mathcal{M}\mathcal{V}
can be written
as

(\mathcal{M}\mathcal{V})_{t_{2}}(x)-(\mathcal{M}\mathcal{V})_{t_{1}}(x)=\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{xx}^{2}p_{t_{2}-s}(x-y)\int_{x}^{y}v_{s}(z)dW(z)dyds\\
+\int_{0}^{t_{1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\partial_{xx}^{2}p_{t_{2}-s}(x-y)-\partial_{xx}^{2}p_{t_{1}-s}(x-y)\right)\int_{x}^{y}v_{s}(z)dW(z)dyds.

Hence, writing the increment
\partial_{xx}^{2}p_{t_{2}-s}(x-y)-\partial_{xx}^{2}p_{t_{1}-s}(x-y)

in terms of a time derivative and owing to the fact that
p
satisfies

\partial_{t}p=\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}\partial_{xx}^{2}p
, we get a
decomposition of the form

(4.44)

(\mathcal{M}\mathcal{V})_{t_{2}}(x)-(\mathcal{M}\mathcal{V})_{t_{1}}(x)=\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}\mathcal{T}_{1}+\mathcal{T}_{2},

where the terms
\mathcal{T}_{1}
and
\mathcal{T}_{2}
are respectively
defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle C_{\sigma}\int_{0}^{t_{1}}ds\int_{\mathbb{R}}dy\int_{t_{1}-s}^{t_{2}-s}\partial_{x}^{4}p_{u}(x-y)du\int_{x}^{y}v_{s}(z)dW(z)

(4.45)

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{xx}^{2}p_{t_{2}-s}(x-y)\int_{x}^{y}v_{s}(z)dW(z)dyds,

where we write
\partial_{x}^{4}p
instead of
\partial_{xxxx}^{4}p

for notational sake. We now estimate
\mathcal{T}_{1}
and
\mathcal{T}_{2}

separately.

Estimation of

\mathcal{T}_{1}
: By a change of variables

w=(y-x)/\sqrt{u},
and owing to relation (
4.40
)
we can write
\mathcal{T}_{1}
as

(4.46)

\mathcal{T}_{1}=C_{\sigma}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{x}^{4}p_{1}(w)dw\int_{0}^{t_{1}}ds\int_{t_{1}-s}^{t_{2}-s}u^{-2}du\int_{x}^{x+\sqrt{u}w}v_{s}(z)dW(z).

We first look at the triple integral inside the
w
-integral, that
is

(4.47)

\mathcal{T}_{1,x,w,t_{1},t_{2}}=\int_{0}^{t_{1}}ds\int_{t_{1}-s}^{t_{2}-s}u^{-2}du\int_{x}^{x+\sqrt{u}w}v_{s}(z)dW(z).

In order to upper bound
\mathcal{T}_{1,x,w,t_{1},t_{2}}
we perform
the change of variable
u=t_{1}+r-s
so that we get

\mathcal{T}_{1,x,w,t_{1},t_{2}}=\int_{0}^{t_{1}}ds\int_{0}^{t_{2}-t_{1}}\frac{dr}{(t_{1}+r-s)^{2}}\int_{x}^{x+\sqrt{t_{1}+r-s}w}v_{s}(z)dW(z).

Then switching the order of integration in
r
and
s
and setting

\rho=t_{1}+r-s
we obtain

\mathcal{T}_{1,x,w,t_{1},t_{2}}=\int_{0}^{t_{2}-t_{1}}dr\int_{r}^{t_{1}+r}\frac{d\rho}{\rho^{1+\beta/2+1-\beta/2}}\int_{x}^{x+\sqrt{\rho}w}v_{t_{1}+r-\rho}(z)dW(z).

This can be easily bounded as

(4.48)

\Big|\mathcal{T}_{1,x,w,t_{1},t_{2}}\big|\leqslant\int_{0}^{t_{2}-t_{1}}r^{\beta/2-1}dr\int_{0}^{t_{1}+r}\frac{d\rho}{\rho^{1+\beta/2}}\left|\int_{x}^{x+\sqrt{\rho}w}v_{t_{1}+r-\rho}(z)dW(z)\right|.

Therefore, plugging (
4.48
) into (
4.47
)
and then (
4.46
), we end up with

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{1}

\displaystyle\leqslant\int_{\mathbb{R}}\big|\partial_{x}^{4}p_{1}(w)\big|dw\int_{0}^{t_{2}-t_{1}}r^{\beta/2-1}dr\int_{0}^{t_{1}+r}\frac{d\rho}{\rho^{1+\beta/2}}\Big|\int_{x}^{x+\sqrt{\rho}w}v_{t_{1}+r-\rho}(z)dW(z)\Big|

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t_{2}-t_{1}}r^{\beta/2-1}dr\cdot\int_{\mathbb{R}}\big|\partial_{x}^{4}p_{1}(w)\big|dw\int_{0}^{t_{1}+r}\frac{d\rho}{\rho^{1+\beta/2}}\Big|\int_{x}^{x+\sqrt{\rho}w}v_{t_{1}+r-\rho}(z)dW(z)\Big|.

We can now resort to Lemma
4.3
(i) with
\gamma_{1}=\gamma_{2}=\beta/2
.
This yields

(4.49)

\mathcal{T}_{1}\leqslant C\kappa\Theta E(a,t_{2})a^{\beta/2}\lambda^{\gamma+\frac{\beta-\alpha}{2}}|t_{2}-t_{1}|^{\beta/2}=C\kappa\Theta E(a,t_{2})a^{\beta/2}\lambda^{-\frac{\alpha-\beta}{4}}|t_{2}-t_{1}|^{\beta/2},

where in the second identity we have used the fact that
\gamma=\frac{\alpha-\beta}{4}
.

Estimation of

\mathcal{T}_{2}
: The upper bound for
\mathcal{T}_{2}

is obtained similarly to
\mathcal{T}_{1}
. Namely starting from
expression (
4.45
) some elementary change of variables
in the space and time variables yield

\mathcal{T}_{2}=\int_{0}^{t_{2}-t_{1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{xx}^{2}p_{r}(x-y)\int_{x}^{y}v_{t_{2}-r}(z)dW(z)dydr.

Therefore setting
y=x+\sqrt{r}w
and resorting to (
4.40
)
in order to replace
p_{r}
by
p_{1}
, we get

\mathcal{T}_{2}=C_{\sigma}\int_{0}^{t_{2}-t_{1}}\frac{dr}{r}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{xx}^{2}p_{1}(w)\int_{x}^{x+\sqrt{r}w}v_{t_{2}-r}(z)dW(z)dw.

The above identity enables the application of (
4.7
), where
we choose
\gamma_{1}=\gamma_{2}=0
. We get

(4.50)

\mathcal{T}_{2}\leqslant C\kappa\Theta E(a,t_{2})a^{\beta/2}\lambda^{-\frac{\alpha-\beta}{4}}|t_{2}-t_{1}|^{\beta/2}.

Hence reporting (
4.49
) and (
4.50
) into the decomposition (
4.44
), the proof of
our claim (
4.41
) is easily achieved.
∎

4.3.3.
The space variation estimate for
\mathcal{M}\mathcal{V}

This section is devoted to the third ingredient in our global strategy,
namely the upper bound on the
\beta
-Hölder norm for the spatial
increments of
\mathcal{M}\mathcal{V}
.
In order to ease notation, we will often use the convention

(4.51)

f(x,x^{\prime})\equiv f(x)-f(x^{\prime}),

valid for any function
f
of a spatial variable
x
in
\mathbb{R}
or

\delta\mathbb{Z}
. This notation will prevail for the remainder of the article.
Our main aim is to prove
the lemma below.

Lemma 4.9
.

Let the assumptions and notations of Proposition

4.6
prevail. Then for any
t\in(0,T]
and
x,x^{\prime}\in[-a,a],

we have

(4.52)

\big|(\mathcal{M}\mathcal{V})_{t}(x,x^{\prime})\big|\leqslant C\cdot\kappa\Theta E(a,t)\cdot\lambda^{-\frac{\alpha-\beta}{4}}a^{\beta/2}|x^{\prime}-x|^{\beta}.

Proof.

Before starting our discussion, recall our identity (
3.13
),
for which we had chosen an arbitrary
a\in\mathbb{R}
. Differentiating
this relation with respect to
x
and taking limits as
\eta\to 0

in the rough path sense, we get that the integral

(4.53)

\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{xx}^{2}p_{s}(x-y)\int_{a}^{y}v_{t-s}(z)dW(z)dyds

does not depend on
a
. This fact will be used without further mention
in the remainder of the proof.

As an application of (
4.53
), let
t\in(0,T]
and
x,x^{\prime}\in[-a,a].
Starting from
the expression (
3.17
) for
\mathcal{M}\mathcal{V}
and owing
to the fact that the lower bound
x
can be chosen arbitrarily in

\int_{x}^{y}v_{s}(z)dW(z)
, it is readily checked that

(4.54)

(\mathcal{M}\mathcal{V})_{t}(x,x^{\prime})=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\partial_{xx}^{2}p_{s}(x^{\prime}-y)-\partial_{xx}^{2}p_{s}(x-y)\right)\int_{x}^{y}v_{t-s}(z)dW(z)dyds.

We now divide our analysis in two cases
according to the respective value of
x^{\prime}-x
and
t
.

Case
(i):
|x^{\prime}-x|^{2}\leqslant t.
With identity
(
4.54
) in mind, let us split the interval
[0,t]

into
[0,|x^{\prime}-x|]\cup[|x^{\prime}-x|,t]
in order to get the following decomposition

(4.55)

(\mathcal{M}\mathcal{V})_{t}(x^{\prime})-(\mathcal{M}\mathcal{V})_{t}(x)=\mathcal{I}_{1}(x^{\prime})-\mathcal{I}_{1}(x)+\mathcal{I}_{2},

where for
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
we have set

(4.56)

\mathcal{I}_{1}(\xi)=\int_{0}^{|x^{\prime}-x|^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\partial_{xx}^{2}p_{s}(\xi-y)\int_{\xi}^{y}v_{t-s}(z)dW(z)\right)dyds,

and where the term
\mathcal{I}_{2}
is given by

(4.57)

\mathcal{I}_{2}=\int_{|x^{\prime}-x|^{2}}^{t}ds\int_{\mathbb{R}}dy\int_{x}^{x^{\prime}}\partial_{x}^{3}p_{s}(u-y)du\int_{x}^{y}v_{t-s}(z)dW(z),

where we use again
\partial_{x}^{3}p
instead of
\partial_{xxx}^{3}p

for notational sake. We proceed to estimate the term
\mathcal{I}_{1}(\xi)

in (
4.56
). To this aim we resort to the same type of change
of variables as in Lemma
4.8
and invoke the identity
(
4.40
) once more. We get

|\mathcal{I}_{1}(\xi)|\leqslant\int_{0}^{|x^{\prime}-x|^{2}}\frac{ds}{s}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\big|\partial_{xx}^{2}p_{1}(w)\big|dw\left|\int_{\xi}^{\xi+\sqrt{s}w}v_{t-s}(z)dW(z)\right|.

We are thus in position to apply Lemma
4.3
(i) with

\tau_{1}=|x^{\prime}-x|^{2}
,
\gamma_{2}=\beta/2
and
\gamma_{1}=0
.
Also we recall that we have chosen
\gamma=\frac{\alpha-\beta}{4}
.
This implies

(4.58)

|\mathcal{I}_{1}(\xi)|\leqslant C\cdot\kappa\Theta E(a,t)\cdot\lambda^{-\frac{\alpha-\beta}{4}}a^{\beta/2}|x^{\prime}-x|^{\beta}.

Let us turn to an upper bound on the term
\mathcal{I}_{2}
in (
4.57
).
Switching the order of integration and thanks to (
4.40
),
one can write

\displaystyle|\mathcal{I}_{2}|

\displaystyle\leqslant\int_{x}^{x^{\prime}}du\int_{|x^{\prime}-x|^{2}}^{t}\frac{ds}{s^{1/2-\beta/2+1+\beta/2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\big|\partial_{x}^{3}p_{1}(w)\big|dw\left|\int_{u}^{u+\sqrt{s}w}v_{t-s}(z)dW(z)\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant|x^{\prime}-x|^{\beta-1}\int_{x}^{x^{\prime}}du\int_{0}^{t}\frac{ds}{s^{1+\beta/2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\big|\partial_{x}^{3}p_{1}(w)\big|dw\left|\int_{u}^{u+\sqrt{s}w}v_{t-s}(z)dW(z)\right|,

where we have enlarged the term
\frac{1}{s^{1/2-\beta/2}}
to
|x^{\prime}-x|^{\beta-1}

and then enlarged the
s
-integral over the region
[0,t].
By applying
Lemma
4.3
(i) to the inner
(s,w,z)
-integral with

\gamma_{1}=\gamma_{2}=\beta/2
, we get

(4.59)

|\mathcal{I}_{2}|\leqslant C\cdot\kappa\Theta E(a,t)\cdot\lambda^{-\frac{\alpha-\beta}{4}}a^{\beta/2}|x^{\prime}-x|^{\beta}.

Reporting (
4.58
) and (
4.59
) into (
4.55
) we have

\big|\mathcal{M}\mathcal{V}_{t}(x,x^{\prime})\big|\leqslant C\cdot\kappa\Theta E(a,t)\cdot\lambda^{-\frac{\alpha-\beta}{4}}a^{\beta/2}|x^{\prime}-x|^{\beta}.

Therefore our claim (
4.52
) is proved whenever
|x^{\prime}-x|^{2}\leqslant t
.

Case
(ii):
|x^{\prime}-x|^{2}>t.
This case is essentially
contained in the above
\mathcal{I}_{1}(\xi)
-estimate. Indeed, starting
directly from (
4.54
) we can write

\mathcal{MV}_{t}(x,x^{\prime})=\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{1}(x^{\prime})-\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{1}(x),

where

\mathcal{I}_{1}^{\prime}(\xi)\triangleq\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{xx}^{2}p_{s}(\xi-y)\int_{\xi}^{y}v_{t-s}(z)dW(z)dyds,\ \ \ \xi\in\mathbb{R}.

The only difference between
\mathcal{I}_{1}^{\prime}(\xi)
and
\mathcal{I}_{1}(\xi)

introduced in Case (i) is that the region for the
s
-integral becomes

[0,t]
rather than
[0,|x^{\prime}-x|^{2}]
. By exactly the same argument
as before, the estimate (
4.58
) becomes

|\mathcal{I}_{1}^{\prime}(\xi)|\leqslant C\cdot\kappa\Theta E(a,t)\cdot\lambda^{-\frac{\alpha-\beta}{4}}a^{\beta/2}t^{\beta/2}.

Since
|x^{\prime}-x|^{2}>t
, it follows that

\displaystyle\big|\mathcal{MV}_{t}(x,x^{\prime})\big|

\displaystyle\leqslant\big|\mathcal{I}_{1}^{\prime}(x^{\prime})\big|+\big|\mathcal{I}_{1}^{\prime}(x)\big|

\displaystyle\leqslant C\cdot\kappa\Theta E(a,t)\cdot\lambda^{-\frac{\alpha-\beta}{4}}a^{\beta/2}|x^{\prime}-x|^{\beta}.

This achieves (
4.52
) for
|x^{\prime}-x|^{2}>t
and finishes our proof.
∎

4.3.4.
The remainder estimate for
\mathcal{M}\mathcal{V}

Recall that the remainder of a controlled process is introduced in
(
3.23
). Moreover, we defined the Gubinelli derivative of
\mathcal{M}\mathcal{V}

as
-2v/\sigma^{2}
in (
3.29
). Gathering those
two relations and recalling notation (
4.51
), we get that the remainder of
(\mathcal{M}\mathcal{V})_{t}

is given as

(4.60)

\mathcal{R}_{t}^{\mathcal{M}\mathcal{V}}(x,x^{\prime})=\mathcal{M}\mathcal{V}_{t}(x,x^{\prime})+\frac{2}{\sigma^{2}}v_{t}(x)W^{1}(x,x^{\prime}).

We will now prove that
\mathcal{R}_{t}^{\mathcal{M}\mathcal{V}}

is indeed a
2\beta
-Hölder increment.

Lemma 4.10
.

Let
\alpha,\beta,\chi,\gamma,\theta,\lambda

be parameters such that (
3.20
) is fulfilled. Recall that the
functions
E
and
Q
are introduced in Definition
3.8
,
and that the norm
\Theta
is given in Definition
3.9
.
Then the remainder
\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{M}\mathcal{V}}
defined
by (
4.60
) satisfies

(4.61)

\big|\mathcal{R}_{t}^{\mathcal{M}\mathcal{V}}(x,x^{\prime})\big|\leqslant C\cdot\kappa E(a,t)\Theta\cdot Q(a,t)\cdot|x^{\prime}-x|^{2\beta},

for all
t\in(0,T]
and
x,x^{\prime}\in[-a,a]
.

A crucial point for proving Lemma
4.10
is to make use
of the decomposition in the following lemma. This decomposition, together with
the estimates developed in Lemma
4.12
and Lemma
4.13

below, will explain why the Gubinelli derivative of
(\mathcal{M}\mathcal{V})_{t}

should be defined to be
-2v_{t}/\sigma^{2}
.

Lemma 4.11
.

As in Lemma
4.10
, we consider the
remainder
\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{M}\mathcal{V}}
given by (
4.60
).
Then for all
t\in[0,T]
and
x,x^{\prime}\in[-a,a]
we have

(4.62)

\mathcal{R}_{t}^{\mathcal{M}\mathcal{V}}(x,x^{\prime})=\mathcal{R}_{t}^{0}(x,x^{\prime})+\frac{2}{\sigma^{2}}v_{t}(x)\left(P_{t}W(x^{\prime})-P_{t}W(x)\right),

where the increment
\mathcal{R}^{0}
is defined by

(4.63)

\mathcal{R}_{t}^{0}(x,x^{\prime})=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\partial_{xx}^{2}p_{s}(x^{\prime}-y)-\partial_{xx}^{2}p_{s}(x-y)\right)\left(\int_{x}^{y}\left(v_{t-s}(z)-v_{t}(x)\right)dW(z)\right)dyds

and where
P_{t}
has been defined after (
3.5
) as
the heat semigroup in
\mathbb{R}
with variance
\sigma^{2}
.

Proof.

Recall that starting from the expression (
3.17
)
for
\mathcal{M}\mathcal{V}
we already obtained the space increment
(
4.54
). We now add and substract
v_{t}(x)

in the integral on the right hand side of (
4.54
).
With the definition (
4.63
) of
\mathcal{R}^{0}
in mind,
this yields

(4.64)

\mathcal{MV}_{t}(x,x^{\prime})=\mathcal{R}_{t}^{0}(x,x^{\prime})+v_{t}(x)\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\partial_{xx}^{2}p_{s}(x^{\prime}-y)-\partial_{xx}^{2}p_{s}(x-y)\right)\,W^{1}(x,y)\,dyds.

Furthermore, the decaying properties of the heat kernel
p
imply
that
\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{xx}^{2}p_{s}(x^{\prime}-y)dy=0
for all

x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
. Therefore one can insert
\pm W(x^{\prime})
in (
4.64
)
and one gets

(4.65)

\mathcal{MV}_{t}(x,x^{\prime})=\mathcal{R}_{t}^{0}(x,x^{\prime})+v_{t}(x)\left(\mathcal{B}_{t}(x^{\prime})-\mathcal{B}_{t}(x)\right),

where the term
\mathcal{B}_{t}(\xi)
is defined for
\xi\in\mathbb{R}

by

\mathcal{B}_{t}(\xi)=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{xx}^{2}p_{s}(\xi-y)\,W^{1}(\xi,y)dyds.

Next we simplify the expression for
\mathcal{B}_{t}(\xi)
by involving
the relation
\partial_{xx}^{2}p_{s}(y)=\frac{2}{\sigma^{2}}\partial_{s}p_{s}(y)
.
Interchanging integral and differentiation, we obtain

(4.66)

\mathcal{B}_{t}(\xi)=\frac{2}{\sigma^{2}}\int_{0}^{t}\partial_{s}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}p_{s}(\xi-y)\,W^{1}(\xi,y)dy\right)ds=\frac{2}{\sigma^{2}}\int_{0}^{t}\partial_{s}\left(P_{s}W(\xi)-W(\xi)\right)ds,

where we recall that
P_{s}
designates the heat semigroup and where
we have used the fact that
\int_{\mathbb{R}}p_{s}(z)dz=1
. We now
simply evaluate the time integral in (
4.66
) in order
to get

\mathcal{B}_{t}(\xi)=\frac{2}{\sigma^{2}}\left(P_{t}W(\xi)-W(\xi)\right).

Plugging this identity in (
4.65
) we end up with

\displaystyle\mathcal{MV}_{t}(x,x^{\prime})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{t}^{0}(x,x^{\prime})+\frac{2}{\sigma^{2}}v_{t}(x)\left(\left(P_{t}W(x^{\prime})-W(x^{\prime})\right)-\left(P_{t}W(x)-W(x)\right)\right),

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{t}^{0}(x,x^{\prime})+\frac{2}{\sigma^{2}}v_{t}(x)\left(P_{t}W(x^{\prime})-P_{t}W(x)\right)-\frac{2}{\sigma^{2}}v_{t}(x)\left(W(x^{\prime})-W(x)\right),

from which our claim (
4.62
) is easily deduced. ∎

In view of Lemma
4.11
, the estimate of
\mathcal{R}^{(\mathcal{M}\mathcal{V})_{t}}(x,x^{\prime})

contains two ingredients: the
\mathcal{R}_{t}^{0}(x,x^{\prime})
-estimate
and the heat semigroup variation estimate. We develop these two ingredients
separately, starting with an estimate for
\mathcal{R}_{t}^{0}(x,x^{\prime})
.

Lemma 4.12
.

We consider the setting of Lemma
4.10
,
and let
\mathcal{R}^{0}
the increment defined by (
4.63
).
Then for any
t\in(0,T]
and
x,x^{\prime}\in[-a,a],
we have

(4.67)

\big|\mathcal{R}_{t}^{0}(x,x^{\prime})\big|\leqslant C\cdot\kappa E(a,t)\Theta\cdot Q(a,t)\cdot|x^{\prime}-x|^{2\beta}.

Proof.

As in the proof of Lemma
4.9
we divide
our discussion into two cases, according to the value of
|x^{\prime}-x|
.

Case (i):

|x^{\prime}-x|^{2}\leqslant t.
In this case, we start
with the following decomposition:

(4.68)

\mathcal{R}_{t}^{0}(x,x^{\prime})=\mathcal{J}+\mathcal{K},

where the terms
\mathcal{J}
and
\mathcal{K}
are respectively
defined by

(4.69)

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}:=\int_{0}^{|x^{\prime}-x|^{2}}ds\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\partial_{xx}^{2}p_{s}(x^{\prime}-y)-\partial_{xx}^{2}p_{s}(x-y)\right)\left(\int_{x}^{y}(v_{t-s}(z)-v_{t}(x))dW(z)\right)dy

(4.70)

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}:=\int_{|x^{\prime}-x|^{2}}^{t}ds\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\partial_{xx}^{2}p_{s}(x^{\prime}-y)-\partial_{xx}^{2}p_{s}(x-y)\right)\left(\int_{x}^{y}(v_{t-s}(z)-v_{t}(x))dW(z)\right)dy.

Next we further divide the integral
\mathcal{J}
in (
4.69
)
as

(4.71)

\mathcal{J}=\mathcal{J}_{1}(x^{\prime})-\mathcal{J}_{1}(x)+\mathcal{J}_{2},

where the terms
\mathcal{J}_{1}(\xi)
and
\mathcal{J}_{2}
are
given by

(4.72)

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{1}(\xi)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{|x^{\prime}-x|^{2}}ds\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{xx}^{2}p_{s}(\xi-y)\left(\int_{\xi}^{y}(v_{t-s}(z)-v_{t}(\xi))dW(z)\right)dy,

(4.73)

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{|x^{\prime}-x|^{2}}ds\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{xx}^{2}p_{s}(x^{\prime}-y)\left((v_{t}(x^{\prime})-v_{t}(x))\cdot W^{1}(x^{\prime},y)\right)dy.

We now proceed to estimate the terms in (
4.69
)-(
4.73
).
Let us start with a bound on the term
\mathcal{J}_{1}(\xi)
defined
by (
4.72
). To this aim, we first resort to the same kind
of change of variables as for (
4.46
). We get

|\mathcal{J}_{1}(\xi)|\leqslant\int_{0}^{|x^{\prime}-x|^{2}}ds\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{|\partial_{xx}^{2}p_{1}(w)|}{s}\cdot\left|\int_{\xi}^{\xi+\sqrt{s}w}(v_{t-s}(z)-v_{t}(\xi))dW(z)\right|dw.

We are now in a position to apply Lemma
4.5
directly (with
\sigma_{1}=0
),
which yields

(4.74)

|\mathcal{J}_{1}(\xi)|\leqslant C\cdot\kappa\Theta E(a,t)a^{\chi}\cdot Q(a,t)\cdot|x^{\prime}-x|^{2\beta}.

As far as the
\mathcal{J}_{2}
integral is concerned, we perform
our usual change of variable in space. We obtain

(4.75)

\mathcal{J}_{2}=\int_{0}^{|x^{\prime}-x|^{2}}\frac{ds}{s}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{xx}^{2}p_{1}(w)\cdot(v_{t}(x^{\prime})-v_{t}(x))\cdot W^{1}(x^{\prime},x^{\prime}+\sqrt{s}w)\,dw.

Then we estimate the right hand side of (
4.75
) thanks to
the fact that
v
verifies Definition
3.9

and
\kappa_{\alpha,\chi}(w)
, as introduced in (
3.21
), is
finite. We obtain

(4.76)

\displaystyle|v_{t}(x^{\prime})-v_{t}(x)|

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle E(a,t)\Theta a^{\beta/2}|x^{\prime}-x|^{\beta},

(4.77)

\displaystyle\big|W^{1}(x^{\prime},x^{\prime}+\sqrt{s}w)\big|

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle C\kappa P_{u}(w)a^{\chi}s^{\alpha/2}.

By substituting (
4.76
)-(
4.77
) into (
4.75
)
and performing the
w
-integral, we arrive at

(4.78)

|\mathcal{J}_{2}|\leqslant C\cdot\kappa E(a,t)\Theta a^{\chi+\beta/2}|x^{\prime}-x|^{\beta}\cdot\int_{0}^{|x^{\prime}-x|^{2}}s^{\alpha/2-1}ds\\
\leqslant C\cdot\kappa E(a,t)\Theta a^{\chi+\beta/2}|x^{\prime}-x|^{\beta}\cdot|x^{\prime}-x|^{\alpha}\leqslant C\cdot\kappa\Theta E(a,t)\cdot Q(a,t)\cdot|x^{\prime}-x|^{2\beta}.

Next we estimate the
\mathcal{K}
-integral given by (
4.70
).
We start by writing
\partial_{xx}^{2}p_{s}(x^{\prime}-y)-\partial_{xx}^{2}p_{s}(x-y)

in terms of a spatial derivative. This yields

\mathcal{K}=\int_{|x^{\prime}-x|^{2}}^{t}ds\int_{\mathbb{R}}dy\int_{x}^{x^{\prime}}\partial_{x}^{3}p_{s}(u-y)du\int_{u}^{y}(v_{t-s}(z)-v_{t}(x))dW(z)dy,

where we have used the fact that integrals like (
4.53
) do not depend on the parameter
a
.
Then we insert
\pm v_{t}(u)
in the integral above in order to get
the decomposition

(4.79)

\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{K}_{1}+\mathcal{K}_{2},

where
\mathcal{K}_{1}
and
\mathcal{K}_{2}
are defined by

(4.80)

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{|x^{\prime}-x|^{2}}^{t}ds\int_{\mathbb{R}}dy\int_{x}^{x^{\prime}}\partial_{x}^{3}p_{s}(u-y)du\int_{u}^{y}(v_{t-s}(z)-v_{t}(u))dW(z)dy,

(4.81)

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{|x^{\prime}-x|^{2}}^{t}ds\int_{\mathbb{R}}dy\int_{x}^{x^{\prime}}\partial_{x}^{3}p_{s}(u-y)du(v_{t}(u)-v_{t}(x))W^{1}(u,y)dy.

We now devote our efforts to upper bound
\mathcal{K}_{1}
and
\mathcal{K}_{2}
.

In order to estimate the
\mathcal{K}_{1}
-integral, we
set again
y=u+\sqrt{s}w
. We obtain

\mathcal{K}_{1}=\int_{x}^{x^{\prime}}du\int_{|x^{\prime}-x|^{2}}^{t}\frac{ds}{s^{3/2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{x}^{3}p_{1}(w)\int_{u}^{u+\sqrt{s}w}(v_{t-s}(z)-v_{t}(u))dW(z)dw.

Along the same lines as for the estimation of
\mathcal{I}_{2}
in
the proof of Lemma
4.9
, we bound the term
s^{-(1/2-\beta)}

by
|x^{\prime}-x|^{\beta-1}
and we enlarge the
s
-integral to the interval

[0,t]
. We get

\mathcal{K}_{1}\leqslant|x^{\prime}-x|^{1-2\beta}\cdot\int_{x}^{x^{\prime}}du\int_{0}^{t}\frac{ds}{s^{1+\beta}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\big|\partial_{x}^{3}p_{1}(w)\big|\cdot\left|\int_{u}^{u+\sqrt{s}w}(v_{t-s}(z)-v_{t}(u))dW(z)\right|dw.

We are now able to apply Lemma
4.5
with
\sigma_{1}=\beta

and we obtain

(4.82)

\mathcal{K}_{1}\leqslant C\cdot\kappa\Theta E(a,t)\cdot Q(a,t)\cdot|x^{\prime}-x|^{2\beta}.

Let us bound the term
\mathcal{K}_{2}
defined by (
4.81
).
The change of variable
y=u+\sqrt{s}w
together with (
4.40
)
yields

(4.83)

\mathcal{K}_{2}=\int_{x}^{x^{\prime}}du\int_{|x^{\prime}-x|^{2}}^{t}\frac{ds}{s^{3/2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{x}^{3}p_{1}(w)(v_{t}(u)-v_{t}(x))W^{1}(u,u+\sqrt{s}w)dw.

In addition, owing to (
3.21
) and the fact that
v
fulfills
the assumptions of Definition
3.9
, we have

|v_{t}(u)-v_{t}(x)|\leqslant E(a,t)\Theta\cdot a^{\beta/2}|x^{\prime}-x|^{\beta}\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\big|W^{1}(u,u+\sqrt{s}w)\big|\leqslant C\kappa P_{u}(w)a^{\chi}s^{\alpha/2}.

Plugging this information into (
4.83
) we get

(4.84)

\mathcal{K}_{2}\leqslant C\cdot\kappa\Theta E(a,t)\cdot a^{\chi+\beta/2}\cdot|x^{\prime}-x|^{1+\beta}\cdot\int_{|x^{\prime}-x|^{2}}^{t}s^{\alpha/2-3/2}ds.

Performing the
s
-integral and resorting to the relation
|x^{\prime}-x|^{2}\leqslant t
,
we end up with

\mathcal{K}_{2}\leqslant C\cdot\kappa\Theta E(a,t)\cdot a^{\chi+\beta/2}\cdot|x^{\prime}-x|^{\alpha+\beta}\leqslant C\cdot\kappa\Theta E(a,t)\cdot Q(a,t)\cdot|x^{\prime}-x|^{2\beta}.

Let us summarize our considerations so far: we plug (
4.74
)
and (
4.78
) into the decomposition (
4.71
)
for
\mathcal{J}
. We also gather (
4.82
) and (
4.84
)
into the decomposition (
4.79
) for
\mathcal{K}
. Then
we report those estimates into the main decomposition (
4.68
).
This achieves our claim (
4.67
) for
|x^{\prime}-x|^{2}\leqslant t
.

Case (ii):

|x^{\prime}-x|^{2}>t.
This case is essentially contained
in the estimate of the term
\mathcal{J}
defined by (
4.69
).
That is, going back to the expression (
4.63
) for
\mathcal{R}_{t}^{0}

and using the fact that the expression (
4.53
) does
not depend on
a
, one can write we have

(4.85)

\mathcal{R}_{t}^{0}(x,x^{\prime})=\mathcal{L}_{1}(x)+\mathcal{L}_{1}(x^{\prime})+\mathcal{L}_{2},

where the quantities
\mathcal{L}_{1}(\xi)
and
\mathcal{L}_{2}

are given by

(4.86)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1}(\xi)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}ds\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{xx}^{2}p_{s}(\xi-y)\int_{\xi}^{y}(v_{t-s}(z)-v_{t}(\xi))dW(z)dy,

(4.87)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}ds\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{xx}^{2}p_{s}(x^{\prime}-y)\cdot\left(v_{t}(x^{\prime})-v_{t}(x)\right)W^{1}(x^{\prime},y)dy.

Then invoking the same kind of arguments as for the term
\mathcal{I}_{1}(\xi)

in (
4.72
), plus the fact that
|x^{\prime}-x|^{2}>t
, we obtain

|\mathcal{L}_{1}(\xi)|\leqslant C\cdot\kappa\Theta E(a,t)\cdot Q(a,t)\cdot t^{\beta}\leqslant C\cdot\kappa\Theta E(a,t)\cdot Q(a,t)\cdot|x^{\prime}-x|^{2\beta}.

Furthermore, thanks to the space regularity of
v
and owing to the
decay of the heat kernel
p
(see (
4.76
)-(
4.77
)
for similar arguments) we get

(4.88)

|\mathcal{L}_{2}|\leqslant C\cdot\kappa E(a,t)\Theta a^{\chi+\beta/2}|x^{\prime}-x|^{\beta}\cdot\int_{0}^{t}s^{\alpha/2-1}ds\\
\leqslant C\cdot\kappa E(a,t)\Theta a^{\chi+\beta/2}|x^{\prime}-x|^{\beta}\cdot t^{\alpha/2}\leqslant C\cdot\kappa E(a,t)\Theta\cdot Q(a,t)\cdot|x^{\prime}-x|^{2\beta}.

Gathering (
4.87
) and (
4.88
) into (
4.85
),
we have shown (
4.67
) for
|x^{\prime}-x|^{2}>t
. Now the
proof of Lemma
4.12
is complete. ∎

4.3.5.
Estimation of the heat semigroup variation

Remember that we have obtained the decomposition (
4.62
)
for
\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{MV}}
. This decomposition involves two
main terms, namely
\mathcal{R}_{t}^{0}(x,x^{\prime})
and the increment

P_{t}W(x^{\prime})-P_{t}W(x)
. In this section we handle the latter increment.
Our main result is summarized in the following lemma.

Lemma 4.13
.

We stick to the notation of Lemma
4.10

and consider the double-sided Brownian motion
W
of equation (
3.1
).
Then for any
t\in(0,T]
and
x,x^{\prime}\in[-a,a],
we have

(4.89)

\big|P_{t}W(x^{\prime})-P_{t}W(x)\big|\leqslant C\cdot\kappa\cdot a^{\chi}\cdot t^{-\beta/2}\cdot|x^{\prime}-x|^{2\beta}.

Proof.

As in Lemmas
4.9
and
4.12
,
we will separate the cases
|x^{\prime}-x|^{2}\leqslant t
and
|x^{\prime}-x|^{2}>t
.

Case (i)
:
|x^{\prime}-x|^{2}\leqslant t
. We start from the from
the very definition of
P_{t}W(x)
and write the increment
p_{t}(x^{\prime}-y)-p_{t}(x-y)

as a spatial derivative in order to get

P_{t}W(x^{\prime})-P_{t}W(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}(p_{t}(x^{\prime}-y)-p_{t}(x-y))W(y)dy=\int_{x}^{x^{\prime}}du\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{x}p_{t}(u-y)W(y)dy.

Then perform the change of variable
y=u+\sqrt{t}w
, invoke relation
(
4.40
) and use the decay properties of
\partial_{x}p_{1}
.
This yields

(4.90)

\displaystyle P_{t}W(x^{\prime})-P_{t}W(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}}\int_{x}^{x^{\prime}}du\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{x}p_{1}(w)W(u+\sqrt{t}w)\,dw

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}}\int_{x}^{x^{\prime}}du\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{x}p_{1}(w)W^{1}(u,u+\sqrt{t}w)dw.

Note that in (
4.90
) the variable
u
is an element
of
[x,x^{\prime}]\subseteq[-a,a].
Therefore, recalling the definition (
3.21
)
of
\kappa
one can bound the increment
W^{1}(u,u+\sqrt{t}w)
as

|W^{1}(u,u+\sqrt{t}w)|\leqslant\kappa\cdot(a+\sqrt{t}|w|)^{\chi}\cdot t^{\alpha/2}|w|^{\alpha}\leqslant C\kappa P_{u}(w)a^{\chi}t^{\alpha/2},

where we recall that
P_{u}(w)
designates a generic polynomial in

w
. Plugging this inequality in (
4.90
) and performing
the integral with respect to the variable
w
, we end up with

(4.91)

\displaystyle\big|P_{t}W(x^{\prime})-P_{t}W(x)\big|

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle C\cdot\kappa a^{\chi}t^{\alpha/2-1/2}|x^{\prime}-x|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle C\cdot\kappa a^{\chi}|x^{\prime}-x|^{2\beta}\cdot|x^{\prime}-x|^{1-2\beta}t^{-\beta/2}t^{\frac{\alpha-\beta}{2}}\cdot\left(\frac{|x^{\prime}-x|^{2}}{t}\right)^{1/2-\beta}

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle C\cdot\kappa a^{\chi}t^{-\beta/2}\cdot|x^{\prime}-x|^{2\beta},

where we have resorted to the fact that
|x^{\prime}-x|^{2}\leqslant t
for
the last step. The estimate (
4.91
) proves
our claim (
4.89
) when
|x^{\prime}-x|^{2}\leqslant t
.

Case (ii):
|x^{\prime}-x|^{2}>t
.
In this case, we simply write

P_{t}W(x^{\prime})-P_{t}W(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}p_{t}(y)W^{1}(x-y,x^{\prime}-y)dy.

Then we invoke (
3.21
) and the change of variable
y=u+\sqrt{t}w

again in order to get

\big|P_{t}W(x^{\prime})-P_{t}W(x)\big|\leqslant\kappa|x^{\prime}-x|^{\alpha}\int_{\mathbb{R}}(a+|y|)^{\chi}p_{t}(y)dy=\kappa|x^{\prime}-x|^{\alpha}\int_{\mathbb{R}}(a+\sqrt{t}|w|)^{\chi}p_{1}(w)dw

Observe that whenever
t<|x^{\prime}-x|^{2}
, we also have
\sqrt{t}\leqslant a
.
Hence we obtain

\displaystyle\big|P_{t}W(x^{\prime})-P_{t}W(x)\big|

\displaystyle\leqslant C\cdot\kappa a^{\chi}\cdot|x^{\prime}-x|^{\alpha}=C\cdot\kappa a^{\chi}\cdot t^{-\beta/2}|x^{\prime}-x|^{2\beta}\cdot t^{\beta/2}|x^{\prime}-x|^{\alpha-2\beta}

(4.92)

\displaystyle\leqslant C\cdot\kappa a^{\chi}\cdot t^{-\beta/2}|x^{\prime}-x|^{2\beta}\cdot t^{\frac{\alpha-\beta}{2}}\leqslant C^{\prime}\cdot\kappa a^{\chi}\cdot t^{-\beta/2}|x^{\prime}-x|^{2\beta}.

We have now proved our claim (
4.89
) when
|x^{\prime}-x|^{2}>t
.
Hence gathering (
4.91
) and (
4.92
)
the desired estimate (
4.89
) is achieved. ∎

We now gather the previous lemmas and prove our main estimate
for the remainder
\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{MV}}
.

Proof of Lemma
4.10
.

The remainder
\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{MV}}

has been decomposed in (
4.62
). Then for an estimate of
the term
\mathcal{R}_{t}^{0}(x,x^{\prime})
in (
4.62
), we simply
refer to Lemma
4.12
. Next we deal with the other term
in (
4.62
), namely

\frac{2}{\sigma^{2}}v_{t}(x)\left(P_{t}W(x^{\prime})-P_{t}W(x)\right).

To this aim, we combine the fact that according to Definition
3.8

we have

\llbracket v\rrbracket_{\beta/2,\beta}^{[0,t]\times[-a,a]}\leqslant E^{\theta,\lambda}(a,t),

together with Lemma
4.13
. We obtain

\big|v_{t}(x)\big|\cdot\big|P_{t}W(x^{\prime})-P_{t}W(x)\big|\leqslant C\cdot\kappa\Theta E(a,t)\cdot a^{\chi}t^{-\beta/2}\cdot|x^{\prime}-x|^{2\beta}.

Gathering this upper bound with our estimate of
\mathcal{R}_{t}^{0}(x,x^{\prime})
,
the proof of Lemma
4.10
is now complete. ∎

4.3.6.
Putting all the estimates together

Having all the previous variational estimates at hand, we can now
complete the proof of Proposition
4.6
.

First of all, Lemma
4.7
gives

(4.93)

\|\mathcal{M}\mathcal{V}\|_{\infty}^{[0,t]\times[-a,a]}\leqslant C\cdot\kappa\Theta E(a,t)\cdot\lambda^{-\frac{\alpha-\beta}{4}}.

Next, Lemma
4.8
and Lemma
4.9
yield

(4.94)

\|\mathcal{M}\mathcal{V}\|_{\beta/2,\beta}^{[0,t]\times[-a,a]}\leqslant C\cdot\kappa\Theta E(a,t)\cdot\lambda^{-\frac{\alpha-\beta}{4}}a^{\beta/2}.

In addition, since we defined
\partial_{W}(\mathcal{MV})
as
-2v/\sigma^{2}

in (
3.29
) and
\mathcal{V}
is a controlled process
satisfying Definition
3.8
, we get

(4.95)

\llbracket\partial_{W}(\mathcal{M}\mathcal{V})\rrbracket_{\beta/2,\beta}^{[0,t]\times[-a,a]}=\frac{2}{\sigma^{2}}\cdot\llbracket v\rrbracket_{\beta/2,\beta}^{[0,t]\times[-a,a]}\leqslant\frac{2}{\sigma^{2}}\cdot\Theta E(a,t).

Finally, Lemma
4.10
yields

(4.96)

\|\mathcal{R}^{(\mathcal{M}\mathcal{V})_{t}}\|_{2\beta}\leqslant C\cdot\kappa\Theta E(a,t)\cdot Q(a,t),

where the functions
E
and
Q
are introduced in (
3.26
).
As a result, plugging (
4.93
)-(
4.96
)
into the definition (
3.27
) of the norm
\Theta=\Theta^{\theta,\lambda}(\mathcal{V})

and recalling that we have chosen
\gamma=\frac{\alpha-\beta}{4}
,
we end up with

\Theta^{\theta,\lambda}(\mathcal{M}\mathcal{V})=\sup_{a\geqslant 1,t\in[0,T]}E(a,t)^{-1}\cdot\left(\llbracket\mathcal{M}\mathcal{V}\rrbracket_{\beta/2,\beta}^{[0,t]\times[-a,a]}+\lambda^{-\gamma}\llbracket\partial_{W}(\mathcal{M}\mathcal{V})\rrbracket_{\beta/2,\beta}^{[0,t]\times[-a,a]}\right.\\
\left.+\lambda^{-\gamma}Q(a,t)^{-1}\|\mathcal{R}^{(\mathcal{M}\mathcal{V})_{t}}\|_{2\beta}\right)\leqslant C(\kappa+1)\lambda^{-\frac{\alpha-\beta}{4}}\Theta.

Therefore, the proof of Proposition
4.6
is complete.

4.4.
Estimating the deterministic input
functions

Recall that the main fixed point problem is associated with the transformation

\hat{\mathcal{M}}
defined by

\hat{\mathcal{M}}:\mathcal{V}\mapsto\hat{\mathcal{M}}\mathcal{V}\triangleq\psi^{1}+\psi^{2}+\mathcal{M}\mathcal{V},

where

(4.97)

\psi_{t}^{1}(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{x}p_{t}(x-y)f_{0}(y)dy,\quad\text{and}\quad\psi_{t}^{2}(x)=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{x}p_{t-s}(x-y)g_{s}(y)dyds.

In order to complete the proof of Theorem
3.11
, we now need
to show that the functions
\psi^{1},\psi^{2}
can be considered
as controlled processes in the space
\mathcal{B}^{\theta,\lambda}

introduced in Definition
3.8
. In order, to achieve
this goal, we assume in this section that
f_{0}\in C_{b}^{2}(\mathbb{R})

and
g\in C_{b}^{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}).

4.4.1.
Estimation of
\psi^{1}

In this section we focus on the term
\psi^{1}
and prove that this
function can be considered as a controlled process. We summarize our
conclusions in the following lemma.

Lemma 4.14
.

Let
f_{0}
be a function in
C_{b}^{2}(\mathbb{R})
,
and consider
\psi^{1}
defined by (
4.97
). Then

\psi^{1}
is an element of
\mathcal{B}^{\theta,\lambda}
, whose
norm
\Theta
(see relation (
3.27
)) can be bounded
as

\Theta^{\theta,\lambda}(\psi^{1})\leqslant C\|f_{0}^{\prime\prime}\|_{\infty}.

Proof.

We first look at the time variations of
\psi^{1}
.
Namely, let
t_{1}<t_{2}.
By definition, we have

\psi_{t_{2}}^{1}(x)-\psi_{t_{1}}^{1}(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}(\partial_{x}p_{t_{2}}(x-y)-\partial_{x}p_{t_{1}}(x-y))f_{0}(y)dy.

Therefore writing
\partial_{x}p_{t_{2}}-\partial_{x}p_{t_{1}}
in
terms of a time derivative, resorting to the relation
\partial_{s}p_{s}=\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}\partial_{xx}^{2}p_{s}

and setting
y=\sqrt{s}w
we get

(4.98)

\psi_{t_{2}}^{1}(x)-\psi_{t_{1}}^{1}(x)=\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\frac{ds}{s^{3/2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{x}^{3}p_{1}(w)f_{0}(x+\sqrt{s}w)dw.

Furthermore, thanks to the decaying properties of the heat kernel

p_{t}
, a straightforward integration by parts procedure shows that

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{x}^{3}p_{t}(w)wdw=-\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{xx}^{2}p_{t}(w)dw=-2\partial_{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}}p_{t}(w)dw=0,

where we have invoked the relation
\int_{\mathbb{R}}p_{t}(w)dw=1

for the last step. In the same way, we also have
\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{x}^{3}p_{t}(w)dw=0

for all
t>0
