\mathcal{A}^{\delta}_{x,t_{j},w,t_{k}}\triangleq\big|\mathcal{I}_{t_{k-1}-t_{j}}^{1,\delta}(x,x+\delta\lfloor\delta^{-1}\sqrt{t_{j}}w\rfloor)\big|.

The right hand side of (
6.43
) resembles its continuous counterpart (
4.7
). As a result, we consider
a stochastic integral term similar to (
4.7
) in our discrete
context.
For
x\in[-a,a]
and our time horizon
T
, set
\rho\triangleq a+T^{1/2}|w|
.
Then along the same lines as for (
4.13
) and invoking Proposition
6.13
, we get

t_{j}^{-1}{\mathcal{A}}_{x,t_{j},w,t_{k}}^{\delta}\leqslant c_{\beta}\kappa\Theta\lambda^{\gamma}E(a,t_{k})P_{u}(w)e^{\theta(1+T)T^{1/2}|w|}e^{-(\lambda+\theta\rho)t_{j}}\phi_{1}(\rho,t_{j}),

where we recall that
P_{u}(w)
designates an arbitrary polynomial
in
w
and where the function
\phi_{i}
,
i=1,2
, is defined by
(
4.14
) (in the current estimate we simply take
\gamma_{1}=\gamma_{2}=0
and thus
\phi_{1}=\phi_{2}
). By substituting the above inequality into (
6.43
), we obtain that

(6.44)

\big|(\mathcal{M}_{1}^{1,\delta}v^{\delta})_{t_{k}}(x)\big|\leqslant C_{3}\kappa\Theta\lambda^{\gamma}E(a,t_{k})\cdot\delta^{2}\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}\delta t_{j}^{-1/2}\sum_{w\in(\delta t_{j}^{-1/2})\cdot\mathbb{Z}}e^{-C_{3}|w|^{2}}e^{-(\lambda+\theta\rho)t_{j}}\phi_{1}(\rho,t_{j}).

Due to the explicit form of
\phi_{1}(\rho,t_{j})
, a simple monotonicity consideration (replacing each grid point
w=(\delta t_{j}^{-1/2})\cdot l
with a generic point in the interval
[(\delta t_{j}^{-1/2})\cdot l,(\delta t_{j}^{-1/2})\cdot(l+1)]
) shows that the discrete spatial
w
-integral in (
6.44
) is uniformly bounded by its continuous counterpart, namely we have

\delta t_{j}^{-1/2}\sum_{w\in(\delta t_{j}^{-1/2})\cdot\mathbb{Z}}e^{-C_{3}|w|^{2}}e^{-(\lambda+\theta\rho)t_{j}}\phi_{1}(\rho,t_{j})\leqslant C_{4}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-C_{3}|w|^{2}}e^{-(\lambda+\theta\rho)t_{j}}\phi_{1}(\rho,t_{j})dw.

It follows that

\big|(\mathcal{M}_{1}^{1,\delta}v^{\delta})_{t_{k}}(x)\big|\leqslant C_{5}\kappa\Theta\lambda^{\gamma}E(a,t_{k})\cdot\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-C_{3}|w|^{2}}dw\times\delta^{2}\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}e^{-(\lambda+\theta\rho)t_{j}}\phi_{1}(\rho,t_{j}).

Note that the discrete
t_{j}
-integral on the right hand side is the discrete counterpart of the quantity (
4.17
) (with
\tau_{1}=t_{k}
). Now we can perform and estimate the discrete
t_{j}
-integral in exactly the same way as in the proof of (
4.19
) to conclude that

\delta^{2}\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}e^{-(\lambda+\theta\rho)t_{j}}\phi_{1}(\rho,t_{j})\leqslant C_{6}\lambda^{\frac{\beta-\alpha}{2}}.

Therefore, we arrive at the following estimate:

(6.45)

\big|(\mathcal{M}_{1}^{1,\delta}v^{\delta})_{t_{k}}(x)\big|\leqslant C_{7}\kappa\Theta E\lambda^{-\frac{\alpha-\beta}{4}}.

(ii) The
j=0
part in the summation (
6.38
) is defined by

(\mathcal{M}_{1}^{0,\delta}v^{\delta})_{t_{k}}(x)\triangleq\delta^{3}\sum_{y\in\delta\mathbb{Z}}\nabla_{x}^{2,\delta}\hat{p}_{0}^{\delta}(x-y)\mathcal{I}_{t_{k-1}}^{1,\delta}(x,y).

This is essentially the quantity
\mathcal{A}_{t}^{1}(x)
defined in (
6.78
) below. By using the discrete heat kernel estimate (
2.81
), exactly the same argument leading to the estimate (
6.87
) gives that

(6.46)

\big|(\mathcal{M}_{1}^{0,\delta}v^{\delta})_{t_{k}}(x)\big|\leqslant C_{8}\kappa\Theta E(a,t_{k})\times\lambda^{\frac{\alpha-\beta}{4}}\delta^{\alpha-2\chi}.

Gathering the estimates (
6.45
) and (
6.46
), one can thus conclude that

(6.47)

\big|(\mathcal{M}_{1}v^{\delta})_{t_{k}}(x)\big|\leq C\kappa\Theta E\Big(\lambda^{-\frac{\alpha-\beta}{4}}+\lambda^{\frac{\alpha-\beta}{4}}\delta^{\alpha-2\chi}\Big).

Step 3: Estimating
\mathcal{M}_{2}^{\delta}v^{\delta}.

By the definition (
6.38
) of
\mathcal{M}_{2}^{\delta}v^{\delta},

we can write it as

(6.48)

(\mathcal{M}_{2}^{\delta}v^{\delta})_{t_{k}}(x)=\delta^{3}\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\sum_{y\in\delta\mathbb{Z}}\nabla_{x}^{2,\delta}\hat{p}_{t_{j}}^{\delta}(x-y)\cdot\left(\delta\sum_{z=x}^{y}\frac{v_{t_{k-1}-t_{j}}^{\delta}(z)+v_{t_{k-1}-t_{j}}^{\delta}(z-\delta)}{2}\right)\cdot c^{\prime}_{\delta},

where we recall that
c^{\prime}_{\delta}
is defined by (
6.41
),
and that
c^{\prime}_{\delta}=O(\delta^{1/2})
. Here we view
\mathcal{M}_{2}^{\delta}v^{\delta}
as a discrete controlled path with respect to
\hat{{\bf U}}_{1}^{\delta}
with zero Gubinelli derivative.
Viewed as a discrete Lebesgue integral, it is easily seen that

(6.49)

\left|\delta\sum_{z=x}^{y}\frac{v_{t_{k-1}-t_{j}}^{\delta}(z)+v_{t_{k-1}-t_{j}}^{\delta}(z-\delta)}{2}\right|\leqslant|y-x|\cdot\|v\|_{\infty}^{[0,t_{k}]\times[(-|x|\vee|y|,|x|\vee|y|)]}.

We now estimate
(\mathcal{M}_{2}^{\delta}v^{\delta})_{t_{k}}(x)

(for
t_{k}\in\llbracket 0,T\rrbracket
and
x\in\llbracket-a,a\rrbracket
)
by decomposing the
j
-summation (
6.48
) into
j=0
and

1\leqslant j\leqslant k-1
as before (since the two parts have different heat kernel
estimates). Firstly, according to the estimate (
2.81
) for
\nabla_{x}^{2,\delta}\hat{p}_{0}^{\delta}

as well as (
6.49
), the “
j=0
” term in (
6.48
)
is magnified by

\delta^{3}\sum_{y\in\delta\mathbb{Z}:|y-x|\leqslant\delta}\frac{C}{\delta^{3}}\times\delta\Theta E(a,t_{k})\times c^{\prime}_{\delta}\leqslant C\delta^{3/2}\Theta E(a,t_{k}).

Similarly, by applying (
2.80
) together with a change of variables
y=x+\delta\lfloor\delta^{-1}t_{j}^{1/2}w\rfloor
,
the “
j>0
” term is estimated as

C_{1}c^{\prime}_{\delta}\times\delta^{2}\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}\frac{1}{\sqrt{t_{j}}}\left(\frac{\delta}{\sqrt{t_{j}}}\sum_{w\in(t_{j}^{-1/2}\delta)\cdot\mathbb{Z}}|w|e^{-C_{2}|w|^{2}}e^{C_{3}|w|}\right)\times\Theta E(a,t_{k}).

The normalised
w
-summation inside the bracket, resembled as a discrete
approximation of the continuous integral
\int_{\mathbb{R}}|w|e^{-C_{2}|w|^{2}+C_{3}|w|}dw
,
is uniformly bounded. The normalised
j
-summation
\delta^{2}\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}1/\sqrt{t_{j}}
,
resembled as a discrete approximation of
\int_{0}^{t}\frac{ds}{\sqrt{s}}
,
is also uniformly bounded. As a consequence, we arrive at the estimate

(6.50)

\big|(\mathcal{M}_{2}^{\delta}v^{\delta})_{t_{k}}(x)\big|\leqslant C\sqrt{\delta}\Theta E(a,t_{k}).

Step 4: Conclusion.
As mentioned above,
we have focused on proper generalizations of Lemmas
4.2
,

4.3
and
4.7
for the sake of conciseness.
The estimations on the time variation, space variation and remainder
terms for
\mathcal{M}_{i}^{\delta}v^{\delta}
(
i=1,2
) can be established in the same lines as Lemmas
4.8
,
4.9
,
4.10
in the continuous case with the aid of the uniform discrete heat kernel estimates (
2.80
). Note that the
j=0
part is always handled separately as before by using (
2.81
) instead. This will result in a factor of
\lambda^{\frac{\alpha-\beta}{4}}\delta^{\alpha-\beta}
(cf. (
6.116
), the second last term of (
6.137
) and (
6.165
) below, respectively). The main estimates, which are similar to (
6.47
)-(
6.50
) are summarised as follows:

(ii) Time variation estimate:

\begin{cases}\big|(\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\delta}v^{\delta})_{t_{2}}(x)-(\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\delta}v^{\delta})_{t_{1}}(x)\big|\leqslant C\left(\lambda^{-\frac{\alpha-\beta}{4}}+\lambda^{\frac{\alpha-\beta}{4}}\delta^{\alpha-\beta}\right)\cdot\kappa\Theta E(a,t_{2})a^{\beta/2}\cdot|t_{2}-t_{1}|^{\beta/2};\\
\big|(\mathcal{M}_{2}^{\delta}v^{\delta})_{t_{2}}(x)-(\mathcal{M}_{2}^{\delta}v^{\delta})_{t_{1}}(x)\big|\leqslant C\sqrt{\delta}\cdot\Theta E(a,t_{2})\cdot|t_{2}-t_{1}|^{\beta/2},\end{cases}

(iii) Space variation estimate:

\begin{cases}\big|(\mathcal{M}_{2}^{\delta}v^{\delta})_{t}(x^{\prime})-(\mathcal{M}_{2}^{\delta}v^{\delta})_{t}(x)\big|\leqslant C\left(\lambda^{-\frac{\alpha-\beta}{4}}+\lambda^{\frac{\alpha-\beta}{4}}\delta^{\alpha-\beta}\right)\cdot\kappa\Theta E(a,t)a^{\beta/2}\cdot|x^{\prime}-x|^{\beta};\\
\big|(\mathcal{M}_{2}^{\delta}v^{\delta})_{t}(x^{\prime})-(\mathcal{M}_{2}^{\delta}v^{\delta})_{t}(x)\big|\leqslant C\sqrt{\delta}\cdot\Theta E(a,t)\cdot|x^{\prime}-x|^{\beta},\end{cases}

(iv) Remainder estimate:

\begin{cases}\big|\mathcal{R}_{t}^{\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\delta}v^{\delta}}(x,x^{\prime})\big|\leqslant C\left(1+\lambda^{\frac{\alpha-\beta}{4}}\delta^{\alpha-\beta}\right)\cdot\kappa\Theta E(a,t)Q(a,t)\cdot|x^{\prime}-x|^{2\beta};\\
\big|\mathcal{R}_{t}^{\mathcal{M}_{2}^{\delta}v^{\delta}}(x,x^{\prime})\big|=\big|(\mathcal{M}_{2}^{\delta}v^{\delta})_{t}(x^{\prime})-(\mathcal{M}_{2}^{\delta}v^{\delta})_{t}(x)-0\cdot\hat{U}^{\delta}(x,x^{\prime})\big|\\
\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \leqslant C\sqrt{\delta}\cdot\Theta E(a,t)t^{-\beta/2}\cdot|x^{\prime}-x|^{2\beta}\leqslant C\sqrt{\delta}\cdot\Theta E(a,t)Q(a,t)\cdot|x^{\prime}-x|^{2\beta}.\end{cases}

As a consequence, we arrive at the desired contraction estimate:

\Theta^{\theta,\lambda}(\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\delta}v^{\delta}+{\mathcal{M}}_{2}^{\delta}v^{\delta})\leqslant C\left(\lambda^{-\frac{\alpha-\beta}{4}}\kappa+\lambda^{\frac{\alpha-\beta}{4}}\delta^{\alpha-\beta}+\sqrt{\delta}\right)\Theta^{\theta,\lambda}(v^{\delta}).

Now we can choose
\lambda
to satisfy

C\lambda^{-\frac{\alpha-\beta}{4}}\hat{\kappa}(\omega)=\frac{1}{4}

where
\hat{\kappa}(\omega)
is defined by (
6.34
) and then require that

0<\delta<\min\big\{\left(\frac{1}{4C}\right)^{2},\left(\frac{1}{4C}\right)^{\frac{2}{\alpha-\beta}}\hat{\kappa}(\omega)^{-\frac{1}{\alpha-\beta}}\big\}.

This ensures that

C\lambda^{-\frac{\alpha-\beta}{4}}\kappa+C\lambda^{\frac{\alpha-\beta}{4}}\delta^{\alpha-\beta}+C\sqrt{\delta}\leqslant\frac{3}{4},

which gives the desired contraction property of
\mathcal{M}^{\delta}
and thus concludes our proof. ∎

The result of Proposition
6.14
easily leads to
the following uniform estimate on the linearly interpolated path
\tilde{v}^{\delta}
(cf. (
6.10
)).

Lemma 6.15
.

Let
\alpha,\beta,\chi,\theta
be given as
in Proposition
6.14
, with
\theta>2
. Recall that
\hat{U}_{1}^{\delta}
is defined in (
5.3
) and Theorem
5.3
, and that
\kappa_{\alpha,\chi}(\hat{{\bf U}}_{1}^{\delta})
is the

\alpha
-Hölder continuous rough path norm of
\hat{{\bf U}}_{1}^{\delta}
.
In addition,
\Theta^{\theta,\lambda}(\tilde{v}^{\delta})
is the
\beta
-Hölder
continuous rough path norm of
\tilde{v}^{\delta}
with respect to

\hat{{\bf U}}_{1}^{\delta}
. Then there exist universal constants
C_{1},C_{2}
depending only on the exponents and
T
, such that

\Theta^{\theta,\lambda}(\tilde{v}^{\delta})\leqslant C_{1}e^{\lambda\delta^{2}}\left(\|f_{0}\|_{\mathcal{C}_{L}^{3}}+\|g\|_{\mathcal{C}_{L}^{3}}\right)

for all
\delta\in(0,\hat{\kappa}^{\prime}(\omega)]
,
where
\lambda>0
is chosen to satisfy (
6.35
) and

\hat{\kappa}^{\prime}(\omega)
is defined by (
6.36
).

Proof.

According to the definition of
\Theta^{\theta,\lambda}
, we need
to estimate four terms: uniform norm, space-variation, time-variation
and the remainder. Let
c
be a universal upper bound of the discrete
rough path norm
\Theta^{\theta/2,\lambda}(v^{\delta})
given by
Proposition
6.14
.

We first consider the uniform norm estimate.
Consider
t\in[0,T]
and
x\in[-a,a]
. We assume that

(6.51)

t_{1}\leqslant t<t_{2},\quad\text{and}\quad x_{1}\leqslant x<x_{2},

for
t_{1},t_{2}
and
x_{1},x_{2}
adjacent points on the grid
\delta^{2}\mathbb{N}\times\delta\mathbb{Z}
. Since we have chosen
\theta>2
, one can apply Proposition
6.14
with
\theta:=\theta/2
. This yields

(6.52)

|\tilde{v}_{t}^{\delta}(x)|\leqslant\max\big\{|v_{t_{i}}^{\delta}(x_{j})|:i,j=1,2\big\}\leqslant c\left(\|f_{0}\|_{\mathcal{C}_{L}^{3}}+\|g\|_{\mathcal{C}_{L}^{3}}\right)\cdot E^{\theta/2,\lambda}(a+\delta,t_{2}).

Furthermore, since we are working with a small
\delta<1
, it is readily checked from (
3.26
) that

(6.53)

E^{\theta/2,\lambda}(a+\delta,t_{2})=e^{\lambda t_{2}+\theta(a+\delta)/2+\theta at_{2}/2}\leqslant C_{\theta}e^{\lambda\delta^{2}}E^{\theta,\lambda}(a,t).

Reporting (
6.53
) into (
6.52
) we end up with the following uniform bound, valid for
t\in[0,T]
and
x\in[-a,a]
:

E^{\theta,\lambda}(a,t)^{-1}|\tilde{v}_{t}^{\delta}(x)|\leqslant cC_{\theta}e^{\lambda\delta^{2}}\left(\|f_{0}\|_{\mathcal{C}_{L}^{3}}+\|g\|_{\mathcal{C}_{L}^{3}}\right).

Next, we consider the space variation estimate. To this aim, we pick
t\in[0,T]
and
x,x^{\prime}\in[-a,a]
. Similarly to (
6.51
), we assume for now that

t_{1}\leqslant t<t_{2},\quad\text{and}\quad x_{1}\leqslant x,x^{\prime}<x_{2}\,,

where
t_{1},t_{2}
and
x_{1},x_{2}
are adjacent grid points. By the definition (
6.10
) of

\tilde{v}^{\delta}
it is clear that

\tilde{v}_{t}^{\delta}(x^{\prime})-\tilde{v}_{t}^{\delta}(x)=\frac{x-x^{\prime}}{x_{2}-x_{1}}\left((1-\mu)v_{t_{1}}^{\delta}(x_{1},x_{2})+\mu v_{t_{2}}^{\delta}(x_{1},x_{2})\right),

where
\mu\triangleq\frac{t-t_{1}}{\delta^{2}}
and
v_{t_{i}}^{\delta}(x_{1},x_{2})\triangleq v_{t_{i}}^{\delta}(x_{2})-v_{t_{i}}^{\delta}(x_{1})
.
Along the same lines as for the uniform bound above, we invoke Proposition
6.14
with
\theta:=\theta/2
. We get

v_{t_{i}}^{\delta}(x_{1},x_{2})\leqslant c\left(\|f_{0}\|_{\mathcal{C}_{L}^{3}}+\|g\|_{\mathcal{C}_{L}^{3}}\right)\cdot E^{\theta/2,\lambda}(a+\delta,t_{i})\lambda^{\frac{\alpha-\beta}{4}}a^{\beta/2}|x_{2}-x_{1}|^{\beta}.

It follows from (
6.53
) that

\displaystyle\big|\tilde{v}_{t}^{\delta}(x^{\prime})-\tilde{v}_{t}^{\delta}(x)\big|

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\|f_{0}\|_{\mathcal{C}_{L}^{3}}+\|g\|_{\mathcal{C}_{L}^{3}}\right)e^{\lambda\delta^{2}}\cdot\big|\frac{x-x^{\prime}}{x_{2}-x_{1}}\big|\cdot E^{\theta,\lambda}(a,t)\cdot\lambda^{\frac{\alpha-\beta}{4}}a^{\beta/2}\cdot|x_{2}-x_{1}|^{\beta}

(6.54)

\displaystyle\leqslant\left(\|f_{0}\|_{\mathcal{C}_{L}^{3}}+\|g\|_{\mathcal{C}_{L}^{3}}\right)e^{\lambda\delta^{2}}\cdot E^{\theta,\lambda}(a,t)\cdot\lambda^{\frac{\alpha-\beta}{4}}a^{\beta/2}\cdot|x^{\prime}-x|^{\beta},

which is the desired estimate. For general
x<x^{\prime}
, let
x_{1}
(respectively,

x_{2}
) be the smallest (respectively, largest) grid point that
is larger than
x
(respectively, smaller than
x^{\prime}
). By considering
the decomposition

\tilde{v}_{t}^{\delta}(x^{\prime})-\tilde{v}_{t}^{\delta}(x)=\tilde{v}_{t}^{\delta}(x^{\prime})-\tilde{v}_{t}^{\delta}(x_{2})+\tilde{v}_{t}^{\delta}(x_{2})-\tilde{v}_{t}^{\delta}(x_{1})+\tilde{v}_{t}^{\delta}(x_{1})-\tilde{v}_{t}^{\delta}(x),

we easily obtain the same type of estimate as in (
6.54
).

The time variation and remainder estimates are treated in a similar
way. For the remainder, the extra point to note is that for
t\in(t_{1},t_{2}]
,
we have
t_{1}^{-\beta/2}\lesssim t^{-\beta/2}
if
t\geqslant\delta^{2}
,
while in the case of
t<\delta^{2}
we do not need the term
t_{1}^{-\beta/2}

due to our definition of
\tilde{v}_{t}^{\delta}
(cf. (
6.10
)).
∎

6.4.
Developing Step 3: comparing
\mathcal{W}^{\delta}
and
\mathcal{W}

Recall that
\mathcal{W}^{\delta}
and
\mathcal{W}
are respectively defined by (
6.14
)
and (
6.15
). This section is devoted to establish an estimate for the distance between
\mathcal{W}^{\delta}
and
\mathcal{W}
, as announced at the end of the Section
6.1
. First in view of the
\hat{U}^{\delta}
-decomposition introduced in (
5.3
)-(
5.4
), we write

\mathcal{W}_{t}^{\delta}(x)=\mathcal{W}_{t}^{1,\delta}(x)+\mathcal{W}_{t}^{2,\delta}(x),

where, for
i=1,2
we have set

(6.55)

\mathcal{W}_{t}^{i,\delta}(x)\triangleq\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\nabla_{x}^{2,\delta}\hat{p}_{\lfloor s\rfloor}^{\delta}(\lfloor x-y\rfloor)\left(\int_{x}^{y}\tilde{v}^{\delta}_{t-s}(z)d\hat{U}_{i}^{\delta}(z)\right)dyds.

From earlier discussions, it is natural to compare
\mathcal{W}^{1,\delta}
with
\mathcal{W}
and view
\mathcal{W}^{2,\delta}
as a remainder.
Let us first handle this remainder term in the decomposition (
6.55
). We label this preliminary step in a lemma.

Lemma 6.16
.

For
\delta>0
let
\mathcal{W}^{2,\delta}
be the process defined by
(
6.55
), where we recall that
\bar{U}_{2}^{\delta}
is defined by
(
5.4
) and
\hat{U}_{2}^{\delta}
is the linear interpolation of
\bar{U}_{2}^{\delta}
. We consider a set of parameters

\alpha,\beta,\chi,\theta,\lambda,\delta
as in Proposition
6.14
. Then there exists a constant
C=C_{\alpha,\beta,\chi,\theta,\lambda,T}
such
that the norm of
\hat{U}_{2}^{\delta}
as a process controlled by
\hat{U}_{1}^{\delta}
satisfies

(6.56)

\Theta_{\hat{U}_{1}^{\delta}}^{\theta,\lambda}(\mathcal{W}^{2,\delta})\leqslant C\left(\|f_{0}\|_{\mathcal{C}_{L}^{3}}+\|g\|_{\mathcal{C}_{L}^{3}}\right)\sqrt{\delta},

where the subscript
\hat{U}_{1}^{\delta}
above means that we consider

\mathcal{W}^{2,\delta}
as a process controlled by
\hat{U}_{1}^{\delta}
.

Proof.

The term
\mathcal{W}^{2,\delta}
is very similar to
\mathcal{M}_{2}^{\delta}v^{\delta}
in (
6.48
). Hence its analysis ressembles what we did in Steps 4 and 5 for the proof of Proposition
6.14
. Namely we consider

\mathcal{W}^{2,\delta}
as a controlled path with respect to
\hat{U}_{1}^{\delta}
, with zero Gubinelli derivative. All the integrals have to be treated as discrete Lebesgue integrals. With the aid of the uniform discrete heat kernel bounds (
2.80
)-(
2.81
), we let the reader check the details leading
to (
6.56
).
∎

With Lemma
6.16
in hand, in what follows we focus on developing the comparison between
\mathcal{W}^{1,\delta}
and
\mathcal{W}
.
Similar to the strategies for Proposition
4.6

and Proposition
6.14
, and recalling that
d_{\hat{{\bf U}}_{1}^{\delta},{\bf W}}(\mathcal{W}^{1,\delta},\mathcal{W})

is defined similarly to (
6.11
), we will split the
study of this quantity into four parts: the uniform distance, the
time variation distance, the space variation distance, and the remainder
distance. In what follows, to simplify notation we will omit all the
super/subscripts when writing the norms and distances. For instance,

\kappa(\hat{{\bf U}}_{1}^{\delta})=\kappa_{\alpha,\chi}(\hat{{\bf U}}_{1}^{\delta})
.
From time to time, we will use “
\apprle
” to denote an inequality
up to a multiplicative constant
C
that does not depend on
\delta,a,t,x,y.

The value of the notation
C
(sometimes denoted as
C_{i}
) may
also differ from line to line.

6.4.1.
The uniform distance estimate

In this subsection we mimic Lemma
4.7
and get a
uniform estimate for
\mathcal{W}^{1,\delta}-\mathcal{W}
.

Lemma 6.17
.

Recall that the exponents
\alpha,\beta,\chi,\theta,\lambda

satisfy (
3.20
) and we set
\gamma=\frac{\alpha-\beta}{4}
. For

\delta>0
,
\mathcal{W}^{1,\delta}
and
\mathcal{W}
are respectively defined by (
6.14
)
and (
6.15
). Then, for any
a\geqslant 1
,
x\in[-a,a]

and
t\in(0,T]
, we have

(6.57)

\big|\mathcal{W}_{t}^{1,\delta}(x)-\mathcal{W}_{t}(x)\big|\\
\leqslant CE(a,t)\lambda^{-\frac{\alpha-\beta}{4}}\left(\kappa(\hat{{\bf U}}_{1}^{\delta})d_{\hat{{\bf U}}_{1}^{\delta},{\bf W}}(\tilde{v}^{\delta},v)+\Theta(v)\rho(\hat{{\bf U}}_{1}^{\delta},{\bf W})+\kappa(\hat{{\bf U}}_{1}^{\delta})\Theta(\tilde{v}^{\delta})\delta^{r}\right),

where
E(a,t)
is defined by (
3.26
),
\rho(\hat{{\bf U}}_{1}^{\delta},{\bf W})

is defined by (
5.9
),
r
is an arbitrary number
that is less than
\alpha-2\chi
and
C
is a positive constant depending only on
\alpha,\beta,T,r
.

Before we prove Lemma
6.17
, we will state some convergence estimates for the discrete and continuous heat kernels
p
. We start with a uniform bound.

Lemma 6.18
.

Consider
\delta>0
,
s\geq\delta^{2}
and
w\in\mathbb{R}
. Let

\hat{p}^{\delta}
be the rescaled kernel from (
2.72
) and
p
be the Gaussian kernel in (
2.73
).
For notational sake we set (see Notation
6.3
for our conventions on integer parts):

(6.58)

k\triangleq\frac{\lfloor\sqrt{s}w\rfloor}{\delta}=\left\lfloor\frac{\sqrt{s}w}{\delta}\right\rfloor_{\mathbb{Z}},\quad\text{and}\quad n\triangleq\frac{\lfloor s\rfloor}{\delta^{2}}=\left\lfloor\frac{s}{\delta^{2}}\right\rfloor_{\mathbb{N}}.

Then the following
uniform upper bound holds true for

w\in\mathbb{R}
:

(6.59)

\big|\nabla_{x}^{2,\delta}\hat{p}_{\delta^{2}n}^{\delta}(\delta k)\big|\vee\left|\frac{1}{\delta^{3}}\nabla_{k}^{2}p_{n}(k)\right|\leqslant\frac{C_{1}}{s^{3/2}}e^{-C_{2}w^{2}}.

In (
6.59
),
\nabla^{2,\delta}
stands for the rescaled gradient given by (
2.75
) and
\nabla_{k}^{2}p_{n}(k)
denotes the second discrete gradient applied to the
heat kernel
p_{t}
in (
2.73
), that is

(6.60)

\nabla_{k}^{2}p_{n}(k)=p_{n}(k+1)+p_{n}(k-1)-2p_{n}(k).

Remark 6.19
.

We are stating and proving Lemma
6.18
with the second order derivatives in order to handle the most challenging context of interest for
us. However, one can prove non gradient estimates in the same way. Let us label the following one, which holds true under the same conditions as for
Lemma
6.18
:

(6.61)

\hat{p}^{\delta}_{\delta^{2}n}(\delta k)\leqslant e^{-C_{3}w^{2}},

where
k
and
n
are given in (
6.58
).

Proof of Lemma
6.18
.

We start with the following elementary observation, valid for
s\geq\delta^{2}
and
w\in\mathbb{R}
:

(6.62)

s\geqslant\delta^{2},w\in\mathbb{R}\implies\begin{cases}C_{1}s\leqslant\lfloor s\rfloor\leqslant C_{2}s,\\
C_{3}e^{-C_{4}w^{2}}\leqslant e^{-C_{5}(\lfloor\sqrt{s}w\rfloor/\sqrt{\lfloor s\rfloor})^{2}}\leqslant C_{6}e^{-C_{7}w^{2}},\end{cases}

where the
C_{i}
’s are universal constants whose exact values are irrelevant. With (
6.62
) in hand, plus recalling that
\lfloor\sqrt{s}w\rfloor=\delta k
and
\lfloor s\rfloor=\delta^{2}n
, we get that the estimate on
\nabla_{x}^{2,\delta}\hat{p}_{\delta^{2}n}^{\delta}(\delta k)
in (
6.59
)
follows directly from Proposition
2.26
. Next we observe
that according to (
2.73
) we have

p_{n}(k)=\frac{1}{(2\pi\sigma^{2}n)^{1/2}}\exp\left(-\frac{k^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}n}\right).

Furthermore, owing to (
6.58
) and setting

(6.63)

u\triangleq\frac{\lfloor\sqrt{s}w\rfloor}{\sqrt{\lfloor s\rfloor}},\quad\text{ and }\quad\eta\triangleq\frac{\delta}{\sqrt{\lfloor s\rfloor}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}},

we have

\frac{k^{2}}{n}=\frac{\lfloor\sqrt{s}w\rfloor^{2}}{\delta^{2}}\times\frac{\delta^{2}}{\lfloor s\rfloor}=u^{2}.

Hence for the quantity
\nabla_{k}^{2}p_{n}(k)
defined by (
6.60
) it is readily checked that

(6.64)

\frac{1}{\delta^{3}}\nabla_{k}^{2}p_{n}(k)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\sigma^{2}}\lfloor s\rfloor^{3/2}}\cdot\frac{1}{\eta^{2}}\left(e^{-\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}(u+\eta)^{2}}+e^{-\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}(u-\eta)^{2}}-2e^{-\frac{u^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}}\right).

We now bound the right hand side of (
6.64
). To this
aim, observe that
\eta\leqslant 1
since we have assumed
s\geqslant\delta^{2}
. Hence using a second order Taylor
approximation of the function
e^{-\frac{u^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}}
it
is easily seen that

(6.65)

\left|\frac{1}{\eta^{2}}\left(e^{-\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}(u+\eta)^{2}}+e^{-\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}(u-\eta)^{2}}-2e^{-\frac{u^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}}\right)\right|\leqslant C_{1}e^{-C_{2}u^{2}}.

Plugging this estimate in (
6.64
) and invoking (
6.62
)
our claim (
6.59
) is easily checked.
∎

The next lemma quantifies the convergence of derivatives of the heat kernel.

Lemma 6.20
.

As in Lemma
6.18
, we consider the Gaussian kernel given by (
2.73
) and the discrete derivative
\nabla_{k}^{2}
in (
6.60
). The notation
k
and
n
in (
6.58
) prevails. Then the following bound holds true for all
s\geq\delta^{2}
and
w\in\mathbb{R}
:

\Big|\frac{1}{\delta^{3}}\nabla_{k}^{2}p_{n}(k)-\frac{1}{s^{3/2}}\partial_{xx}^{2}p_{1}(w)\Big|\lesssim\frac{\delta}{\sqrt{s}}\cdot\frac{e^{-Cw^{2}}}{s^{3/2}}.

Proof.

We start from the expression (
6.64
) for

\nabla_{k}^{2}p_{n}(k)
, and we substract
\partial_{xx}^{2}p_{1}(u)

(recall that
u
is introduced in (
6.63
)). Similarly to (
6.65
), we introduce a third order taylor expansion
(as opposed to the second order expansion alluded to in Lemma
6.18
). We get that

(6.66)

\left|\frac{1}{\delta^{3}}\nabla_{k}^{2}p_{n}(k)-\frac{1}{\lfloor s\rfloor^{3/2}}\partial_{xx}^{2}p_{1}(u)\right|\lesssim\frac{\delta}{\sqrt{s}}\frac{e^{-Cw^{2}}}{s^{3/2}}\,,

where we have used the relations in (
6.62
). Next,
we evaluate the difference

(6.67)

\mathcal{Q}_{s,w}=\frac{1}{s^{3/2}}\partial_{xx}^{2}p_{1}(w)-\frac{1}{\lfloor s\rfloor^{3/2}}\partial_{xx}^{2}p_{1}(u),

where we recall again that
u=\lfloor\sqrt{s}w\rfloor/\sqrt{\lfloor s\rfloor}
. To this aim we define two interpolating paths of the form

s(r)\triangleq(1-r)s+r\lfloor s\rfloor,\quad\text{and}\quad x(r)\triangleq(1-r)\frac{\lfloor\sqrt{s}w\rfloor}{\sqrt{\lfloor s\rfloor}}+rw=(1-r)u+rw.

Differentiating along those paths we have

(6.68)

\mathcal{Q}_{s,w}=-\mathcal{Q}^{1}_{s,w}+\mathcal{Q}^{2}_{s,w},

where the two terms in (
6.68
) are respectively defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}^{1}_{s,w}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{3}{2}\int_{0}^{1}s(r){}^{-5/2}\partial_{xx}^{2}p_{1}(x(r))dr\cdot(s-\lfloor s\rfloor)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}^{2}_{s,w}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}s(r)^{-3/2}\partial_{x}^{3}p_{1}(x(r))dr\cdot\left(w-u\right).

For the term
\mathcal{Q}^{1}_{s,w}
above, we bound
s(r)^{-5/2}
by
s^{-5/2}
, the kernel
\partial_{x}^{3}p_{1}(w)
by
e^{-Cw^{2}}
and
s-\lfloor s\rfloor
by
\delta^{2}
. This yields

\mathcal{Q}^{1}_{s,w}\lesssim\frac{\delta^{2}}{s^{5/2}}e^{-Cw^{2}}.

For the term
\mathcal{Q}^{2}_{s,w}
in (
6.68
), we first note that

\displaystyle\Big|\frac{\lfloor\sqrt{s}w\rfloor}{\sqrt{\lfloor s\rfloor}}-w\Big|

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\frac{|\lfloor\sqrt{s}w\rfloor-\sqrt{s}w|}{\sqrt{\lfloor s\rfloor}}+\frac{|w|\cdot|\sqrt{s}-\sqrt{\lfloor s\rfloor}|}{\sqrt{\lfloor s\rfloor}}

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\frac{\delta}{\sqrt{\lfloor s\rfloor}}+|w|\cdot\left|\left(1+\frac{s-\lfloor s\rfloor}{\lfloor s\rfloor}\right)^{1/2}-1\right|\leqslant\frac{\delta}{\sqrt{\lfloor s\rfloor}}+\frac{|w|}{2}\cdot\frac{\delta^{2}}{\lfloor s\rfloor}.

As a result, bounding
s(r)^{-3/2}
and
\partial_{x}^{3}p_{1}
as for

\mathcal{Q}^{1}_{s,w}
, we get

\mathcal{Q}^{2}_{s,w}\lesssim\left(\frac{\delta}{\sqrt{\lfloor s\rfloor}}+\frac{|w|}{2}\cdot\frac{\delta^{2}}{\lfloor s\rfloor}\right)\frac{e^{-Cw^{2}}}{s^{3/2}}.

Combining the estimates for
\mathcal{Q}^{1}_{s,w}
and
\mathcal{Q}^{2}_{s,w}
and recalling the decomposition (
6.68
), we have thus obtained

(6.69)

\mathcal{Q}_{s,w}\lesssim\frac{\delta}{\sqrt{s}}\frac{e^{-Cw^{2}}}{s^{3/2}}.

Eventually combining (
6.66
), (
6.67
) and (
6.69
), this finishes the proof of our lemma.
∎

We now proceed to prove Lemma
6.17
.

Proof of Lemma
6.17
.

For
t\in[0,T]
and
x\in[-,a,a]
decompose the difference
\mathcal{W}_{t}^{1,\delta}(x)-\mathcal{W}_{t}(x)

as

(6.70)

\mathcal{W}_{t}^{1,\delta}(x)-\mathcal{W}_{t}(x)=\mathcal{K}_{t}^{1}(x)+\mathcal{K}_{t}^{2}(x),

where
\mathcal{K}_{t}^{1}(x)
and
\mathcal{K}_{t}^{2}(x)
are
given by

(6.71)

\mathcal{K}_{t}^{1}(x)\triangleq\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\nabla_{x}^{2,\delta}\hat{p}_{\lfloor s\rfloor}^{\delta}(\lfloor x-y\rfloor)-\partial_{xx}^{2}p_{s}(x-y)\right)\mathcal{I}_{t-s}^{1,\delta}(x,y)dyds,

(6.72)

\mathcal{K}_{t}^{2}(x)\triangleq\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{xx}^{2}p_{s}(x-y)\left(\mathcal{I}_{t-s}^{1,\delta}(x,y)-\mathcal{I}_{t-s}(x,y)\right)dyds,

and where we recall that

\mathcal{I}_{t-s}^{1,\delta}(x,y)\triangleq\int_{x}^{y}\tilde{v}_{t-s}^{\delta}(z)d\hat{{\bf U}}_{1}^{\delta}(z),\qquad\mathcal{I}_{t-s}(x,y)\triangleq\int_{x}^{y}v_{t-s}(z)dW(z).

With this decomposition in hand, we now divide our estimate (
6.57
)
in several steps.

Step 1: estimate for

\mathcal{K}_{t}^{2}(x)
. For the term

\mathcal{K}_{t}^{2}(x)
in (
6.72
) we easily get that,
for
a\geqslant 1
,
t\in(0,T]
and
x\in[-,a,a]
we have

(6.73)

|\mathcal{K}_{t}^{2}(x)|\leqslant CE(a,t)\lambda^{-\frac{\alpha-\beta}{4}}\left(\kappa(\hat{{\bf U}}_{1}^{\delta})d_{\hat{{\bf U}}_{1}^{\delta},{\bf W}}(\tilde{v}^{\delta},v)+\Theta(v)\rho(\hat{{\bf U}}_{1}^{\delta},{\bf W})\right).

Indeed, the patient reader can check that (
6.73
) is an
easy variation of Lemma
4.3
and Lemma
4.7
.

Step2 : decomposition for

\mathcal{K}_{t}^{1}(x)
. In order
to estimate
\mathcal{K}_{t}^{1}(x)
we will decompose this integral
into small and large time domain. Namely, we write

(6.74)

\mathcal{K}_{t}^{1}(x)=\mathcal{K}_{t}^{11}(x)+\mathcal{K}_{t}^{12}(x),

where

(6.75)

\mathcal{K}_{t}^{11}(x)=\int_{0}^{t\wedge\delta^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\nabla_{x}^{2,\delta}\hat{p}_{\lfloor s\rfloor}^{\delta}(\lfloor x-y\rfloor)-\partial_{xx}^{2}p_{s}(x-y)\right)\mathcal{I}_{t-s}^{1,\delta}(x,y)dyds,

and

(6.76)

\mathcal{K}_{t}^{12}(x)=\int_{t\wedge\delta^{2}}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\nabla_{x}^{2,\delta}\hat{p}_{\lfloor s\rfloor}^{\delta}(\lfloor x-y\rfloor)-\partial_{xx}^{2}p_{s}(x-y)\right)\mathcal{I}_{t-s}^{1,\delta}(x,y)dyds.

We now bound
\mathcal{K}^{11}
and
\mathcal{K}^{12}
in two different
ways. More specifically, for the small time regime we shall rely on
the fact that
\hat{p}^{\delta}
and
p
average to small quantities,
while in the large time regime we will invoke the local central limit
theorem for
\hat{p}^{\delta}
.

Step 3: Small time estimates
. For small times, that is when

s\leqslant\delta^{2}
, we will not use the difference
\hat{p}^{\delta}-p
.
Therefore we simply decompose
\mathcal{K}_{t}^{11}(x)
into

(6.77)

\mathcal{K}_{t}^{11}(x)=\mathcal{A}_{t}^{1}(x)+\mathcal{A}_{t}^{2}(x),

with

(6.78)

\mathcal{A}_{t}^{1}(x)=\int_{0}^{t\wedge\delta^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\big|\nabla_{x}^{2,\delta}\hat{p}_{0}^{\delta}(\lfloor y\rfloor)\big|\cdot\big|\mathcal{I}_{t-s}^{1,\delta}(x,x+y)\big|dyds,

(6.79)

\mathcal{A}_{t}^{2}(x)=\int_{0}^{t\wedge\delta^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}s^{-1}\big|\partial_{xx}^{2}p_{1}(w)\big|\cdot\big|\mathcal{I}_{t-s}^{1,\delta}(x,x+\sqrt{s}w)\big|dwds,

where for
\mathcal{A}_{t}^{2}(x)
we have invoked our usual change
of variable
y=\sqrt{s}w
.

We first estimate
\mathcal{A}_{t}^{1}(x)
. This is based
on the same kind of decomposition as in the proof of Lemma
4.3

but requires different estimates since the spatial integral is no
longer of Gaussian type. More specifically, note that
\nabla_{x}^{2,\delta}\hat{p}_{0}(\lfloor y\rfloor)

is supported on
\{y:|y|\leqslant\delta\}
, and replace the Gaussian
bounds on the kernel
\hat{p}^{\delta}
by the simplified version (
2.81
) of
(
2.80
):

(6.80)

\big|\nabla_{x}^{2,\delta}\hat{p}_{0}^{\delta}(\lfloor y\rfloor)\big|\lesssim\frac{1}{\delta^{3}}\,\mathds{1}_{\{|u|\leqslant\delta\}}.

We now bound the term
\mathcal{I}_{t-s}^{1,\delta}(x,x+y)
in the
right hand side of (
6.78
). Namely one gets, similarly to
(
6.42
),

\displaystyle\Big|\mathcal{I}_{t-s}^{1,\delta}(x,x+y)\Big|=\Big|\int_{x}^{x+y}\tilde{v}_{t-s}^{\delta}(z)d\hat{{\bf U}}_{1}^{\delta}(z)\Big|

\displaystyle\leqslant C\kappa(\hat{{\bf U}}_{1}^{\delta})\Theta(\tilde{v}^{\delta})\lambda^{\frac{\alpha-\beta}{4}}E(a,t)e^{-(\lambda+\theta(a+|y|))s}\times\left((a+|y|)^{\chi}|y|^{\alpha}+(a+|y|)^{2\chi}|y|^{2\alpha}\right.

(6.81)

\displaystyle\ \ \ \left.+(a+|y|)^{2\chi+\beta/2}|y|^{2\alpha+\beta}+(a+|y|)^{2\chi+\beta/2}|y|^{\alpha+2\beta}+(a+|y|)^{2\chi}(t-s)^{-\beta/2}|y|^{\alpha+2\beta}\right).

Applying this inequality to (
6.78
), we obtain a relation
of the form

(6.82)

\mathcal{A}_{t}^{1}(x)\leqslant\kappa(\hat{{\bf U}}_{1}^{\delta})\Theta(\tilde{v}^{\delta})\lambda^{\frac{\alpha-\beta}{4}}E(a,t)\sum_{i=1}^{5}\mathcal{A}_{t}^{1i},

and the terms
\mathcal{A}_{t}^{1i}
have to be estimated separately.
For the sake of conciseness we will just focus on
\mathcal{A}_{t}^{11}

and
\mathcal{A}_{t}^{15}
in the sequel.

With (
6.81
) in hand, the expression we have obtained
for
\mathcal{A}_{t}^{11}
is

\mathcal{A}_{t}^{11}=\int_{0}^{t\wedge\delta^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\big|\nabla_{x}^{2,\delta}\hat{p}_{0}^{\delta}(\lfloor y\rfloor)\big|\cdot e^{-(\lambda+\theta(a+|y|))s}(a+|y|)^{\chi}|y|^{\alpha}dyds.

Hence owing to (
6.80
), the
fact that
\hat{p}_{0}^{\delta}
is supported in
[-\delta,\delta]
and the assumption
t\leqslant\delta^{2}
,
we get

(6.83)

\mathcal{A}_{t}^{11}\lesssim\frac{1}{\delta^{3}}\int_{\{y:|y|\leqslant\delta\}}(a+|y|)^{\chi}|y|^{\alpha}dy\int_{0}^{\delta^{2}}e^{-(\lambda+\theta(a+|y|))s}ds.

Next in order to eliminate the factor
(a+|y|)^{\chi}
above, we
apply Hölder’s inequality with
p=\chi^{-1}
and
q=(1-\chi)^{-1}

to the time integral. This yields

\int_{0}^{\delta^{2}}e^{-(\lambda+\theta(a+|y|))s}ds\leqslant\left(\int_{0}^{\delta^{2}}e^{-p(\lambda+\theta(a+|y|))s}ds\right)^{1/p}\cdot\left(\int_{0}^{\delta^{2}}1ds\right)^{1/q}\lesssim\frac{\delta^{2(1-\chi)}}{(\lambda+\theta(a+|y|))^{\chi}}.

It follows that

(6.84)

\mathcal{A}_{t}^{11}\lesssim\frac{1}{\delta^{3}}\int_{\{y:|y|\leqslant\delta\}}\frac{(a+|y|)^{\chi}|y|^{\alpha}\delta^{2(1-\chi)}}{(\lambda+\theta(a+|y|))^{\chi}}dy\lesssim\frac{1}{\delta^{3}}\cdot\delta^{2(1-\chi)}\cdot\delta^{\alpha+1}=\delta^{\alpha-2\chi}.

Referring to our decomposition (
6.82
), let us now analyze
the term
\mathcal{A}_{t}^{15}
. According to (
6.81
)
we have

\mathcal{A}_{t}^{15}=\int_{0}^{t\wedge\delta^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\big|\nabla_{x}^{2,\delta}\hat{p}_{0}^{\delta}(\lfloor y\rfloor)\big|e^{-(\lambda+\theta(a+|y|))s}(a+|y|)^{2\chi}(t-s)^{-\beta/2}|y|^{\alpha+2\beta}dyds.

We can thus resort to inequality (
4.26
) and (
6.80
)
in order to write

\mathcal{A}_{t}^{15}\lesssim\frac{1}{\delta^{3}}\int_{0}^{t\wedge\delta^{2}}\int_{\{y:|y|\leqslant\delta\}}c^{c}(\lambda+\theta(a+|y|))^{-c}s^{-c}\cdot(a+|y|)^{2\chi}(t-s)^{-\beta/2}|y|^{\alpha+2\beta}dyds.

By choosing
c\triangleq 2\chi
above, we obtain

\mathcal{A}_{t}^{15}\lesssim\frac{1}{\delta^{3}}\int_{\{y:|y|\leqslant\delta\}}|y|^{\alpha+2\beta}dy\int_{0}^{t\wedge\delta^{2}}s^{-2\chi}(t-s)^{-\beta/2}ds.

Therefore the elementary change of variables
s=(t\wedge\delta^{2})v

yields

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{t}^{15}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{\delta^{3}}\int_{\{y:|y|\leqslant\delta\}}|y|^{\alpha+2\beta}dy\cdot(t\wedge\delta^{2})^{1-2\chi-\beta/2}\cdot\int_{0}^{1}v^{-2\chi}(1-v)^{-\beta/2}dv

(6.85)

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{\delta^{3}}\cdot\delta^{\alpha+2\beta+1}\cdot\delta^{2(1-2\chi-\beta/2)}=\delta^{\alpha+\beta-4\chi}.

The estimates for the space-time integral of the other summands in
(
6.81
) is similar to the one for
\mathcal{A}_{t}^{11}
.
We let the patient reader check that we obtain

(6.86)

\mathcal{A}_{t}^{12}\lesssim\delta^{2(\alpha-2\chi)},\qquad\mathcal{A}_{t}^{13}\lesssim\delta^{2(\alpha-2\chi)},\qquad\mathcal{A}_{t}^{14}\lesssim\delta^{\alpha+\beta-4\chi},

respectively. Summarizing our considerations on the terms
\mathcal{A}_{t}^{1i}
,
we have obtained relations (
6.84
), (
6.85
),
(
6.86
). Comparing the exponents of
\delta

in those inequalities and recalling that (
3.20
) imposes
\chi<\beta/2
,
it is clear that the dominant term is given by
\delta^{\alpha-2\chi}
.
Plugging this information back into (
6.82
), we end up
with

(6.87)

\mathcal{A}_{t}^{1}(x)\lesssim\kappa(\hat{{\bf U}}_{1}^{\delta})\Theta(\tilde{v}^{\delta})\lambda^{\frac{\alpha-\beta}{4}}E(a,t)\cdot\delta^{\alpha-2\chi}.

We now turn to the term
\mathcal{A}_{t}^{2}
defined by (
6.79
).
This quantity is given by an integral involving the continuous kernel

p_{1}
, and we will thus argue similarly to Lemma
4.3
.
Also recall that our main goal here is to extract a factor
\delta^{r}

with a strictly positive
r
. With this objective in mind, recall
that (as in the proof of Lemma
4.3
) the quantity
P_{u}(w)

designates an arbitrary polynomial in
w
and that
T
is our time
horizon. Similarly to (
4.10
) we thus get

(6.88)

\mathcal{A}_{t}^{2}(x)\lesssim\kappa(\hat{{\bf U}}_{1}^{\delta})\Theta(\tilde{v}^{\delta})\lambda^{\frac{\alpha-\beta}{4}}E(a,t)\\
\times\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{\theta(1+T)\sqrt{T}|w|-Cw^{2}}P_{u}(w)dw\int_{0}^{t\wedge\delta^{2}}e^{-(\lambda+\theta\rho)s}\varphi_{\rho}(s)ds\,,

where
\rho\triangleq a+\sqrt{T}|w|
and the function
\varphi_{\rho}

is given by

(6.89)

\varphi_{\rho}(s):=\rho^{\chi}s^{\alpha/2-1}+\rho^{2\chi}s^{\alpha-1}+\rho^{2\chi+\beta/2}s^{\alpha+\beta/2-1}\\
+\rho^{2\chi+\beta/2}s^{\alpha/2+\beta-1}+\rho^{2\chi}(t-s)^{-\beta/2}s^{\alpha/2+\beta-1}=:\sum_{i=1}^{5}\varphi_{\rho}^{i}(s).

Plugging (
6.89
) into (
6.88
),
let us call
\mathcal{A}_{t}^{2i}
the term corresponding to the
time integral
\int_{0}^{t\wedge\delta^{2}}e^{-(\lambda+\theta\rho)s}\varphi_{\rho}^{i}(s)ds
.
All those quantities are treated very similarly and we only show how
to handle
\mathcal{A}_{t}^{21}
below. Namely using the expression
for
\varphi_{\rho}^{1}
in (
6.89
), one can write

\mathcal{A}_{t}^{21}
as

\mathcal{A}_{t}^{21}\triangleq\rho^{\chi}\int_{0}^{t\wedge\delta^{2}}e^{-(\lambda+\theta\rho)s}s^{\alpha/2-1}ds.

We now apply Hölder’s inequality with two conjugate exponents
p,q

in order to get

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{t}^{21}

\displaystyle\leqslant\rho^{\chi}\left(\int_{0}^{t\wedge\delta^{2}}e^{-p(\lambda+\theta\rho)s}ds\right)^{1/p}\cdot\left(\int_{0}^{t\wedge\delta^{2}}s^{(\alpha/2-1)q}ds\right)^{1/q}

\displaystyle\leqslant\rho^{\chi}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-p(\lambda+\theta\rho)s}ds\right)^{1/p}\cdot\left(\int_{0}^{\delta^{2}}s^{(\alpha/2-1)q}ds\right)^{1/q}.

We can easily integrate the two terms in the right hand side above.
We then choose
p\triangleq\chi^{-1},q\triangleq(1-\chi)^{-1}
in
order to get

(6.90)

\mathcal{A}_{t}^{21}\lesssim\frac{\rho^{\chi}}{(\lambda+\theta\rho)^{\chi}}\,\delta^{\alpha-2+2/q}\lesssim\delta^{\alpha-2\chi}.

In a similar way, the other summands are estimated as

(6.91)

\mathcal{A}_{t}^{22}\vee\mathcal{A}_{t}^{23}\lesssim\delta^{2(\alpha-2\chi)},\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathcal{A}_{t}^{24}\vee\mathcal{A}_{t}^{25}\lesssim\delta^{\alpha+\beta-4\chi}.

Therefore gathering (
6.91
) and (
6.90
)
into (
6.88
) we arrive at

(6.92)

\mathcal{A}_{t}^{2}(x)\lesssim\kappa(\hat{{\bf U}}_{1}^{\delta})\Theta(\tilde{v}^{\delta})\lambda^{\frac{\alpha-\beta}{4}}E(a,t)\cdot\delta^{\alpha-2\chi}.

We can conclude our small time estimate by plugging (
6.92
)
and (
6.87
) into (
6.77
).
This yields the following uper bound, valid for
t\leqslant\delta^{2}
:

(6.93)

\mathcal{K}_{t}^{11}(x)\lesssim\kappa(\hat{{\bf U}}_{1}^{\delta})\Theta(\tilde{v}^{\delta})\lambda^{\frac{\alpha-\beta}{4}}E(a,t)\cdot\delta^{\alpha-2\chi}.

Step 4: Large time estimates.
This step is dedicated to handle
the integral over
s\geqslant\delta^{2}
defining
\mathcal{K}_{t}^{12}(x)

in (
6.76
). More specifically, resorting to our usual change
of variable
y=x+\sqrt{s}w
, one can recast
\mathcal{K}_{t}^{12}(x)

as

\mathcal{K}_{t}^{12}(x)=\int_{t\wedge\delta^{2}}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\nabla_{x}^{2,\delta}\hat{p}_{\lfloor s\rfloor}^{\delta}(\lfloor\sqrt{s}w\rfloor)-s^{-3/2}\partial_{xx}^{2}p_{1}(w)\right)\mathcal{I}_{t-s}^{1,\delta}(x,x+\sqrt{s}w)\cdot\sqrt{s}\,dwds.

We now insert the quantity
\nabla_{x}^{2}p_{n}(x)
defined by (
6.60
) in the right
hand side above (with
k,n
defined in
(
6.58
)) to get a decomposition of the form

(6.94)

\Big|\mathcal{K}_{t}^{12}(x)\Big|\leqslant\mathcal{B}_{t}^{1}(x)+\mathcal{B}_{t}^{2}(x),

with
\mathcal{B}_{t}^{1}(x)
and
\mathcal{B}_{t}^{2}(x)
respectively
defined by

(6.95)

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{t}^{1}(x)

\displaystyle\triangleq\int_{t\wedge\delta^{2}}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\big|\nabla_{x}^{2,\delta}\hat{p}_{\lfloor s\rfloor}^{\delta}(\lfloor\sqrt{s}w\rfloor)-\frac{1}{\delta^{3}}\nabla_{k}^{2}p_{n}(k)\big|\cdot\big|\mathcal{I}_{t-s}^{1,\delta}(x,x+\sqrt{s}w)\big|\cdot\sqrt{s}\,dwds,

(6.96)

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{t}^{2}(x)

\displaystyle\triangleq\int_{t\wedge\delta^{2}}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\big|\frac{1}{\delta^{3}}\nabla_{k}^{2}p_{n}(k)-s^{-3/2}\partial_{xx}^{2}p_{1}(w)\big|\cdot\big|\mathcal{I}_{t-s}^{1,\delta}(x,x+\sqrt{s}w)\big|\cdot\sqrt{s}\,dwds.

Let us proceed to bound the term
\mathcal{B}_{t}^{1}(x)
in (
6.95
). To this aim, we start by recalling the definition
(
2.72
) of
\hat{p}_{t}^{\delta}
and (
2.75
) for

\nabla_{x}^{2,\delta}
. This gives

(6.97)

\nabla_{x}^{2,\delta}\hat{p}_{\lfloor s\rfloor}^{\delta}(x)=\frac{1}{\delta^{3}}\nabla_{x}^{2}p_{\lfloor s\rfloor/\delta^{2}}^{d}\!\left(\frac{x}{\delta}\right),

where
\nabla_{x}^{2}
in the right hand side above stands for the discrete derivative. In addition, Theorem
2.25
states
that for
s\geqslant\delta^{2}

\Big|\nabla_{x}^{2,\delta}p_{\lfloor s\rfloor/\delta^{2}}^{d}\left(\frac{x}{\delta}\right)-\nabla_{x}^{2}p_{\lfloor s\rfloor/\delta^{2}}\left(\frac{x}{\delta}\right)\Big|\leqslant\frac{C_{1}}{(\lfloor s\rfloor/\delta^{2})^{5/2}}\lesssim\frac{\delta^{5}}{\lfloor s\rfloor^{5/2}}.

Plugging this information into (
6.97
) we get

\Big|\nabla_{x}^{2,\delta}\hat{p}_{\lfloor s\rfloor}^{\delta}(x)-\frac{1}{\delta^{3}}\nabla_{x}^{2}p_{\lfloor s\rfloor/\delta^{2}}\left(\frac{x}{\delta}\right)\Big|\lesssim\frac{\delta^{2}}{\lfloor s\rfloor^{5/2}}.

In particular, for
x=\lfloor\sqrt{s}w\rfloor
and
k,n
defined in
(
6.58
) we end up with

(6.98)

\Big|\nabla_{x}^{2,\delta}\hat{p}_{\lfloor s\rfloor}^{\delta}(\lfloor\sqrt{s}w\rfloor)-\frac{1}{\delta^{3}}\nabla_{k}^{2}p_{n}(k)\Big|\lesssim\frac{\delta^{2}}{\lfloor s\rfloor^{5/2}}.

Next we improve our upper bound (
6.98
) by introducing an extra interpolating parameter
b\in[0,1]
. Whenever
s\geqslant\delta^{2}
, combining (
6.98
) and the uniform bounds in Lemma
6.18
, we get the following inequality for all
w\in\mathbb{R}
:

\displaystyle\Big|\nabla_{x}^{2,\delta}\hat{p}_{\lfloor s\rfloor}^{\delta}(\lfloor\sqrt{s}w\rfloor)-\frac{1}{\delta^{3}}\nabla_{k}^{2}p_{n}(k)\Big|

\displaystyle\leqslant\Big|\nabla_{x}^{2,\delta}\hat{p}_{\lfloor s\rfloor}^{\delta}(\lfloor\sqrt{s}w\rfloor)-\frac{1}{\delta^{3}}\nabla_{k}^{2}p_{n}(k)\Big|^{1-b}\cdot\left(\Big|\nabla_{x}^{2,\delta}\hat{p}_{\lfloor s\rfloor}^{\delta}(\lfloor\sqrt{s}w\rfloor)\Big|^{b}+\Big|\frac{1}{\delta^{3}}\nabla_{k}^{2}p_{n}(k)\Big|^{b}\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\frac{\delta^{2}}{\lfloor s\rfloor^{5/2}}\right)^{1-b}\cdot\left(\frac{1}{s^{3/2}}e^{-C_{1}w^{2}}\right)^{b}\lesssim\frac{\delta^{2(1-b)}}{s^{5/2-b}}e^{-C_{2}w^{2}}.

Reporting this inequality in (
6.95
), it follows that

(6.99)

\mathcal{B}_{t}^{1}(x)\lesssim\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\delta^{2(1-b)}}{s^{2-b}}e^{-Cw^{2}}\cdot\big|\mathcal{I}_{t-s}^{1,\delta}(x,x+\sqrt{s}w)\big|dwds.

It remains to handle the term
\mathcal{I}_{t-s}^{1,\delta}(x,x+\sqrt{s}w)
in the right hand side of (
6.99
). Now recall that
\mathcal{I}_{t-s}^{1,\delta}
is the stochastic integral defined by (
6.16
). As in the proof of Lemma
4.3
(see also the estimates after (
6.43
)), we shall upper bound bound this quantity by five terms:

(6.100)

\big|\mathcal{I}_{t-s}^{1,\delta}(x,x+\sqrt{s}w)\big|\lesssim\kappa(\hat{{\bf U}}_{1}^{\delta})\Theta(\tilde{v}^{\delta})\lambda^{\frac{\alpha-\beta}{4}}E(a,t)\cdot P_{u}(w)(\mathcal{B}_{s}^{11}+\cdots+\mathcal{B}_{s}^{15}),

where
P_{u}(w)
is some polynomial in
|w|
. For simplicity, we
only discuss the first term

\mathcal{B}_{s}^{11}=e^{-(\lambda+\theta\rho)s}s^{\alpha/2}\rho^{\chi},\quad\text{where}\quad\rho\triangleq a+\sqrt{T}|w|.

Reporting this definition in (
6.100
) and then (
6.99
), we get that the corresponding term in
\mathcal{B}_{t}^{1}(x)
is

\overline{\mathcal{B}}_{t}^{11}\equiv\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\delta^{2(1-b)}}{s^{2-b}}P_{u}(w)e^{-Cw^{2}}\mathcal{B}_{s}^{11}\,dwds.

To extract a factor of
\delta^{r}
from this term, we use the fact that

s\geqslant\delta^{2}
to write

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{B}}_{t}^{11}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}P_{u}(w)e^{-Cw^{2}}\rho^{\chi}dw\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\delta^{2(1-b)-2\gamma_{1}+2\gamma_{1}}}{s^{1-b-\gamma_{1}}}e^{-(\lambda+\theta\rho)s}\cdot s^{\alpha/2-1-\gamma_{1}}ds

\displaystyle\leqslant\delta^{2\gamma_{1}}\cdot\int_{\mathbb{R}}P_{u}(w)e^{-Cw^{2}}\rho^{\chi}dw\int_{0}^{t}e^{-(\lambda+\theta\rho)s}\cdot s^{\alpha/2-1-\gamma_{1}}ds,

where
\gamma_{1}>0
is some constant to be specified.
According to Hölder’s inequality with
p\triangleq 1/\chi
(
1/q=1-\chi
),
we obtain that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}e^{-(\lambda+\theta\rho)s}\cdot s^{\alpha/2-1-\gamma_{1}}ds

\displaystyle\leqslant\left(\int_{0}^{t}e^{-p(\lambda+\theta\rho)s}ds\right)^{1/p}\cdot\left(\int_{0}^{t}s^{q(\alpha/2-1-\gamma_{1})}ds\right)^{1/q}

\displaystyle\lesssim(\lambda+\theta\rho)^{-\chi}\cdot T^{\alpha/2-\chi-\gamma_{1}}.

Note that
\gamma_{1}
needs to be less than
\alpha/2-\chi
so
that the second time integral in the above inequality is finite. As
a consequence, the
\overline{\mathcal{B}}^{11}_{t}
-term produces an upper estimate
of the form

\overline{\mathcal{B}}^{11}_{t}\leqslant C_{\alpha,\beta,T,\gamma_{1}}\kappa(\hat{{\bf U}}_{1}^{\delta})\Theta(\tilde{v}^{\delta})\lambda^{\frac{\alpha-\beta}{4}}E(a,t)\cdot\delta^{2\gamma_{1}}.

The other terms
\mathcal{B}^{12},\ldots,\mathcal{B}^{15}
are discussed
in a similar way and the resulting factors of
\delta^{r^{\prime}}
are all
of higher order (i.e.
r^{\prime}>2\gamma_{1}
). Therefore, we arrive at

\mathcal{B}_{t}^{1}(x)\leqslant C_{\alpha,\beta,T,r}\kappa(\hat{{\bf U}}_{1}^{\delta})\Theta(\tilde{v}^{\delta})\lambda^{\frac{\alpha-\beta}{4}}E(a,t)\cdot\delta^{r}

where
r
is any given constant that is less than
\alpha-2\chi
.

In order to complete the large time estimates, according to our decomposition (
6.94
), it remains to upper bound the term
\mathcal{B}_{t}^{2}(x)
given by (
6.96
).
This will be an easy consequence of Lemma
6.20
.
Namely a direct application of this lemma yields

\mathcal{B}_{t}^{2}(x)\leqslant\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\delta}{\sqrt{s}}\cdot\frac{1}{s}e^{-Cw^{2}}\big|\mathcal{I}_{t-s}^{1,\delta}(x,x+\sqrt{s}w)\big|dwds.

Hence by the same kind of analysis as in the
\mathcal{B}^{1}
-case, we
arrive at

\mathcal{B}_{t}^{2}(x)\leqslant C_{\alpha,\beta,T,r}\kappa(\hat{{\bf U}}_{1}^{\delta})\Theta(\tilde{v}^{\delta})\lambda^{\frac{\alpha-\beta}{4}}E(a,t)\cdot\delta^{r}

where
r
is any constant that is less than
\alpha-2\chi
. This
is exactly the same estimate as the one for
\mathcal{B}^{1}
.

By putting (
6.73
) and the estimates of
\mathcal{A}^{1},\mathcal{A}^{2},\mathcal{B}^{1},\mathcal{B}^{2}

together, we have thus completed the proof of Lemma
6.17
.
∎

6.4.2.
The time variation estimate.

In order to state the main result of this section, let us introduce some more notation.

Notation 6.21
.

Let
\theta>2
,
a\geqslant 1
and
\beta
be coefficients satisfying the assumptions of Definition
3.8
and Definition
3.9
. We consider some additional parameters
\theta^{\prime}<\theta
and
\beta^{\prime}>\beta
still satisfying the same assumptions. The norm
\Theta
in Definition
3.9
with parameters
\theta^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}
will be denoted
\Theta^{\theta^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}

while the usual norm is written as
\Theta
. In the sequel for a function
h
defined on
[0,T]
, the time increments of
h
will be denoted by

h_{t_{1},t_{2}}=h_{t_{2}}-h_{t_{1}},\quad\text{for}\quad 0\leqslant t_{1}<t_{2}\leqslant T.

As a slight elaboration of Proposition
6.14
, we state a lemma about the controlled norms of
\mathcal{W}
and
\mathcal{W}^{1,\delta}
. Its proof is similar to that of Proposition
6.14
and omitted for the sake of conciseness.

Lemma 6.22
.

For
\delta>0
, let
\mathcal{W},\mathcal{W}^{1,\delta}
be the processes defined by
(
6.14
)-(
6.15
). Let
(\theta^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})
be given fixed parameters satisfying the constraints specified in Notation
6.21
. Let
\bar{\kappa}(\omega)
be defined by (
6.19
). Then there exist positive constants
C_{1},C_{2}
depending only on the underlying exponents, such that

(6.101)

\Lambda^{\theta^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}\equiv\sup_{\delta\geqslant 0}\Theta^{\theta^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}(\mathcal{W}^{1,\delta})\vee\Theta^{\theta^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}(\mathcal{W})\leqslant C_{1}\bar{\kappa}(\omega)e^{C_{1}\lambda}\left(\|f_{0}\|_{\mathcal{C}_{L}^{3}}+\|g\|_{\mathcal{C}_{L}^{3}}\right)

provided that
\lambda=\lambda_{\omega}
is chosen to satisfy

C_{2}\lambda^{-\frac{\alpha-\beta^{\prime}}{4}}\bar{\kappa}(\omega)=\frac{1}{4}.

We now turn to the announced estimate for the time variations of
\mathcal{W}^{1,\delta}-\mathcal{W}
.

Lemma 6.23
.

Let the notation of Lemma
6.22
, as well as Notation
6.21
, prevail. In particular the processes

\mathcal{W},\mathcal{W}^{1,\delta}
are introduced in (
6.14
)-(
6.15
) and the exponents
\alpha,\beta
satisfy relation
(
3.20
). Then for any
a\geqslant 1,

x\in[-a,a]
and

0\leqslant t_{1}<t_{2}\leqslant T
, we have

\displaystyle\big|\mathcal{W}_{t_{1},t_{2}}^{1,\delta}(x)-\mathcal{W}_{t_{1},t_{2}}(x)\big|

\displaystyle\leqslant CE(a,t_{2})a^{\beta/2}|t_{2}-t_{1}|^{\beta/2}\left(\lambda^{-\frac{\alpha-\beta}{4}}\left(\kappa(\hat{{\bf U}}_{1}^{\delta})d_{\hat{{\bf U}}_{1}^{\delta},{\bf W}}(\tilde{v}^{\delta},v)\right.\right.

(6.102)

\displaystyle\ \ \ \left.\left.+\Theta(v)\rho(\hat{{\bf U}}_{1}^{\delta},{\bf W})\right)+\lambda^{\frac{\alpha-\beta}{4}}\kappa(\hat{{\bf U}}_{1}^{\delta})\Theta(\tilde{v}^{\delta})\delta^{\alpha-\beta}+\Lambda^{\theta^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}\delta^{\beta^{\prime}-\beta}\right),

where we recall that
\mathcal{W}_{t_{1},t_{2}}^{1,\delta}(x)\triangleq\mathcal{W}_{t_{2}}^{\delta}(x)-\mathcal{W}_{t_{1}}^{\delta}(x)

and similarly for
\mathcal{W}_{t_{1},t_{2}}(x)
. We also recall that in (
6.102
) the quantity
E
is given by (
3.26
),

\rho
is defined by (
5.9
) and
d_{\hat{{\bf U}}_{1}^{\delta},{\bf W}}
is introduced in (
6.11
).

Proof of Lemma
6.23
.

We divide this proof again into several steps. Some technical considerations are similar to previous results and will only be sketched.

Step 1: Decomposition of the time increments.

We begin by decomposing the time increment into

(6.103)

\mathcal{W}_{t_{1},t_{2}}^{1,\delta}(x)-\mathcal{W}_{t_{1},t_{2}}(x)=\mathcal{T}^{1}_{t_{1},t_{2}}(x)+\mathcal{T}^{2}_{t_{1},t_{2}}(x),

where the increments
\mathcal{T}^{1},\mathcal{T}^{2}
are defined by

(6.104)

\mathcal{T}^{1}_{t_{1},t_{2}}(x)\triangleq\int_{0}^{t_{1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(Q_{s}^{\delta}(t_{2},x,y)-Q_{s}^{\delta}(t_{1},x,y)\right)dyds,\quad\mathcal{T}^{2}_{t_{1},t_{2}}(x)\triangleq\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}Q_{s}^{\delta}(t_{2},x,y)dyds,

and where we have used the notation

Q_{s}^{\delta}(t,x,y)\triangleq\nabla_{x}^{2,\delta}\hat{p}_{\lfloor t-s\rfloor}^{\delta}(\lfloor x-y\rfloor)\cdot\mathcal{I}_{s}^{\delta}(x,y)-\partial_{xx}^{2}p_{t-s}(x-y)\cdot\mathcal{I}_{s}(x,y),

with
\mathcal{I}_{s}^{\delta}
,
\mathcal{I}_{s}
given by (
6.16
). Also recall that in the sequel we keep on using our Notation
6.3
for the
\delta
-integer parts
\lfloor x\rfloor
and
\lfloor t\rfloor
.
The remainder of the proof is dedicated to upper bound terms
\mathcal{T}^{1},\mathcal{T}^{2}
in (
6.104
).

Step 2: Bounding
\mathcal{T}^{2}
.

In order to bound
\mathcal{T}^{2}
in (
6.104
), we further
decompose this term as

(6.105)

\mathcal{T}^{2}_{t_{1},t_{2}}(x)=\mathcal{T}^{21}_{t_{1},t_{2}}(x)+\mathcal{T}^{22}_{t_{1},t_{2}}(x),

where
\mathcal{T}^{21},\mathcal{T}^{22}
are respectively defined by

(6.106)

\mathcal{T}^{21}_{t_{1},t_{2}}(x)\triangleq\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\nabla_{x}^{2,\delta}\hat{p}_{\lfloor t_{2}-s\rfloor}^{\delta}(\lfloor x-y\rfloor)-\partial_{xx}^{2}p_{t_{2}-s}(x-y)\right)\cdot\mathcal{I}_{s}^{\delta}(x,y)dyds,

(6.107)

\mathcal{T}^{22}_{t_{1},t_{2}}\triangleq\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{xx}^{2}p_{t_{2}-s}(x-y)\cdot\left(\mathcal{I}_{s}^{\delta}(x,y)-\mathcal{I}_{s}(x,y)\right)dyds.

The estimate of
\mathcal{T}^{22}_{t_{1},t_{2}}(x)
above follows the same lines as in
the proof of Lemma
4.8
(see (
4.50
)). For sake of conciseness, we just state the result here:

(6.108)

|\mathcal{T}^{22}_{t_{1},t_{2}}(x)|\leqslant C\lambda^{-\frac{\alpha-\beta}{4}}E(a,t_{2})a^{\beta/2}|t_{2}-t_{1}|^{\beta/2}\cdot\left(\kappa(\hat{{\bf U}}_{1}^{\delta})d_{\hat{{\bf U}}_{1}^{\delta},{\bf W}}(\tilde{v}^{\delta},v)+\Theta(v)\rho(\hat{{\bf U}}_{1}^{\delta},{\bf W})\right).

To estimate
\mathcal{T}^{21}_{t_{1},t_{2}}(x)
, we set
\tau\triangleq t_{2}-t_{1}

and resort to the decomposition

(6.109)

\mathcal{T}^{21}_{t_{1},t_{2}}(x)=\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{\tau}^{-}(x)+\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{\tau}^{+}(x),

where

(6.110)

\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{t_{1},t_{2}}^{-}(x)=\int_{0}^{\tau\wedge\delta^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\nabla^{2,\delta}\hat{p}_{\lfloor r\rfloor}^{\delta}(\lfloor y\rfloor)-\partial_{xx}^{2}p_{r}(y)\right)\cdot\mathcal{I}_{t_{2}-s}^{\delta}(x,x+y)dyds

(6.111)

\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{t_{1},t_{2}}^{+}(x)=\int_{\tau\wedge\delta^{2}}^{\tau}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\nabla^{2,\delta}\hat{p}_{\lfloor r\rfloor}^{\delta}(\lfloor y\rfloor)-\partial_{xx}^{2}p_{r}(y)\right)\cdot\mathcal{I}_{t_{2}-s}^{\delta}(x,x+y)dyds.

Now the arguments in order to estimate (
6.110
) and (
6.111
) are essentially similar to what we did in Lemma

6.17
for the terms
\mathcal{K}^{11}
and
\mathcal{K}^{12}
(see (
6.75
)-(
6.76
)). In order to abbreviate our computations, we will only detail the bound for the small
time integral
\hat{\mathcal{T}}^{-}
. As in Lemma
6.17
, the large time integral
\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{t_{1},t_{2}}^{+}(x)
is treated
by using the local CLT and the Taylor approximation of the Gaussian
kernel.

In order to handle the small time increment
\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{\tau}^{-}(x)
, we proceed exactly as in (
6.77
).
That is, recalling that
\tau=t_{2}-t_{1}
, we write

(6.112)

\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{\tau}^{-}(x)\leqslant\mathcal{A}^{1}_{\tau}(x)+\mathcal{A}^{2}_{\tau}(x),

where
\mathcal{A}^{1}
and
\mathcal{A}^{2}
are respectively given by

(6.113)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{1}_{\tau}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\tau\wedge\delta^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left|\nabla^{2,\delta}\hat{p}_{\lfloor r\rfloor}^{\delta}(\lfloor y\rfloor)\right|\cdot\left|\mathcal{I}_{t_{2}-s}^{\delta}(x,x+y)\right|\,dyds

(6.114)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{2}_{\tau}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\tau\wedge\delta^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left|\partial_{xx}^{2}p_{r}(y)\right|\cdot\left|\mathcal{I}_{t_{2}-s}^{\delta}(x,x+y)\right|\,dyds.

Here again, for the sake of conciseness we shall only upper bound the term
\mathcal{A}^{1}_{\tau}(x)
above. Namely recall that

\nabla^{2,\delta}\hat{p}_{\lfloor 0\rfloor}^{\delta}
is supported in

\{y\in\mathbb{R};|y|\leqslant\delta\}
. Moreover we have established (
6.80
) above (which is a consequence of (
2.81
)), that is

(6.115)

\big|\nabla_{x}^{2,\delta}\hat{p}^{\delta}_{0}(\lfloor y\rfloor)\big|\lesssim\frac{1}{\delta^{3}},\qquad\ \ \ \text{for }|y|\leqslant\delta.

In addition, we know from (
6.81
) that the rough
integral
\mathcal{I}_{t_{2}-s}^{\delta}(x,x+y)
is bounded above
by five basic terms. The first term appearing on the right hand side
of (
6.81
) together with (
6.115
)
yield the following integral in
\mathcal{A}^{1}_{\tau}(x)
:

\mathcal{A}^{11}_{\tau}(x)\triangleq\frac{1}{\delta^{3}}\int_{0}^{\tau\wedge\delta^{2}}\int_{\{y:|y|\leqslant\delta\}}(a+|y|)^{\chi}|y|^{\alpha}e^{-(\lambda+\theta(a+|y|))s}dyds.

Owing to condition (
3.20
), we have
\chi<\beta/2
. Therefore, for all
a\geqslant 1
we get

|\mathcal{A}^{11}_{\tau}(x)|\lesssim\frac{a^{\beta/2}}{\delta^{3}}\int_{0}^{\tau\wedge\delta^{2}}\int_{\{y:|y|\leqslant\delta\}}|y|^{\alpha}dyds\\
\lesssim\frac{a^{\beta/2}}{\delta^{3}}\cdot\delta^{\alpha+1}\cdot(\tau\wedge\delta^{2})^{\beta/2+1-\beta/2}\leqslant\frac{a^{\beta/2}}{\delta^{3}}\cdot\delta^{\alpha+1}\cdot\tau^{\beta/2}\cdot\delta^{2-\beta}=a^{\beta/2}\tau^{\beta/2}\delta^{\alpha-\beta}.

In the inequality above, notice that we have extracted a power
\tau^{\beta/2}=(t_{2}-t_{1})^{\beta/2}
, which is our expected time regularity for
v
.
This explains the appearance of the factor
\delta^{\alpha-\beta}

in Lemma
6.23
. The other four terms on the right
hand side of (
6.81
) lead to four corresponding
integrals in
\mathcal{A}^{1}_{\tau}(x)
, all of which having order
\delta^{r}

with some
r>\alpha-\beta
. As a result, we obtain that

(6.116)

|\mathcal{A}^{1}_{\tau}(x)|\lesssim\kappa(\hat{{\bf U}}_{1}^{\delta})\Theta(\tilde{v}^{\delta})\lambda^{\frac{\alpha-\beta}{4}}E(a,t_{2})a^{\beta/2}|t_{2}-t_{1}|^{\beta/2}\cdot\delta^{\alpha-\beta}.

A similar argument leads to exactly the same upper bound for
\mathcal{A}^{2}_{\tau}(x)
. Hence reporting (
6.116
)
into (
6.112
), then back into (
6.109
) and (
6.105
), we have proved that
\mathcal{T}^{2}_{t_{1},t_{2}}(x)
satisfies an inequality of the form (
6.102
). This completes the estimate for
\mathcal{T}_{2}
.

Step 3: Bounding
\mathcal{T}^{1}
.
We now consider the term
\mathcal{T}^{1}
defined in (
6.103
). The analysis is in fact similar to what we performed in (
4.44
), and we only point out the main ingredients. In the first place we write

(6.117)

\mathcal{T}^{1}_{t_{1},t_{2}}(x)=\mathcal{T}^{11}_{t_{1},t_{2}}(x)+\mathcal{T}^{12}_{t_{1},t_{2}}(x),

where we define

(6.118)

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{11}_{t_{1},t_{2}}(x)

\displaystyle\triangleq\int_{0}^{t_{1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(J_{t_{1},t_{2}}^{\delta}(s,x-y)-J_{t_{1},t_{2}}(s,x-y)\right)\mathcal{I}_{s}^{\delta}(x,y)dyds,

(6.119)

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{12}_{t_{1},t_{2}}(x)

\displaystyle\triangleq\int_{0}^{t_{1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}J_{t_{1},t_{2}}(s,x-y)\left(\mathcal{I}_{s}^{\delta}(x,y)-\mathcal{I}_{s}(x,y)\right)dyds\,,

and where to ease notation we have set:

(6.120)

\displaystyle J_{t_{1},t_{2}}^{\delta}(s,u)

\displaystyle\triangleq\nabla_{x}^{2,\delta}\hat{p}_{\lfloor t_{2}-s\rfloor}^{\delta}(\lfloor u\rfloor)-\nabla_{x}^{2,\delta}\hat{p}_{\lfloor t_{1}-s\rfloor}^{\delta}(\lfloor u\rfloor),

(6.121)

\displaystyle J_{t_{1},t_{2}}(s,u)

\displaystyle\triangleq\partial_{xx}^{2}p_{t_{2}-s}(u)-\partial_{xx}^{2}p_{t_{1}-s}(u).

We now handle the terms
\mathcal{T}^{11},\mathcal{T}^{12}
above.
The estimate of
\mathcal{T}^{12}_{t_{1},t_{2}}(x)
follows the
same lines as in the proof of Lemma
4.8
. We end up with

(6.122)

|\mathcal{T}^{12}_{t_{1},t_{2}}(x)|\leqslant C\lambda^{-\frac{\alpha-\beta}{4}}E(a,t_{2})a^{\beta/2}|t_{2}-t_{1}|^{\beta/2}\cdot\left(\kappa(\hat{{\bf U}}_{1}^{\delta})d_{\hat{{\bf U}}_{1}^{\delta},{\bf W}}(\tilde{v}^{\delta},v)+\Theta(v)\rho(\hat{{\bf U}}_{1}^{\delta},{\bf W})\right).

For the integral
\mathcal{T}^{11}_{t_{1},t_{2}}(x)
, we invoke the discrete heat equation (
2.78
) to write, for
s_{1}<s_{2}
in
\delta^{2}\mathbb{N}
and
u\in\delta\mathbb{Z}
,

(6.123)

\hat{p}_{s_{2}}^{\delta}(u)-\hat{p}_{s_{1}}^{\delta}(u)=\sum_{t_{j}=s_{1}}^{s_{2}-\delta^{2}}\left(\hat{p}_{t_{j+1}}^{\delta}(u)-\hat{p}_{t_{j}}^{\delta}(u)\right)=\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}\delta^{2}\cdot\sum_{t_{j}=s_{1}}^{s_{2}-\delta^{2}}\nabla_{x}^{2,\delta}\hat{p}_{t_{j}}^{\delta}(u).

In addition, the sum in the right hand side above can be written as a continuous time integral. We get

(6.124)

\hat{p}_{s_{2}}^{\delta}(u)-\hat{p}_{s_{1}}^{\delta}(u)=\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}\int_{s_{1}}^{s_{2}}\nabla_{x}^{2,\delta}\hat{p}_{\lfloor r\rfloor}^{\delta}(u)dr.

Now recall that the fourth discrete derivative
\nabla_{x}^{4,\delta}
is defined by (
2.77
). Moreover, it is readily checked that
\nabla_{x}^{2,\delta}(\nabla_{x}^{2,\delta}f)=\nabla_{x}^{4,\delta}f
for
f
defined on the grid
\delta\mathbb{Z}
. Therefore applying
\nabla_{x}^{2,\delta}
on both sides of (
6.124
) it follows that

J_{t_{1},t_{2}}^{\delta}(s,u)=\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}\delta^{2}\cdot\sum_{t_{j}=\lfloor t_{1}-s\rfloor}^{\lfloor t_{2}-s\rfloor-\delta^{2}}\nabla^{4,\delta}\hat{p}_{t_{j}}^{\delta}(\lfloor u\rfloor).

Applying the same kind of manipulations to the continuous difference
J_{t_{1},t_{2}}(s,u)
in (
6.121
) and then reporting those expressions in (
6.118
) we end up with

(6.125)

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{11}_{t_{1},t_{2}}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}\int_{0}^{t_{1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\int_{\lfloor t_{1}-s\rfloor}^{\lfloor t_{2}-s\rfloor}\nabla_{x}^{4,\delta}\hat{p}_{\lfloor r\rfloor}^{\delta}(\lfloor x-y\rfloor)dr-\int_{t_{1}-s}^{t_{2}-s}\partial_{x}^{4}p_{r}(x-y)dr\right)\mathcal{I}_{s}^{\delta}(x,y)dyds.

Similarly to what we did for Lemma
6.17
we need to deal with the cases
|t_{2}-t_{1}|\leqslant\delta^{2}

and
|t_{2}-t_{1}|>\delta^{2}
separately in (
6.125
). Indeed, analyzing
\mathcal{T}^{11}_{t_{1},t_{2}}(x)

in the former case will not produce a useful time-variation estimate
and we will adopt a more generic argument instead. We first discuss
the case when
|t_{2}-t_{1}|>\delta^{2}
.

Case I:
|t_{2}-t_{1}|>\delta^{2}
.

For
|t_{2}-t_{1}|>\delta^{2}
the following holds true for all
s\in[0,t_{1}]
:

\lfloor t_{1}-s\rfloor<t_{1}-s<\lfloor t_{2}-s\rfloor<t_{2}-s.

According to this elementary fact we decompose
\mathcal{T}^{11}
into

(6.126)

\mathcal{T}^{11}_{t_{1},t_{2}}(x)=\mathcal{T}^{111}_{t_{1},t_{2}}(x)-\mathcal{T}^{112}_{t_{1},t_{2}}(x)+\mathcal{T}^{113}_{t_{1},t_{2}}(x)

where
\mathcal{T}^{111},\mathcal{T}^{112},\mathcal{T}^{113}
are
respectively defined by

(6.127)

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{111}_{t_{1},t_{2}}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}\int_{0}^{t_{1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\lfloor t_{1}-s\rfloor}^{t_{1}-s}\nabla_{x}^{4,\delta}\hat{p}_{\lfloor r\rfloor}^{\delta}(\lfloor x-y\rfloor)\,\mathcal{I}_{s}^{\delta}(x,y)\,drdyds,

(6.128)

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{112}_{t_{1},t_{2}}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}\int_{0}^{t_{1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\lfloor t_{2}-s\rfloor}^{t_{2}-s}\nabla_{x}^{4,\delta}\hat{p}_{\lfloor r\rfloor}^{\delta}(\lfloor x-y\rfloor)\,\mathcal{I}_{s}^{\delta}(x,y)drdyds,

(6.129)

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{113}_{t_{1},t_{2}}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}\int_{0}^{t_{1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{t_{1}-s}^{t_{2}-s}\left(\nabla_{x}^{4,\delta}\hat{p}_{\lfloor r\rfloor}^{\delta}(\lfloor x-y\rfloor)-\partial_{x}^{4}p_{r}(x-y)\right)\,\mathcal{I}_{s}^{\delta}(x,y)\,drdyds.

The analysis of these terms is an adaptation of the
calculations developed in the proof of Lemma
4.8
. For
the sake of simplicity, we only give a brief discussion on
\mathcal{T}^{112}_{t_{1},t_{2}}(x)

and point out the main extra ingredients. The final estimate for
\mathcal{T}^{11}_{t_{1},t_{2}}(x)

is stated in (
6.134
) below.

Let us handle the term
\mathcal{T}^{112}_{t_{1},t_{2}}
in (
6.128
). We first resort to our usual change of variable

y=x+\sqrt{r}w
, which yields

\mathcal{T}^{112}_{t_{1},t_{2}}(x)=\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}\int_{0}^{t_{1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\lfloor t_{2}-s\rfloor}^{t_{2}-s}\nabla_{x}^{4,\delta}\hat{p}_{\lfloor r\rfloor}^{\delta}(\lfloor\sqrt{r}w\rfloor)\cdot\sqrt{r}\cdot\mathcal{I}_{s}^{\delta}(x,x+\sqrt{r}w)drdwds.

Moreover, since
t_{2}-t_{1}>\delta^{2}
and
0\leqslant s\leqslant t_{1}
, we have

t_{2}-s\geqslant t_{2}-t_{1}>\delta^{2}\implies\lfloor t_{2}-s\rfloor\geqslant\delta^{2}.

According to the uniform Gaussian estimate (
2.80
),
we get the following upper bound for all
r\in[\lfloor t_{2}-s\rfloor,t_{2}-s]
and
w\in\mathbb{R}
:

\big|\nabla_{x}^{4,\delta}\hat{p}_{\lfloor r\rfloor}^{\delta}(\lfloor\sqrt{r}w\rfloor)\big|\leqslant\frac{C_{1}}{r^{5/2}}e^{-C_{2}w^{2}}.

Plugging this estimate into (
6.128
) and setting
u=\lfloor t_{2}-s\rfloor+r
,
it follows that

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{112}_{t_{1},t_{2}}(x)

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-Cw^{2}}dw\int_{0}^{t_{1}}ds\int_{\lfloor t_{2}-s\rfloor}^{t_{2}-s}u^{-2}\big|\mathcal{I}_{s}^{\delta}(x,x+\sqrt{u}w)\big|du

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-Cw^{2}}dw\int_{0}^{t_{1}}ds\int_{0}^{(t_{2}-s)-\lfloor t_{2}-s\rfloor}(\lfloor t_{2}-s\rfloor+r)^{-2}\,\big|\mathcal{I}_{s}^{\delta}(x,x+\sqrt{\lfloor t_{2}-s\rfloor+r}w)\big|dr.

We now invoke the relation
(t_{2}-s)-\lfloor t_{2}-s\rfloor\leq\delta^{2}
again and apply Fubini’ s theorem plus the change of variable
v=t_{2}-s
. This yields

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{112}_{t_{1},t_{2}}(x)

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{0}^{\delta^{2}}dr\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-Cw^{2}}dw\int_{0}^{t_{1}}\frac{ds}{(t_{2}-s+2r)^{2}}\;\big|\mathcal{I}_{s}^{\delta}(x,x+\sqrt{\lfloor t_{2}-s\rfloor+r}w)\big|

(6.130)

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{0}^{\delta^{2}}dr\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-Cw^{2}}dw\int_{t_{2}-t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\frac{dv}{v^{2}}\;\big|\mathcal{I}_{t_{2}-v}^{\delta}(x,x+\sqrt{\lfloor v\rfloor+r}w)\big|.

As we have seen several times (cf. (
6.80
)), the estimate of the rough integral

\mathcal{I}_{t_{2}-v}^{\delta}(x,x+\sqrt{\lfloor v\rfloor+r}w)

involves five basic terms. With the observation that

\lfloor v\rfloor+r\leqslant v+\delta^{2}\leqslant v+t_{2}-t_{1}\leqslant 2v,

the first term among the five leads us to the consideration of the
integral

\rho^{\chi}\int_{t_{2}-t_{1}}^{t_{2}}e^{-(\lambda+\theta\rho)v}v^{\alpha/2-2}dv\leqslant\rho^{\beta/2}\int_{t_{2}-t_{1}}^{\infty}v^{\alpha/2-2}dv\lesssim\rho^{\beta/2}|t_{2}-t_{1}|^{\alpha/2-1}

where
\rho\triangleq a+\sqrt{T}|w|
. By including the outer two
integrals in (
6.130
), this term is further estimated as

(6.131)

a^{\beta/2}|t_{2}-t_{1}|^{\alpha/2-1}\delta^{2}=a^{\beta/2}\delta^{2}|t_{2}-t_{1}|^{\frac{\alpha-\beta}{2}-1}\cdot|t_{2}-t_{1}|^{\beta/2}\leqslant a^{\beta/2}\delta^{\alpha-\beta}\cdot|t_{2}-t_{1}|^{\beta/2},

where we have used the relation
t_{2}-t_{1}\geq\delta^{2}
for the last inequality. Notice that (
6.131
) is compatible with our claim (
6.102
). Next in the five terms decomposition alluded to above, one can check (as in the proof of Lemma
6.17
) that the other four terms yield higher powers of the form
\delta^{r}
with
r>\alpha-\beta
. Gathering those estimates we have obtained

(6.132)

\big|\mathcal{T}^{112}_{t_{1},t_{2}}(x)\big|\lesssim E(a,t_{2})a^{\beta/2}|t_{2}-t_{1}|^{\beta/2}\lambda^{\frac{\alpha-\beta}{4}}\kappa(\hat{{\bf U}}_{1}^{\delta})\Theta(\tilde{v}^{\delta})\delta^{\alpha-\beta}.

The term
\mathcal{T}^{111}_{t_{1},t_{2}}(x)
is treated along the same lines, with a small difference. Namely one should separate the
cases
t_{1}\leqslant\delta^{2}
and
t_{1}>\delta^{2}
. For the case

t_{1}>\delta^{2}
, one can follow exactly the same arguments as for
(
6.132
). Whenever
t_{1}\leqslant\delta^{2}
one should rely on the fact that
\nabla_{x}^{4,\delta}\hat{p}_{0}^{\delta}
is compactly supported, similarly to (
6.79
) and (
6.83
). We leave the details to the patient reader.

In order to handle the term
\mathcal{T}^{113}_{t_{1},t_{2}}(x)
in
(
6.129
), we gather Theorem
2.25
with uniform Gaussian estimates. This enables to write, for all
r\geq\delta^{2}
and
w\in\mathbb{R}
,

\big|\nabla_{x}^{4,\delta}\hat{p}_{\lfloor r\rfloor}^{\delta}(\lfloor\sqrt{r}w\rfloor)-r^{-5/2}\partial_{x}^{4}p_{1}(w)\big|\lesssim\frac{\delta}{\sqrt{r}}\cdot\frac{e^{-Cw^{2}}}{r^{5/2}},\ \ \ \forall r\geqslant\delta^{2},w\in\mathbb{R}.

Then arguing similarly to
\mathcal{T}^{111}_{t_{1},t_{2}}(x)
and
\mathcal{T}^{112}_{t_{1},t_{2}}(x)
we get

(6.133)

\big|\mathcal{T}^{113}_{t_{1},t_{2}}(x)\big|\lesssim E(a,t_{2})a^{\beta/2}|t_{2}-t_{1}|^{\beta/2}\lambda^{\frac{\alpha-\beta}{4}}\kappa(\hat{{\bf U}}_{1}^{\delta})\Theta(\tilde{v}^{\delta})\delta^{\alpha-\beta}.

Eventually, putting together (
6.132
) and (
6.133
) and recalling the decomposition (
6.126
), we arrive at the following bound for the case
t_{2}-t_{1}\geq\delta^{2}
:

(6.134)

|\mathcal{T}^{11}_{t_{1},t_{2}}(x)|\lesssim E(a,t_{2})a^{\beta/2}|t_{2}-t_{1}|^{\beta/2}\lambda^{\frac{\alpha-\beta}{4}}\kappa(\hat{{\bf U}}_{1}^{\delta})\Theta(\tilde{v}^{\delta})\delta^{\alpha-\beta}.

Case II
:
|t_{2}-t_{1}|\leqslant\delta^{2}.
The argument for this case is generic. Recall that
\theta^{\prime}<\theta

and
\beta^{\prime}>\beta
are parameters (
\beta^{\prime}
satisfies the same constraints
as
\beta
does) such that

(6.135)

e^{(\theta^{\prime}-\theta)a+(\theta^{\prime}-\theta)aT}a^{\frac{\beta^{\prime}-\beta}{2}}\leqslant 1\,,\quad\text{for all}\quad a\geqslant 1.

The notation
\Theta^{\theta^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}
for the controlled path norm
indicates that the exponents
(\theta,\beta)
are replaced by
(\theta^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})

whenever applicable. We also denote
E^{\theta^{\prime}}(a,t_{2})
as the
exponential weight function
E(\cdot,\cdot)
defined in Definition

3.8
with
\theta
being replaced by
\theta^{\prime}
. By
treating
w^{\delta},w
as controlled paths with respect to the
\Theta^{\theta^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}
-norm,
we directly apply the triangle inequality to get

\displaystyle\big|\mathcal{W}_{t_{1},t_{2}}^{1,\delta}(x)-\mathcal{W}_{t_{1},t_{2}}(x)\big|

\displaystyle\leqslant\big|\mathcal{W}_{t_{1},t_{2}}^{1,\delta}(x)\big|+|\mathcal{W}_{t_{1},t_{2}}(x)|

\displaystyle\leqslant E^{\theta^{\prime}}(a,t_{2})a^{\beta^{\prime}/2}\left(\Theta^{\theta^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}(w^{\delta})+\Theta^{\theta^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}(w)\right)|t_{2}-t_{1}|^{\beta^{\prime}/2}

(6.136)

\displaystyle\leqslant E^{\theta}(a,t_{2})a^{\beta/2}\left(\Theta^{\theta^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}(w^{\delta})+\Theta^{\theta^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}(w)\right)\delta^{\beta^{\prime}-\beta}|t_{2}-t_{1}|^{\beta/2},

where the last inequality follows from the assumption that
|t_{2}-t_{1}|\leqslant\delta^{2}
.
This yields the last term appearing on the right hand side of (
6.102
).

Step 4: Conclusion.

The proof of Lemma
6.23
is now complete by reporting (
6.122
), (
6.134
) and (
6.136
) into the decompositions (
6.117
) and (
6.103
).
∎

6.4.3.
The space variation estimate

The analysis for this part follows the same steps as in
Section
4.3.3
, with the discussion of multiple cases
(small time versus large time, that is
t\leqslant|x^{\prime}-x|^{2}
versus
t>|x^{\prime}-x|^{2}
). The separation small versus large time is also largely similar to the time variation case in Section
6.4.2
. We only state
the final result below and leave the details to the patient reader. Notice that below we use the notation (
4.51
) for spatial increments.

Lemma 6.24
.

As in Lemma
6.23
we consider some parameters

\theta^{\prime},\beta^{\prime},\theta,\theta^{\prime}
according to Notation
6.21
as well as the processes
\mathcal{W}
,
\mathcal{W}^{1,\delta}
in (
6.14
)-(
6.15
). Following our notation (
4.51
), set

\mathcal{W}_{t}(x,x^{\prime})\triangleq\mathcal{W}_{t}(x^{\prime})-\mathcal{W}_{t}(x),\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{W}_{t}^{1,\delta}(x,x^{\prime})\triangleq\mathcal{W}_{t}^{1,\delta}(x^{\prime})-\mathcal{W}_{t}^{1,\delta}(x).

Also recall that
\alpha,\beta,\chi
fulfill condition (
3.20
) and that the quantities
\Lambda
are introduced in (
6.101
). Then, for any
a\geqslant 1,

x,x^{\prime}\in[-a,a]

and
t\in[0,T]
, we have

\displaystyle\big|\mathcal{W}_{t}^{1,\delta}(x,x^{\prime})-\mathcal{W}_{t}(x,x^{\prime})\big|

\displaystyle\leqslant CE(a,t)a^{\beta/2}|x^{\prime}-x|^{\beta}\left(\lambda^{-\frac{\alpha-\beta}{4}}\left(\kappa(\hat{{\bf U}}_{1}^{\delta})d_{\hat{{\bf U}}_{1}^{\delta},{\bf W}}(\tilde{v}^{\delta},v)\right.\right.

(6.137)

\displaystyle\ \ \ \left.\left.+\Theta(v)\rho(\hat{{\bf U}}_{1}^{\delta},{\bf W})\right)+\lambda^{\frac{\alpha-\beta}{4}}\kappa(\hat{{\bf U}}_{1}^{\delta})\Theta(\tilde{v}^{\delta})\delta^{\alpha-\beta}+\Lambda^{\theta^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}\delta^{\beta^{\prime}-\beta}\right).

6.4.4.
The remainder estimate.

In Proposition
6.14
we have bounded the rough path norm of
v^{\delta}
without referring explicitly to its rough path decomposition. We will now give a more specific formula in this direction.
Namely, going back to relation (
6.14
) and since
\mathcal{W}=\mathcal{M}\mathcal{V}
, equation (
3.29
) asserts that the derivative
\partial_{{\bf W}}\mathcal{W}_{t}(x)
is

(6.138)

\partial_{{\bf W}}\mathcal{W}_{t}(x)=\partial_{{\bf W}}v_{t}(x)=-\frac{2}{\sigma^{2}}v_{t}(x).

As in relation (
4.60
) we thus introduce a remainder term for

\mathcal{W}
, seen as a process controlled by
\bf W
:

(6.139)

\mathcal{R}_{{\bf W}}^{\mathcal{W}_{t}}(x,x^{\prime})=\mathcal{W}_{t}(x,x^{\prime})+\frac{2}{\sigma^{2}}v_{t}(x)W^{1}(x,x^{\prime}).

Analogously, we shall define similar quantities for
\mathcal{W}^{1,\delta}
seen as a process controlled by
\hat{{\bf U}}_{1}^{\delta}
:

(6.140)

\displaystyle\partial_{\hat{U}_{1}^{\delta}}\mathcal{W}_{t}^{1,\delta}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{2}{\sigma^{2}}\tilde{v}_{t}^{\delta}(x),

(6.141)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\hat{{\bf U}}_{1}^{\delta}}^{\mathcal{W}_{t}^{1,\delta}}(x,x^{\prime})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{t}^{1,\delta}(x,x^{\prime})+\frac{2}{\sigma^{2}}\tilde{v}_{t}^{\delta}(x)\hat{U}_{1}^{\delta}(x,x^{\prime}).

Notice that in (
6.139
) and (
6.141
) we have used the notation (
4.51
) for spatial increments.
Notice also that (
6.141
) gives a controlled path for the stochastic integral term
\mathcal{W}^{1,\delta}
only. Taking into account the fact that one expects the deterministic terms in
(
6.1
)-(
6.2
)
to have a null Gubinelli derivative, relation (
6.141
) can also
be translated into a decomposition for the process
v^{\delta}
. We label
this decomposition for further use:

(6.142)

\tilde{v}_{t}^{\delta}(x,x^{\prime})=-\frac{2}{\sigma^{2}}\tilde{v}_{t}^{\delta}(x)\hat{U}_{1}^{\delta}(x,x^{\prime})+\mathcal{R}_{\hat{{\bf U}}_{1}^{\delta}}^{\tilde{v}_{t}^{\delta}}(x,x^{\prime}).

We will go back to this decomposition in Section
6.5
. For now,
the main task in this section is to compare the remainder terms
\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{W}_{t}}
and

\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{W}_{t}^{1,\delta}}
above. Our result is summarized in the lemma below.

Lemma 6.25
.

We use the same notation as in Lemma
6.24
. Let
\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{W}_{t}}
and

\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{W}_{t}^{1,\delta}}
be the remainders respectively defined by (
6.139
) and (
6.141
). Then for every
a\geqslant 1,

x,x^{\prime}\in[-a,a]

and
t\in[0,T]
, we have

\displaystyle\big|\mathcal{R}_{\hat{{\bf U}}_{1}^{\delta}}^{\mathcal{W}_{t}^{1,\delta}}(x,x^{\prime})-\mathcal{R}_{{\bf W}}^{\mathcal{W}_{t}}(x,x^{\prime})\big|

\displaystyle\leqslant CE(a,t)a^{\chi}(a^{\beta/2}+t^{-\beta/2})|x^{\prime}-x|^{2\beta}\left[\left(\kappa(\hat{{\bf U}}_{1}^{\delta})d_{\hat{{\bf U}}_{1}^{\delta},{\bf W}}(\tilde{v}^{\delta},v)+\Theta(v)\rho(\hat{{\bf U}}_{1}^{\delta},{\bf W})\right)\right.

(6.143)

\displaystyle\left.\ \ \ +\lambda^{\frac{\alpha-\beta}{4}}\left(\kappa({\bf W})\Theta(v)\delta^{\alpha-\beta}+\left(\Lambda^{\theta^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}+\kappa(\hat{{\bf U}}_{1}^{\delta})\Theta(\tilde{v}^{\delta})\right)\delta^{\frac{\alpha}{\beta^{\prime}+\beta}(\beta^{\prime}-\beta)}\right)\right].

Let us prepare for the proof of Lemma
6.25
.
As in Sections
6.4.2
and
6.4.3
, we only provide the details for those estimates
that do not follow from a simple adaptation of the analysis in Section

4.3
. We begin by recalling that according to (
6.7
) we have set
\mathcal{W}=\mathcal{M}\mathcal{V}
and the remainder for
\mathcal{W}
is spelled out in (
6.139
). Otherwise stated the remainder of

\mathcal{W}
as a controlled process is given by (
4.62
), that is

(6.144)

\mathcal{R}_{{\bf W}}^{\mathcal{W}_{t}}(x,x^{\prime})=\mathcal{R}_{t}^{0}(x,x^{\prime})+\frac{2}{\sigma^{2}}v_{t}(x)P_{t}W(x,x^{\prime}),

where
\mathcal{R}_{t}^{0}(x,x^{\prime})
is defined by (
4.63
)
and
P_{t}
is the heat semigroup on
\mathbb{R}
with generator

\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}\Delta
. To compare (
6.144
) with the discrete remainder,
we shall make use of a similar decomposition of
\mathcal{R}_{\hat{{\bf U}}_{1}^{\delta}}^{\mathcal{W}_{t}^{1,\delta}}

given in the lemma below.

Lemma 6.26
.

Let
\mathcal{W}^{1,\delta}
be the process introduced in
(
6.55
), and recall that its remainder
\mathcal{R}_{\hat{{\bf U}}_{1}^{\delta}}^{\mathcal{W}}
is defined by (
6.141
). Then for all
t\in[0,T]
and
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}
, the following decomposition holds true:

(6.145)

\mathcal{R}_{\hat{{\bf U}}_{1}^{\delta}}^{\mathcal{W}_{t}^{1,\delta}}(x,x^{\prime})=\mathcal{R}_{t}^{0,\delta}(x,x^{\prime})+\frac{2}{\sigma^{2}}\tilde{v}_{t}^{\delta}(x)\left(\hat{P}_{t}^{\delta}\hat{U}_{1}^{\delta}(x,x^{\prime})+\mathcal{Q}_{t}^{\delta}(x,x^{\prime})-\mathcal{K}_{t}^{\delta}(x,x^{\prime})\right).

In (
6.145
) we have set

(6.146)

\mathcal{R}_{t}^{0,\delta}(x,x^{\prime})\triangleq\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\nabla_{x}^{2,\delta}\hat{p}_{\lfloor s\rfloor}^{\delta}(\lfloor x^{\prime}-y\rfloor-\nabla_{x}^{2,\delta}\hat{p}_{\lfloor s\rfloor}^{\delta}(\lfloor x-y\rfloor))\right)\\
\times\int_{x}^{y}\left(\tilde{v}_{t-s}^{\delta}(z)-\tilde{v}_{t}^{\delta}(x)\right)d\hat{{\bf U}}_{1}^{\delta}(z)dyds,

and for
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
we also define

(6.147)

\displaystyle\hat{P}_{t}^{\delta}\hat{U}_{1}^{\delta}(\xi)

\displaystyle\triangleq\int_{\mathbb{R}}\hat{p}_{\lfloor t\rfloor}^{\delta}(\lfloor\xi-y\rfloor)\hat{U}_{1}^{\delta}(y)dy,

(6.148)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{t}^{\delta}(\xi)

\displaystyle\triangleq\frac{t-\lfloor t\rfloor}{\delta^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\hat{p}_{\lfloor t\rfloor+\delta^{2}}^{\delta}(\lfloor\xi-y\rfloor)-\hat{p}_{\lfloor t\rfloor}^{\delta}(\lfloor\xi-y\rfloor)\right)\hat{U}_{1}^{\delta}(y)dy,

(6.149)

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}^{\delta}(\xi)

\displaystyle\triangleq\hat{P}_{0}^{\delta}\hat{U}_{1}^{\delta}(\xi)-\hat{U}_{1}^{\delta}(\xi).

Also recall our convention (
4.51
) for increments of the form
\mathcal{Q}_{t}^{\delta}(x,x^{\prime})
,
\mathcal{K}_{t}^{\delta}(x,x^{\prime})
.

Proof.

The proof is purely algebraic and reproduces some of the steps in Lemma
4.11
. Starting from (
6.14
), some elementary manipulations yield

(6.150)

\mathcal{W}_{t}^{1,\delta}(x,x^{\prime})=\mathcal{R}_{t}^{0,\delta}(x,x^{\prime})+\tilde{v}_{t}^{\delta}(x)\cdot I_{t}^{\delta}(x,x^{\prime}),

where
\mathcal{R}_{t}^{0,\delta}(x,x^{\prime})
is defined by (
6.146
)
and

(6.151)

I_{t}^{\delta}(\xi)\triangleq\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\nabla_{x}^{2,\delta}\hat{p}_{\lfloor s\rfloor}^{\delta}(\lfloor\xi-y\rfloor)\hat{U}_{1}^{\delta}(y)dyds.

Similarly to what we have done in (
6.123
), one can switch from space to time gradients of
\hat{p}^{\delta}
in (
6.151
) thanks to the discrete heat equation (
2.78
). Namely write

\frac{\hat{p}_{s+\delta^{2}}^{\delta}(u)-\hat{p}_{s}^{\delta}(u)}{\delta^{2}}=\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}\nabla_{x}^{2,\delta}\hat{p}_{s}^{\delta}(u),\quad\text{ for all }\quad(s,u)\in\delta^{2}\mathbb{N}\times\delta\mathbb{Z}.

Then we can recast (
6.151
) as

I_{t}^{\delta}(\xi)=\frac{2}{\sigma^{2}}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\hat{p}_{\lfloor s\rfloor+\delta^{2}}^{\delta}(\lfloor\xi-y\rfloor)-\hat{p}_{\lfloor s\rfloor}^{\delta}(\lfloor\xi-y\rfloor)}{\delta^{2}}\hat{U}_{1}^{\delta}(y)dyds.

By further expressing the above time integral as
\int_{0}^{t}=\int_{0}^{\lfloor t\rfloor}+\int_{\lfloor t\rfloor}^{t}
,
the first integral yields
\frac{2}{\sigma^{2}}(\hat{P}_{t}^{\delta}\hat{U}_{1}^{\delta}-\hat{P}_{0}^{\delta}\hat{U}_{1}^{\delta})(\xi)
by expressing the time integral as a discrete sum,
while the other one yields
\frac{2}{\sigma^{2}}\mathcal{Q}_{t}^{\delta}(\xi)
.
Summarizing, we have obtained the relation

(6.152)

I_{t}^{\delta}(\xi)=\frac{2}{\sigma^{2}}\left((\hat{P}_{t}^{\delta}\hat{U}_{1}^{\delta}-\hat{P}_{0}^{\delta}\hat{U}_{1}^{\delta})(\xi)+\mathcal{Q}_{t}^{\delta}(\xi)\right).

Plugging (
6.152
) into (
6.150
) we thus obtain

\mathcal{W}_{t}^{1,\delta}(x,x^{\prime})=\frac{2\tilde{v}_{t}^{\delta}(x)}{\sigma^{2}}\left((\hat{P}_{t}^{\delta}\hat{U}_{1}^{\delta}-\hat{P}_{0}^{\delta}\hat{U}_{1}^{\delta})(x,x^{\prime})+\mathcal{Q}_{t}^{\delta}(x,x^{\prime})\right)+\mathcal{R}_{t}^{0,\delta}(x,x^{\prime}).

Now add and subtract the term
\frac{2\tilde{v}_{t}^{\delta}(x)}{\sigma^{2}}\hat{U}_{1}^{\delta}(x,x^{\prime})
to the above expression. This yields

\mathcal{W}_{t}^{1,\delta}(x,x^{\prime})=\frac{2\tilde{v}_{t}^{\delta}(x)}{\sigma^{2}}\hat{U}_{1}^{\delta}(x,x^{\prime})+\frac{2\tilde{v}_{t}^{\delta}(x)}{\sigma^{2}}\left(\hat{P}_{t}^{\delta}\hat{U}_{1}^{\delta}(x,x^{\prime})+\mathcal{Q}_{t}^{\delta}(x,x^{\prime})-\mathcal{K}_{t}^{\delta}(x,x^{\prime})\right)+\mathcal{R}_{t}^{0,\delta}(x,x^{\prime}),

where
\mathcal{K}_{t}^{\delta}
is defined by (
6.149
). This proves our claim (
6.145
).
∎

Remark 6.27
.

The appearance of the
\mathcal{K}^{\delta}
-term is a special feature
in the interpolated discrete equation which does not arise in the
continuous case. Indeed, it is due to the fact that
x\mapsto\hat{p}_{0}^{\delta}(\lfloor x\rfloor)

is not the Dirac delta function on
\mathbb{R}.

We now turn our attention to the proof of our main estimate
for the remainder.

Proof of Lemma
6.25
.

We have seen that
\mathcal{R}_{{\bf W}}^{\mathcal{W}_{t}}
and
\mathcal{R}_{\hat{{\bf U}}_{1}^{\delta}}^{\mathcal{W}_{t}^{1,\delta}}
are respectively given by (
6.139
) and (
6.141
), with decompositions (
6.144
) and (
6.145
). Therefore our global strategy will be based on the following estimates:

(i) compare
\mathcal{R}_{t}^{0,\delta}(x,x^{\prime})
versus
\mathcal{R}_{t}^{0}(x,x^{\prime})
;

(ii) compare
\tilde{v}_{t}^{\delta}(x)(\hat{P}_{t}^{\delta}\hat{U}_{1}^{\delta}(x^{\prime})-\hat{P}_{t}^{\delta}\hat{U}_{1}^{\delta}(x))
versus

v_{t}(x)(P_{t}W(x^{\prime})-P_{t}W(x))
;
